HARVARD UNIVERSITY LIBRARY 

IX

MADE IN THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA

PREFACE 

We have endeavored to supply in this little book such information, suggestion, and stimulation as seem essential to active citizenship in the United States of America. We have placed the emphasis on "active" citizenship because we believe that no other form is really effective. No other form meets the challenge of present-day conditions. Our education on the whole is too passive. It should be vitalized into the more active and aggressive type. This is particularly true of civic education in this era of transition. As a result of his training the student should be made to feel and act on his own initiative. He should become a self-starter. 

A study of the Constitution of the United States, excellent as such a study is in every respect, can not furnish all the training necessary to American citizenship. 

The Constitution contains the fundamental principles upon which our institutions are based but it does not and should not embrace a treatment of every phase of citizenship. Our life is much more intricate and complex than that of our fathers' day and demands a discussion of many civic topics which find no logical place in a constitution.

On the other hand, the Constitution should not be ignored or neglected. It is the oldest and best written 

Constitution in existence and is still in splendid working order and giving excellent results. Some writers make the mistake of minimizing its importance in civic

vi 

PREFACE 

instruction. They choose to give the major part of their attention to a lot of miscellaneous and disconnected topics which, though useful, are not fundamental in civic training. We have attempted to avoid both of these extremes and to present a reasonable and wellbalanced program of instruction. We have aimed to treat the larger and more important aspects of American citizenship in a simple, practical and stimulating way. 

This plan should lead to a thinking and an active citizenship—which after all is the main purpose of education. 

The Constitution of the United States, in an abbreviated and simplified form, is included in the latter part of the book. The purpose of this is obvious. Much of the legal phraseology of the Constitution is unintelligible to Junior High School pupils. Many of its terms are meaningless to them. We have attempted to convey the meaning and the substance of the document in simpler terms. We believe that this simplified form will be far more serviceable to the pupils than the complete text of the document. This complete text can come later.

We believe that a study of the principles and topics contained in this little book will give boys and girls a fairly adequate preparation for the complex life of the community in which they find themselves. There can be no more important training than this. 

The authors are indebted to Miss Edith Hoyle, of the High School of the University of Michigan, who read the manuscript and made many valuable suggestions in the preparation of the book. 

JAMES A. Woodburn

THOMAS F. MORAN

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP CHAPTER I COMMUNITY LIFE 

The ideal citizen looks out not for himself alone, but regards himself as but a part of the community in which he lives. 

JOHN HABBERTON 

We have first to learn that civilized life requires us to live in communities, and that each of us as an active citizen owes to his community his full co-operation, public spirit, and obedience to the law, in return for the benefits of community protection and service.

The home, the class, the church, the playground may rightly be called communities. School children are members of them all. Any number of persons living together and having common interests makes a community. Indeed, the whole world may be thought of as a community. Rapid communication and travel have brought the countries of the world more closely together. They have formed a society of nations and they are trying to find rules for living together in peace. So we have a community of nations as well as of persons.

Whenever several persons wish to live together and

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

enjoy one another's society, they will have to make some regulations for the government of the group, in order to prevent strife and discord and injury.

Robinson Crusoe. We are all familiar with the story of Robinson Crusoe, a man who was shipwrecked 

FRIEND OR FOE? 

When Robinson Crusoe came upon the footprint of a man, his surprise was mingled with delight that he was at last to have a neighbor, and with fear that the neighbor might be an enemy. on a lonely island. The nearest land was hundreds of miles away. There he was alone, thrown up by the sea. All others on the wrecked ship had been drowned. So far as Crusoe knew, there was no other man or woman or child on the island. 

One day Crusoe came across some tracks upon the damp sand. They were the fresh footprints of a barefoot man. He now knew that there was another man upon the island. He searched for him, and soon the two men stood face to face. What should they do? Should they fight and try to kill one another? If they did that and one killed the other, the survivor would have a harder time to save himself from death by starvation or exposure. The two men decided to live together and help one another. Each one needed a neighbor and helper. So Robinson

Crusoe and his man Friday formed a community and

COMMUNITY LIFE 

3 began to co-operate and work for one another. The one who had been more civilized was able to lead and give directions; the other was willing to follow. Community Civics had begun on their island. 

Civics has to do with Civilized Life. But our civics has little or nothing to do with savage cannibals and wild beasts who know only "the law of the jungle," to kill one another and eat one another. Nor is it concerned with shipwrecked sailors or with hermits living their lonely lives. It deals with civilized men, women, and children, who live together in society as we see them in communities about us. It deals with the benefits of government, with the laws, duties and privileges of citizens, with the life of the community, what we receive from that life and what we owe to it. 

The Need of Co-operation. If we are going to live together we must co-operate and help one another. We must all work for the good of the group. No one can be allowed to have his own way and do just as he pleases without regard to the welfare of others. We must be willing to give up our own way for the good of all. 

This is true of the Boy Scouts, the Campfire Girls, a debating club, a home, a school, a church, a lodge, or any body of people who have common desires to accomplish. There must be team work. Take, for example, a football team. If eleven boys play football they must play together, or the chances are, ten to one, that they will lose their games. Every boy plays not for himself but for the team. If a boy pays no attention to the rules or the signals; if he attempts to "go it alone" and make a “grand stand play" for his own glory, he is likely to lose the game and wreck the team.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Courtesy General Electric Company A TRIUMPH OF CO-OPERATION 

Trains, planes, and ships move on strict schedule because of the co-operation of thousands of skilled workers whose devotion to duty enables us to enjoy the countless benefits of community life.

COMMUNITY LIFE 

5 

That is a very selfish way to play. Every boy must give himself up to the play agreed upon, or the play which the quarterback calls, and they must all push and "buck the line" together, to make a hole or get 

KEYSTONE PLAYING THE GAME ACCORDING TO RULES 

their halfback around the end. The game must be played each for all and all for each.

Public Spirit in the Community. So it is in our community life. We are all bound up together and each one must look out for all the rest. We must obey the rules, in the family, the school, the neighborhood, the city, the village, the social club, the state, the nation. A good citizen will obey the law and feel free to do only what does not injure others. He will do more than that. He will try to help others and promote the welfare of the community. 

A Good Community Safeguards its Citizens. One wintry day a lady was walking along the streets of a little city. She came to a turn where there was a sloping sidewalk. She slipped and fell on the icy pavement

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

and was lame the rest of her days. If some one had only put some cinders or ashes on the slippery walk 

A PAINFUL ACCIDENT 

Might this have been avoided? this unfortunate woman might have been spared years of suffering and lameness. Had some one done her a wrong? Was the community to blame for her injury? Was she expected to look out for herself without any thought or care from any one else? Are not the rest of us to blame for not making the sidewalks safe to walk on? Since the streets and pave- ments are for everybody, we all have to plan for the safety and convenience of one another. 

Do you think of other ways in which injury may occur by thoughtlessness or carelessness in the community? 

Are railway crossings safe in your town? Have accidents occurred at these crossings? 

A Good Citizen Safeguards his Community. Once a man went out into the roadway to pick up a piece of barbed wire. He was afraid it might puncture a tire or entangle a horse and cause him to run away and hurt himself or kill somebody. This man had neither an automobile nor a horse of his own, and no children to get in front of a runaway horse. Why, then, should he

COMMUNITY LIFE 

7 care, and put himself to so much trouble? Because he was thinking of others. He knew that other people had automobiles and horses and children and that the middle of a busy street was no place for a piece of barbed wire any more than a sidewalk is a place for a banana peel. That was a very civil thing for him to do.

It showed that he was a good citizen and that he had community spirit. Such a man will be willing to help the community in other ways and more important ones and keep it safe from all kinds of dangers. 

Do you know of citizens who have done things unselfishly for the sake of the community? Can you think of ways by which people could help to keep the sidewalks of your community clean from litter and rubbish?

Do you have stop signals at all dangerous street crossings? Are your schoolhouse steps and church steps sanded when slippery? What should you do if you see a broken telephone wire down in the street? An electric wire? How can you tell which kind it is?

The Community Welfare Comes First. New condi tions bring new rules. The use of the automobile has made co-operation and courtesy very necessary qualities. It is especially necessary that drivers observe the rules of co-operation and courtesy, for the lack of these qualities may endanger the safety and lives of all. 

When traffic regulations were first established in a small New England village, an old gentleman who had been accustomed to drive through the streets unhindered said, "I am going to hitch up my horse and buggy and drive down town and I'd like to see anybody stop me." No doubt he soon saw the reasonableness of the new rule, if there was much cross-street traffic. If

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

every driver of horses or of automobiles in a big city should insist upon driving straight ahead as he pleases, nobody could drive at all. The streets would be blocked and no one could get anywhere. The common welfare makes regulation necessary which is better for each and better for all. If any wild and reckless driver refuses to obey the signals, for the sake of the welfare and safety of us all an officer of the law should be there ready to arrest him. 

SLOW SLOW

TO TO RIGHT RIGHT THE "SILENT COP" 

Community Life Requires 

Leadership and Obedience to 

Law. A well regulated community recognizes the rights of all. It wishes to give every man a "square deal," to give him a chance to live and have a home and to have his home protected from danger. Therefore the community should govern itself in such a way as to promote the good and restrain the evil; to prevent the things that tend to break up the community life and to do the things that will make it as easy as possible to do right and as hard as possible to do wrong. To bring this about the people must work together. They must obey the rules of the community. There must be leaders, or officers in authority, to guide and direct. If every one goes his own way, without thinking of others and regardless of the welfare of the community, the result is strife and discord, disorder, and chaos. Nothing can

COMMUNITY LIFE 

9 be done. If every one starts out to do what he pleases, 'every fellow for himself and the devil take the hindmost," there can be no society, no community life, no 

NESMITH SAFETY DEMANDS OBEDIENCE TO TRAFFIC SIGNALS 

Traffic on Fifth Ave., New York, going north and south when the red light in the tower holds back east and west bound cars and pedestrians. 

Such a system is necessary in cities and busy towns. school, no church, no family, no home, nothing to bind the people together. 

Every One's Help is Needed. Every man has his task in the community. The success and happiness of all depend on how each does his part. If a boy in school fails to prepare himself for life, he may become a burden to the community. If the watchman goes to sleep at the switch, lives may be endangered. If the train despatcher fails to send the right message, two

ΙΟ 

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

passenger trains may come into collision. If the janitor oversleeps, or neglects his duty the school may be cold and the children will suffer. If the miners quit work, how shall we keep our houses warm? If the farmers go on a strike, how shall we get food? If the railroad men "walk out" how can business go on?

It is clear, then, that we all have our work to do. 

We are all "members one of another," and we must all bear one another's burdens. So we study community civics to learn how boys and girls and men and women can best live together and work together for the good of all.

1. What is meant by Community Spirit? 

2. Show how working and living together in a community are like playing together in a game. 

3. Make a list of occupations that are necessary or useful in furnishing you a living -food, shelter, clothing. How many people do you suppose this involves? 

4. Show how the people of your community depend upon one another. Is this true of the nations of the world? 

5. Can a peaceful community of nations be established? How should nations act toward one another in a world community? 

6. What are the principal differences between the savage and the civilized man?

CHAPTER II FAMILY LIFE 

Birds in their little nests agree; 

And 'tis a shameful sight, When children of one family 

Fall out, and chide, and fight.

ISAAC WATTS 

The home is the foundation of society. In this Chapter we see how disloyalty and wrong-doing broke up the home in primitive times, and how our pioneer homes faced dangers and hardships. 

We see how, in the present time, love of home promotes love of country, and how good government is necessary for the safety and happiness of the home.

The home is the first community we know anything about. There we begin to live. There we find the family. In the family we find the first government in the world, for there the father and the mother decide what ought to be done and the children obey. The first lesson of childhood is to learn to obey, to respect father and mother, to do what will please them and bring them help and comfort. "Honor thy father and thy mother" is an old precept with a promise of a long life for those who keep the commandment. Children who disobey their parents and have no regard for their authority are likely to lead disorderly lives and to disobey the law of the land. 12

FAMILY LIFE 

13 Love of Home and Family. Nothing is stronger than love of home. A child should have a home life that will give him happy memories in the years to come. The home tie will always bind the family together. There the children have had good times at work and play. 

The father and mother try to keep the family together until the children grow up and go out to make new homes of their own. Then when the family reunion 

KEYSTONE A LARGE FAMILY REUNION 

The old gentleman in the center is surrounded by seventy-eight of his direct descendants who have met to honor his ninetieth birthday. There are five generations in this family group. comes, it may be that the parents and grandparents, the uncles, aunts, cousins, children and grandchildren may all meet together as one great family, or many families in one. The family tie binds the kith and kin together. In ancient times, when a family became so enlarged by numerous descendants, it was known as a "clan" or "tribe." The head of the family was called a "Patriarch," and he became the ruler of the tribe.

He was the leader, and his word was the law.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

An Early Home Broken up by Disloyalty and Crime. 

An old Bible story tells us that in an early home, in the very beginning of human society, there were two brothers in the family. These brothers followed different occupations and had different forms of religious worship. One day in envy and wrath one of the brothers rose up and killed the other. For this he was punished. He heard a voice telling him that his brother's blood cried out against him from the ground, and he was sent out from his home and family to wander as a fugitive and a vagabond on the face of the earth. 

Cain, the murderer, had violated the fundamental law of the family, the law of brotherhood and of human society.

What was to be done with such a brother but to banish him from the home? On what condition should he be allowed to return? When he was called to account he said, "Am I my brother's keeper?" In what sense was he his brother's keeper? Are we our "brothers' keepers in the community? Or, should every man attend to his own business and let other people do as they please? 

Would you not arouse your neighbor if you knew his house was on fire? Or warn him against any danger that might threaten his life or safety? Would you try to regulate his habits if they did not interfere with the peace and order of the community? Who is your neighbor? How near must one live to you to be your neighbor?

Early American Home Builders. The founders of 

America were home builders, not mere adventurers.

The Pilgrim mothers came with the Pilgrim fathers. 

Mothers were needed to establish homes in the wilderness. The early settlers in Virginia did not succeed

FAMILY LIFE 

15 very well until women came to be wives and mothers and helpers in the home. One reason the English won against the French in America was because the English built permanent homes and kept expanding their homes. from one settlement to another, while the French were chiefly explorers and traders wandering from place to place among the 

Indians. Because the Indians had no permanent homes they fell back and disappeared before the white pioneers. Sometimes the Indians lived in villages and cultivated the fields, raising what

A TYPICAL LOG CABIN HOME 

is known as "Indian corn"; but usually the Indians lived a roving, unsettled, uncivilized life. They had no real homes. They lived in tepees and dug-outs. 

They did not build houses or roads or cities or establish schools. So Indian civilization gave way before a civilization of homes.

The Pioneer Home in the West. When the early pioneers went to the West beyond the Alleghenies, they first built their log cabin homes. These were often far apart. At times they would be burned by the Indians, the father killed and the family carried into captivity. 

In some settlements block houses were built at central places to which the people could flee for refuge when the Indians were on the war path.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Many of these pioneer houses were mere shacks, barely good enough to give shelter from the storms of winter. Some of them had the bare earth for a floor. 

Some of them were "open faced" cabins, with one side exposed to the weather. Abraham Lincoln lived in that kind of house in his first winter in Indiana. The furniture was meager a broad board for a table; the fireplace in a mud chimney the only place for heating and cooking; two poles stuck between the logs of the cabin for a bedstead, with straw and leaves for a mattress. But however poor and humble the pioneer's house might be, it was the first desire of every family to have a home, so the children might be protected and guided and brought up around the family fireside. 

What is the meaning of the saying, “A man's house is his castle? In what circumstances may the officer of the law search a man's house? 

Home Education. There are two old sayings that have much truth in them. "As the twig is bent, so the tree is inclined." If you do not straighten a bent twig in the beginning, you will have a crooked tree. So it is with a boy. If he starts right, say for ten years, he will be right when he is grown. If he starts wrong, his future is uncertain. The other saying is, "The child is father to the man." That is, whatever qualities of character a boy acquires in childhood, he will show in manhood. Of course the same thing is true of a girl. So home education is of the greatest importance. 

A child learns more in his first six years than in any other equal period in his life. He learns to talk from father and mother and the other children. He learns

FAMILY LIFE 

17 the names of many things, and their uses. He learns how to walk and run and play. He learns the habits of father and mother and naturally grows up to be like them. The talk at the table or at the fireside will go far toward shaping the lives of the children. If the boy and the girl are required to do some part of the work of the household they will learn habits of industry, promptness, regularity, faithfulness and responsibility. 

If they see in their daily lives, by the example of their parents, the qualities of honesty, truthfulness, fairness, unselfishness, courtesy and regard for others, the children will develop these qualities in their own lives. So the home education is the foundation for the child's life.

Saving the Home. Since the home is so important it should be saved from dangers that threaten to destroy it. Sometimes there is poverty in the family and the children have to leave home because they cannot be provided for. The father may be out of a job, or in ill health, or lazy and good-for-nothing, and he cannot, or does not, provide shelter, food, and comfort for the family. In such cases the children may be without much real chance in life. Outside help may be needed to save the children for good habits and good work. The helpful, neighborly spirit in the community is called for. Many homes have struggled successfully against poverty and have been real blessings to their communities. "Be it ever so humble there's no place like home."

The Home and its Neighbors. "No man liveth to himself." "Bear one another's burdens." The homes of the community help one another. No home exists

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

all by itself. That would be a lonely life. Every home has its neighborhood and its neighbors. The people of the neighborhood need one another. In early pioneer days, though homes were far apart, a man's neighbors came over to help him. They came to the "log rollings" to lend a hand in the building of his log cabin. 

A PIONEER'S SHACK 

Crudely made, of tar paper and rough boards, but the pioneer called it home.

No one could do such heavy work by himself. He in turn helped others. Barn raisings, corn huskings, apple peelings, and quilting parties were times of friendly visits and mutual aid. A good neighborly spirit was cultivated. Likewise, there was neighborhood co-operation for protection against the Indians, and against horse thieves and marauders and other

FAMILY LIFE 

19 enemies of their peace and safety. It was by neighborhood co-operation that the people opened and improved their roads, established neighborhood schools, found entertainment, and contributed from their meager means to support the teacher and the preacher. 

A MODERN HOME 

Inexpensive, small, but well kept. Such a home is a credit to both owner and community.

The Home and Civil Government. When families and neighbors acted together to restrain evil doers and to promote their common interest, then they had a government. Government was a natural outcome of their needs, if the homes and lives of men and women were to be made secure. All can do for each what each cannot do for himself, especially when popula-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

tion increases and the communities become larger. 

Men who live together in neighborhoods have learned that they must respect one another's rights. They must preserve law and order. "Order is heaven's first law" and without law and order civilized communities cannot exist or make progress in the world. 

So the story of the home tells why we need civil government: 

A NEIGHBORHOOD 

In this typical group of American homes there live families who express their active citizenship in their respect and consideration for the rights of their neighbors. 

1. To afford safety in the homes, on the highways and in business. 

2. To preserve the peace, that people may live without fear of violence, or without constantly fighting to protect themselves and their homes. 

3. To maintain order, for the protection of life, liberty and property.

FAMILY LIFE 

21 4. To prevent crime, by restraining and punishing criminals. 

5. To secure justice between man and man, to protect the weak against the strong, to settle justly the quarrels of men by law, not war, to uphold the right and put down the wrong. Justice is the object of government and justice brings peace. 

To obtain these blessings for every community, governments are established. A good government is a great blessing, and when it does its work well, affording a square deal and justice to all, good citizens will support and pay for it gladly. 

We shall see that the government does more than preserve order, secure justice, and prevent evil things. 

It does many good things for the people which individuals cannot do so well for themselves. 

1. What are the essential features of a good home? Is a fine mansion essential? Why? 

2. Name some rights and duties of the children in a home. Of the parents. What habits tend to break up the home? 

3. Explain how good homes help to make a good community. 

4. Show how the pioneer home provided for its own needs.

Name some modern inventions that have made household work lighter and easier. 

5. Is home life better in the country than in the city? Give reasons for your answer.

REFERENCES JORDAN, ZILLER and BROWN, Home and Family. 

MCGILL and SCHIFFRES, The New Economic Citizen. 

MIRICK, G. A., Home Life Around the World. 

ADDAMS, JANE, The Spirit of Youth and the City Streets.

CHAPTER III  

Religion, morality, and knowledge being essential to good government and the happiness of mankind, schools and the means of education shall be forever encouraged. 

- ORDINANCE OF 1787 

In order to be better citizens of the school community, it is necessary to pause and consider what the school does for us and what we should do for the school. The following pages show how schools began in the colonies, how the states now provide for education, what benefits come from education, and how school life prepares for citizenship and service. 

The greatest enterprise in America is education. 

Every community has some kind of school. A community without a school would be a strange place in which to live. In America nearly all the children start to school when they are about six years old. Till then they are taught at home by their parents, or nurses, or their older brothers and sisters, or they attend kindergarten classes. There are now free public schools from the kindergarten to the high school, and about onefourth of all the people in America are attending these schools. There are about 30 million school children and 872,000 teachers, and the people are spending over two billion dollars a year to keep these schools open. 23

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

EDUCATIONAL PROGRESS 

From the one-room rural school in Richmond, Indiana, has grown the modern high school shown above. Children from a distance are brought in the school bus.

Evidently the people believe in education. There is nothing they believe in more.

The School Age. Children go to the graded school after the age of six. At about fourteen they can complete the 8th grade, or it may be the 9th grade. If they complete the senior high school it will take three or four years more. At eighteen or nineteen they are ready for college and the college course, too, usually requires four years. Beyond that comes the professional school - medicine, law, teaching, or whatever it may be. So, if one goes through all these schools he is 25 years old, or more, before he begins his life work. As a matter of fact, only one in eight of those who enter the public schools finishes the high school and only one in 44 graduates from college. It is estimated that only 1 in 7 of those finishing the high school goes through college. So all the education received by the great mass of American children comes from the common graded schools. 

Is there a compulsory school law in your State? 

How is it enforced? 

Why do children drop out of school? Is it because they are not interested? Or because they are not able to do the school work? Or because they have to go to work to earn a living? If they are not interested, is that the fault of the school? Should any child be denied an education because of poverty? How can the poor be helped? 

Early Provision for Education. Only a short time after America was settled, provision was made for education. Harvard College was founded in 1636, only 16 years after the Pilgrims came. William and

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Mary College was founded in Virginia in 1693, and Yale in Connecticut in 1701. Columbia, Pennsylvania, and

Princeton came within the next fifty years. From these colleges were sent out educated men -lawyers, physicians, ministers, teachers who knew the value of education and who worked among the people for the education of others and for the establishment of common schools. The State Universities of the old 

Northwest in Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Michigan and

Wisconsin - were planned and established before these states could prepare for a system of public free education in graded schools. So we see, as a rule, the college came first and college trained men laid the foundations for the community schools. 

In Massachusetts as early as 1647 a law provided that taxes should be levied to employ a schoolmaster in every village or township where there were as many as fifty families. The other colonies did not make such provision and it is estimated that fully half the people in colonial times could neither read nor write. Education was a luxury for the few, for the high-born or well-to-do who could pay for it. 

The States Provide for Education. It should be kept in mind that it is the States, not the National Government, that support our schools. The Federal 

Government offers money aid for vocational schools to such States as will provide for these schools and are willing to grant sums of money. There is a Bureau of Education at Washington which collects statistics and information and publishes these, but there is no 

Department of Education in the President's cabinet.

Many people think there ought to be. The National

THE MODERN HIGH SCHOOL PREPARES FOR SERVICE

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Government supports the Naval Academy at Annapolis and the West Point Military Academy and some Indian schools, but apart from these the National Government leaves education to the States and local State supervision.

States Unequal in Supporting Education. So education is a state matter. Some do better than others. 

Some keep the country schools open eight or nine months in the year; others for only five or six months.

Some pay the teachers well; some pay them poorly. 

Some have state-wide control with uniform courses of study and uniform textbooks. Others leave control to local bodies, the counties, townships, school districts, and each city is allowed a good deal of independence in school management. Some States make no provision for requiring the richer districts to help the poorer districts. In some States the county is the unit of control, in others the township, in others still the school district. It is a problem to know just how much state control should be established for the good of the schools. 

It is not necessary to have all schools alike, but, perhaps, the larger areas could help the smaller, and every school may do well to know how other schools are managed. 

Why do you go to school? 

What good are they doing 

Purposes of an Education. 

Why do the schools exist? for the boys and girls and for the men and women of the community? Nearly all parents, though they may have had little education, want their children to be better educated and have a better chance in life than they had themselves. So they will make many sacrifices to keep their children in school, and they always rejoice

when the schools can be improved. Let us notice some of the benefits which the boys and girls may receive from the school. 

1. Their education enables them to enjoy life more. 

Enjoying life is not always a matter of wealth. The rich are often more miserable than the poor. It is a 

NO SCHOOL PROGRAM IS COMPLETE WITHOUT PHYSICAL TRAINING 

To equip us for active citizenship the schools must provide for our physical development. pleasure to be able to use your mind, to read good books, to hear and understand public entertainments, to know what is going on in the world around you. 

The ignorant person does none of these things very well. 

2. Education enables one to become a good citizen and to serve his fellow men. The chief reason for having the public schools is to make good citizens. 

This is the best way for the State to defend itself and

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

to make itself strong. This is the reason why it is right to tax everybody for the support of the school. The 

State does it for self preservation. No matter whether there are children in the home or not, every home should help the school, because when citizens are educated all homes are safer. In America the people rule and make the laws. If they were all ignorant what kind of rule would there be? All men and women can vote. If they are so ignorant that they cannot read a newspaper, or an address, or even the names upon their ballots what kind of voters will they be? The people's education must go along with the people's rule; we cannot have one without the other. 

Ignorance is a poor foundation for the State. Such a 

State could not last long. A democratic government rests upon the intelligence of the people. 

It is true there are educated scoundrels who are not a help but a burden to the community. Education may make a bad man worse. The better educated he is the more harm he may do. But the heart must be educated as well as the head. "Exceptions prove the rule," and as a rule education will improve a man's character as well as add to his knowledge. A citizen serves the State by his character. If he is upright and honest and makes an honest living and is willing to help the community, he is a valuable citizen and the more intelligent and the better educated he is, the more valuable he will be.

Are highly educated men sometimes unable to earn a living? Are uneducated men often able to do so? If so, would this prove that there is no earning value in an education? Name some occupations which an educated person could fill which an uneducated person could not fill.

Old Time Ignorance and Old Time Schools. It is surprising to learn of the ignorance that prevailed in former times. In the middle ages 

In the middle ages hardly any one except the priests and monks could read or write. It was the monks in the monasteries who copied the books of the Bible and preserved them. The masses of the people were in total ignorance, not one in ten thousand could read. Books existed only in the form of large manuscripts copied by hand. Even after the printing press came (1446) education spread slowly. 

Only a few could go to the Universities, and there were no common schools. Not till the 19th century was the free public school established. As late as 

1850 in many of our States nearly half of the people could neither read nor write. In some States it was worse. In the American army during the World War officers reported, after certain tests, that one out of five of the soldiers could not write a respectable letter.

Such appeared to be the case after all the work of our public schools for so many years. But there are hopeful signs. Since the World War enrollment in high. schools and colleges has greatly increased. Within ten

years while the population of the country increased 20 per cent the enrollment in American colleges and universities increased 65 per cent. 

Trained Teachers. In the earlier days many of the teachers were ignorant and poorly trained. They taught only for a short time in winter, as they had to make a part of their living in other ways during the rest of the year. Teaching had not come to the dignity of a profession. In many places the minister or the priest, being the only educated person in the community, did most of the teaching. At times an "old time schoolmaster" did the teaching. He would open what was called a "subscription school." That is, he would go about the neighborhood and induce parents to sign his paper, or subscription, by which the father agreed to send his children to school for a term of a few weeks, and perhaps he would pay the master 50 cents a week for each child. The master was able to teach only the "spelling book" and the "three r's, readin', 'ritin', and 'rithmetic." The schools were not graded; children of all ages, from six to sixteen, were in the same room, all taught by the same teacher, usually a man, who did not believe in spoiling the child by sparing the rod. 

So the ferule or the hickory switch was close at hand and it was a common occurrence to whip the children who were unruly or did not learn their lessons. 

Schoolhouses. In those early days the schoolhouses were of the poorest kind. They were usually one-room log houses, with the bare earth or rough boards for a floor. The seats were rude benches without backs; or they were boards whose ends rested on two logs, often so high that the feet of the smaller

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

children could not touch the floor, (or the ground).

There was no school furniture to speak of no blackboards, no maps, no slates nor pencils, no ink nor paper, and as for textbooks each child brought what his home happened to have. The school room was heated by a fireplace; so a part of the room would be too hot, another part too cold. Sometimes the window panes consisted of oiled paper, as glass was too expensive to be had. In wintry weather the cold wind would whistle through the chinks, or cracks, between the logs. Such were the schools in the pioneer days of poverty and hardship.

Think of the difference between those old schools and ours, in teachers, in buildings, in equipment. Now the teachers are well trained in college and normal school. 

In these days the finest building in a town is usually the graded school building or the high school. In these schools are all kinds of modern apparatus for study and teaching. There are libraries, reference books, maps and globes for teaching history and English. There are laboratories with the necessary apparatus for physics, chemistry, botany, and other sciences. 

There are shops and tools and machines for technical and manual training, and gymnasiums for physical training, and kitchens, dining halls, and places for handiwork, and textile materials and apparatus for teaching domestic science. Boys and girls learn to make useful things with their hands, and they may learn a trade which will support them in life. The school has now largely taken the place of the master workman in training the apprentice. 

Let us think, too, how much wider is the range of

studies in these days. In the old days reading, writing and arithmetic were the only studies. Now the school children have a chance to study physiology and hygiene, literature and drawing, civics, history, nature study, geography, music, play, physical training, manual training, sewing, cooking and other domestic subjects, millinery, typewriting, stenography, agriculture, and commerce.

One may now study as many subjects and get as thorough an education in a good high school as he could have done in many colleges 30 or 40 years ago. 

How much is spent in your State every year to support the public schools? Can you think of ways in which the schools could be improved? 

How can good teachers be obtained? What else should a teacher consider besides the salary? Garfield once said, "A good college may be found with Mark

Hopkins on one end of a log and a student on the other.' 

What did he mean by that?

How many normal schools are there in your State for training teachers? Have you a Parent-Teacher 

Association in your community? What is its purpose? 

What does it do?

How are the school authorities chosen in your community? Is there an active interest in the schools? 

If not, how can it be aroused? 

Do the Schools Pay? The people have to bear heavy tax burdens. Many people find it hard to pay their taxes and to keep their children in school. Parents work hard and deny themselves many pleasures and comforts to provide food, clothing, shelter, books, and school privileges for their children. Would it not be unfair of the children to neglect their opportunities,

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

waste their time, and get very little benefit from the provision made for them?

Certainly in such a case the school would not pay. 

But if a boy or girl succeeds in school life, then the school is the greatest venture and the greatest investment in the world. School is a part of life and nothing 

THE TRACK TEAM GIVES THE SCHOOL A THRILL 

can be more certain than that success in school brings success after school days. By good, faithful, steady work one wins the rewards in school or in life. The very qualities and habits that bring success or failure in school bring success or failure in business, in a profession, or in any of life's vocations. These successful qualities are most easily acquired in youth. The way young people "play the game of life" in school and meet the tests and contests there will determine very largely how they get on in the tests that are sure to come as soon as they leave school.

School Spirit. It is a privilege and an honor to be a member of a school. The loyal members of a school will strive in every way to build it up, to promote its success, so they can take pride in its achievements. 

They can help to make known its needs and to create a sentiment in the community that will supply these SEERT 

RAH! RAH! RAH! WE WIN! needs. Loyal members of the school will be ashamed ever to bring dishonor upon their school, or to allow their poor scholarship and neglect of their studies to bring down the standing of their school among other schools. True school loyalty will lead them to think first of the welfare and good name of the whole school rather than of some little clique or club. 

The loyal school boy or girl may go in for "school activities." While he receives the pleasure and training from these activities he will strive to bring honor

صوصی 

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

to his school by his good work. He may "try out" for debating or oratory, or the school paper, for the glee club, or orchestra, for leadership in games and recreation, for basket ball, or for other forms of athletics. In these he learns the spirit of generous rivalry. 

You can stand by your school and root for your teams — not by betting nor boasting, nor by any discourtesy to the competing teams, but by loyally supporting every honorable effort to win. If you go into these contests for the "honor of your school" you can bring it nothing but dishonor by playing "dirty ball" or by coarse and unmanly conduct. The members of the team or school can never think well of themselves, nor will others think well of them, if they win by dishonorable or tricky means. 

Do "school activities" interfere with studies? Do you have a regular time for studying each subject? 

What good study habits have you to suggest from your experience? What is your most interesting subject of study? Does the value of a subject depend upon interest in it? If you dislike a subject is that a good reason for dropping it? 

What do you consider good qualities of sportsmanship? What mean qualities need to be restrained and prevented? Why will good sports be killed off if dishonorable tactics are allowed to win?

Training for Service and Citizenship. It may be said that the noblest purpose of the school is to train the young for service and citizenship. Service is the highest ideal of life. He is the greatest who serves the best. The boy or girl is the best member of the school who can do the most for it, who thinks most not of

what he can get out of the school but of what he can put into it. In this way he gets most out of it. Thus the school is giving the younger members of society a chance to live together and work together for the common good of all. You are not learning how to live in the future but how to live now. You are not learning merely how to become citizens; you are citizens now. 

You are co-operating in all good aims and purposes with your teachers, your fellow students, with your parents and school officials of the community. The good disposition and good habits you possess now are the best guarantees for good citizenship in the future. The school is your civic practice ground.

1. Make a list of ways in which a student may serve the school. In which the school may serve the community. 

How may the home help the school? 

2. Who is a good citizen of the school? How is a citizen of the school getting training that will help to make him a good citizen in adult life? 

3. What are the benefits of inter-school athletics? What evils come from such contests? Do you honor a good athlete more than a good student? What kind of inter-school contests can you propose? 

4. How can loyalty to your school be cultivated and manifested? 

Name some form of disloyalty. 

5. What is your object in going to school? Do you expect to go to college? Why? 

6. High school buildings and apparatus are expensive. Should they be idle, or unused, all summer?

CHAPTER IV THE CIVIL COMMUNITY: LOCAL GOVERNMENT 

Nothing excites more anxious concern than that of placing the interests of our fellow citizens in the hands of honest men. 

-THOMAS JEFFERSON 

The civil community could not exist without government. This 

Chapter deals with the development of local government in our country from the colonial settlements to a union of the States. 

It touches briefly upon the duties of our various officers in county and town.

From most of the communities we have named we can get away, if we wish. We are not compelled to belong to them. More than half the people in America do not belong to any church. Nearly all help to support the school, but only a few grown-ups are members of the school or take part in its work. We may join a lodge or a club or a society of some kind, or not join, just as we please. But there is one community from which we cannot get away. That is the state. The civil government of the state is over us all. All these other communities are subject to the law of the state. It may be possible for a man to find some remote and barbarous region of the world where there is no state authority or civil government or civilized life, but it would not be easy and it would not be safe. 4I

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

The Need of Civil Government. If a group of civilized men ever find themselves in an unsettled country without a government they proceed immediately to organize one. When gold was discovered in California in 1848 men came flocking there from all parts of the world, all sorts and conditions of men. Of course there NOTICE.

LAWBREAKERS, BEWARE! 

This historic poster suggests the wild chases after horse thieves to bring them to justice.

Once a year this society still meets in Worcester, Massachusetts, although its meetings now are wholly social. were bad men among them who were lawless and criminal. There was no government, no law, no officers, no courts. 

The California settlers had lived under some form of civil government and they saw that some form of law and order was necessary. The leaders and best men first organized a "vigilance com- mittee," a kind of "law and order society," to restrain the lawless, to try criminals, That is, they to hang horse thieves and murderers. applied for a short time "lynch law" for self protection, which is justified only when there is no other law in

CIVIL COMMUNITY: LOCAL GOVERNMENT 43 

the community. But as soon as possible this lynch law gave way to recognized authority. The military commander with his soldiers came in to protect the people in their right to peace, security and property. Then as soon as possible a civil government was organized, a constitution was adopted, and California was admitted as one of the States of the Union. This was all done in two years, and it illustrated how Americans were quick and anxious to establish orderly civil government.

Military Government Must be under Civil Govern ment. In a new country there are likely to be strife, disorder and violence. Soldiers may be sent in to preserve order and restrain men from crime. Military government is efficient for quick action in bringing about order. But the military commander should always take orders from the civil authority as soon as civil order is established. Military rule may be despotic and overbearing. There is no chance for self government. So the military force is to be regarded as an arm or agency of the civil government and should be subject to its control. 

Why is it not good to have soldiers control the Government?

In America Civil Government Began Locally. When the Pilgrim Fathers came to America they brought their government with them. Before they landed at 

Plymouth Rock they entered into an agreement, or compact, in which they said they would live in an orderly way under civil rulers. In New England the colonial settlements, or towns, were pretty close to-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

gether. In the South the people lived farther apart on large plantations and their civil divisions were called counties or districts or parishes. In Louisiana these divisions are still called parishes. 

There was very little outside control over these 

O YALE UNIVERSITY PRESS CHRONICLES OF AMERICA MOTION PICTURE THE PILGRIMS BY PERMISSION THE MAYFLOWER COMPACT 

Elder William Brewster is shown reading the agreement in which the Pilgrims declared their purpose to set up a government and to make just laws. local colonial settlements. Charters were granted by the King providing a form of government. Most 

Colonial Governors were appointed by the Crown or the Proprietor and a Governor's Council was provided for, like an upper house, or Senate, in our Legislatures. These were to help and advise the Governor. 

But, for the most part, the colonists were left to themselves to manage their own local government. As the towns grew in number and the colonial boundaries

CIVIL COMMUNITY: LOCAL GOVERNMENT 45 

became fixed they felt the need of a general meeting to consider the interests of the whole colony. So the towns sent representatives to a meeting or assembly which acted like a State Legislature to make provisions and laws for all the settlements in the colony. All the 

THE HOUSE OF BURGESSES 

This is the name by which the General Assembly in Virginia was known. 

Patrick Henry, who is pictured here addressing the house, was a county representative. settlements agreed to be bound by these laws passed by the Assembly since their representatives had enacted them. They were trying to provide for the common defense and the general welfare of all the settlements in the colony. This was the way representative government began in America.

In 1619 the House of Burgesses of Virginia met. This represented the people in the different counties of that colony. This legislature proceeded to make laws for

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

the whole colony. Similar law-making bodies arose in the other colonies.

The Settlements Formed a Union and Became States. 

The New England towns and the Virginia counties were not changed after they had sent representatives to a colonial assembly. They still continued to govern themselves in the old way in all their local matters. 

But a colonial state grew up by this union of the settlements and this Colonial Assembly governed itself in all its internal affairs which were of concern to all the people and parts of the colony. Later the thirteen colonies all united to resist what they considered unfair laws by Great Britain - laws which interfered with their self-government. With the coming of American independence, the Colonies became States. Very little change was brought about in their local governments, except that State Constitutions which the people adopted for themselves took the place of charters which had been granted by the King. Rhode 

Island and Connecticut kept their old charters, Connecticut till 1818 and Rhode Island till 1842.

Thus, we see, the towns and settlements came first. 

They made the States and the States made the Union. 

The local communities grew into the State communities and the State communities grew into the national community. When the American State came into the 

Union it had control of all local matters within its borders. It gave up only a few of its powers which could best be exercised by the nation in the interest of all. Later Congress organized Territories whose inhabitants formed new States and these were admitted into the Union on an equality with the other States.

CIVIL COMMUNITY: LOCAL GOVERNMENT 47 

Did the States make the Union or the Union make the 

States? May States withdraw from the Union if they choose? When was that decided?

Forms of Local Government: 

Officers and their 

at Duties.

Let us notice some of the forms of local gov- ernment. A State is subdivided into counties. A 

State may divide a county into two counties or unite two counties into one. The United States may not do that with the States. No State may be divided without its consent, nor may two States be united into one without the consent of both. The States provided for this when they made the Constitution. 

The States existed before the United States least thirteen of them did, and the later States have the same rights within the Union and the same relation to it that the original States have. The States are more independent than the counties; they are not mere subdivisions of the Union nor mere creatures of the National Government. The States have certain independent rights and powers of their own with which the nation may not interfere.

County Officers. The State creates counties to collect taxes and administer local laws. Court is held at the County Seat. The court house is the place where the county offices are, where taxes are paid, and county business is transacted. Counties vary in size and population, also in the way they are governed, according to the laws of the various States. Usually there is a county court, or a circuit court for more than one county, to try cases at law.

The County Commissioners (called Supervisors in some States,) have general oversight of the county

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

business. They have charge of the county buildings; they may build a new court house if they think it is needed; they buy the supplies and make contracts for the county; they are responsible for building new roads and bridges (apart from State Highways); and they have general charge of spending the county's 

BROWN BROS COUNTY COURT HOUSE, AKRON, OHIO 

money. So they are the most important officers of the county.

The Sheriff is the peace officer of the county. He carries out the orders of the court, arrests offenders, executes the law and keeps the peace. He may appoint deputies to assist him, or call on other citizens, or he may call on the Governor of the State to send the militia to his aid, if need be. 

The Clerk keeps a record of the proceedings of the court, issues marriage licenses, keeps a record of births

CIVIL COMMUNITY: LOCAL GOVERNMENT 49 

and deaths, and he (or the Auditor) acts as secretary for the county commissioners.

The Treasurer takes care of the money of the county. 

The Auditor takes care of the accounts and checks up (audits) the books of the Treasurer, and other county officers.

The Recorder, or Register of Deeds, makes a record of deeds, wills, leases, contracts, and other papers that deal with the transfer of real property that is, lands and houses, not personal property. 

The Coroner, usually a physician, holds inquests or inquiries over the bodies of those who may have died. by accident or in some mysterious way. He calls a "coroner's jury” and seeks evidence as to the cause and circumstances of the death. If there is evidence of wrong doing it is turned over to the prosecuting attorney for use in the courts. If the sheriff's office becomes vacant the coroner often succeeds to it. 

The County Superintendent of Schools has charge of the rural schools.

The County Attorney is the lawyer for the county. 

He gives legal advice to the Commissioners and appears for the county in any lawsuit for or against the county.

There is also a Prosecuting Attorney, sometimes called State's Attorney or District Attorney, whose business is to prosecute persons for violating the law. The Judge, the Sheriff and the Prosecuting Attorney are the law officers of the county whose business it is to see that the laws are enforced and that criminals are restrained and punished. These officers have been extremely busy in recent years.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP The Western Township and the New England 

Town. The civil township in the West and the South is a division within the county. These civil townships generally correspond (though not always) to the congressional townships which were made into regular shapes of six miles square in the survey of the public lands by the United States Government. A congressional township is made up of 36 sections, each section containing 640 acres. This system applies only west of Ohio in States sometimes called “public land States." 

In the civil township the principal officer is the Township Trustee, or Supervisor, who, in some states, has charge of the country schools, appoints the teachers, and acts on the County School Board which selects the County Superintendent of schools. In some states the County Superintendent, or County School Commissioner, is elected directly by the people. The Township Trustee may be the Overseer of the Poor and he has charge of the general business of the township. The township has also a road superintendent, assessor, and justices of the peace to try petty cases and disputes. 

The New England Town, which is still the unit for local government in that section, corresponds to the western township. The town is an irregular district of country with a central village and perhaps other villages. The old town meeting is a gathering of voters once or twice a year, at which Selectmen are elected, who act as the town officers. The meeting also selects a town clerk who keeps a record of births, deaths, and marriages; a treasurer, a tax assessor, and constables, who collect the taxes. There are also school committees, overseers of the poor, and health officers.

CIVIL COMMUNITY: LOCAL GOVERNMENT 51 

These officers are all responsible to the town meeting. 

In the annual town meeting these officers make their reports, taxes are levied, improvements are ordered, appropriations made. There is free discussion and by direct democratic action the people decide for themselves what their local government shall do. 

All this made the town meeting a good training school in politics. Here was full debate, questions were asked, criticisms offered, new policies were proposed which were attacked and defended. Young people who attended could see government in action where honest men were wrestling with local problems. It was in the town meeting that Samuel Adams, John Adams, James 

Otis, Josiah Quincy, Roger Sherman and other New

England statesmen of the American Revolution had the training which helped to prepare them for the great service which they rendered to their country.

U. S. Army?

CHAPTER V  

It is not an official that keeps the streets clean. It is the citizen, and if the citizen does not realize fully that keeping the streets clean is as much a part of citizenship as voting, he may elect officials by the dozen or score, and his streets will not be clean. 

· ADOLPH ROEDER 

The city provides for the education, the health, the convenience and the safety of its citizens. Traffic regulations, clean and well-lighted streets, parks and playgrounds, fire and police protection, telephone service and transportation, the care of dependents the city offers these benefits and many more to its people. This Chapter explains also the advantages of city zoning and city planning, and the differences between the city manager and the commission plan of government. 

When our country was young and Washington was President fully nine-tenths of the people in America lived in the country. Now in some States, like Massachusetts and Rhode Island, nine-tenths of the people live in cities. In 1789 there were only six cities in 

America with a population as high as 8000 people. 

Now there are over 700 such cities. Washington was a farmer and when he left his Virginia farm or plantation to become President he saw two of the greatest cities in the country, Philadelphia and New York, but neither of them had as many as 40,000 people. Now 53

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

New York has over 6,000,000, Chicago over 3,000,000, Philadelphia about 2,000,000, Detroit with 1,500,000,

Los Angeles over 1,000,000, and many other large cities are approaching the million mark, such as Cleveland, Baltimore, St. Louis, Boston, and Pittsburgh. the 

Why have the Cities Grown? If you will look at map in your Geography you will see that nearly 

ZION & PASSING C DEWING GALLOWAY. N. Y PITTSBURGH STEEL PLANTS 

A busy section of a great industrial city, which owes its rapid growth to its nearness to the coal and iron mines as well as to its shipping advantages. all these great cities are at sea ports or lake ports, or on some river course. At these places there is a "break in transportation" when goods are shipped from one part of the country to another. That is, the goods must be changed from land routes to water routes or the other way round. This makes necessary great elevators, warehouses, storage rooms, and stock yards. 

Many laborers must be employed in loading and unloading. Many railroads run into these cities, and they bring their shops and workmen.

Another reason why cities have grown is because of the great growth of factories. Modern inventions have brought much machinery into use. Thousands of workmen find employment in the city factories. The shipping facilities are good and the raw materials and food supplies can easily be brought from the surrounding country. Pittsburgh is at the head of river navigation and is a center of coal and iron supplies. Lake schooners with cheap freight rates bring to Cleveland iron ore for her steel factories. St. Louis on the Mississippi is favorably situated both for river trade and transcontinental traffic. 

Can you account for the growth of other cities? Why do young men and women go from the country to live in the cities? Is it easier to find employment in the city?

What other advantages do cities offer in education and entertainment? Is the population of rural districts declining? If so, can you give any reasons? Why are so many city people going to the country to live? If you had the money to live where and how you chose, which would you choose, the city or the country? Why? 

What do Cities do for their People? There are many things which the people expect the city to do. 

1. It must provide for educating the children.

Every child ought to be able to attend a free public school. Some cities provide not only a graded school and a high school but also a college for their young people. 

2. The city should look out for the health of the people. It must provide for a good pure water supply, see that the food of the people is not impure, and that

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

the milk for the children is sweet and wholesome. The city must have a good sewerage system and see that the household garbage is disposed of. The garbage and rubbish from houses, restaurants and hotels, from kitchens and dining rooms, if left exposed, would decay 

DEWING GALLOWAY PROVIDING FOR A PURE WATER SUPPLY 

One system of purifying the water is to aerate it, or throw it into the air in jets, after which it flows through clean sand into delivery pipes that take it into homes and other buildings. and cause discomfort and sickness. The garbage collector with his wagon should make his regular rounds from house to house. 

3. The city should control the street traffic, keep the streets from being overcrowded, regulate the parking of cars and make the crossings as free from danger as possible. 

4. The city should keep its streets clean and its

sidewalks and bridges in repair. Hundreds of street cleaners are employed in the large cities for this purpose. 

5. The city should provide a system of parks and playgrounds for rest and recreation. By means of 

CEWING GALLOWAY, NY FREE SHOWER BATH 

In some large cities the Fire Department sets up sprays attached to the fire hydrants. In warm weather this adds to the children's comfort and health. these the city people can find breathing places and have an outing. 

6. The city should provide for fire protection. Fire in a city may be very destructive. Lives are always endangered and at times whole sections of a city have been swept away by a fire. This was the case in the great Chicago fire in 1871, which was started by care-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

lessness. Cities now prevent the building of sheds and hay barns and other "fire traps" within the fire area of the city. (See Chapter XIV on Fire Protec tion, page 208.) 

7. The city should provide a lighting system. The streets cannot be left dark at night. The city must see that the houses are supplied with gas and electricity for cooking and lighting. 

8. The city has to make some arrangement for communicating by telephone and for some system of transportation, for getting about from place to place. The cities extend for so many miles and the homes of the people are so far from where they work and shop and do business that some system of rapid transit is necessary. So the city provides overhead and underground railways, street cars and busses, to take passengers from one part of the city to another. In the business districts thousands of people live and work in crowded areas, underground in cellars and basements, and far up in the air in tall "skyscrapers, thirty or forty stories high. (The Empire State Building, opened in New York on May 1, 1931, is eighty-six stories high.) Systems of cheap and easy transportation have helped to make our cities grow and become great business centers. The thousands of workers, shoppers, sight-seers, and pleasure seekers can be accommodated. The Elevated and the Subway have their locals and their express trains carrying thousands of passengers daily for many miles. Steam trains too, in frequent runs and at cheap rates carry passengers (commuters) to and fro between their business and their homes in suburban towns.

Means for City Service are Privately Owned. Some cities own and control their gas and electric service and their own water systems. Usually the telephone and street car systems are owned by private corporations. 

The people who use these agencies are dependent for good service either on public officers or on the corpora- 

DOWNTOWN CLEVELAND, SHOWING TERMINAL TOWER AND GROUP PLAN DEVELOPMENT ON LAKEFRONT 

tions. Sometimes the city grants a charter to a private corporation which agrees to supply the houses, homes, offices and city buildings with gas, electricity and water. 

Nobody can live in a city very well without these conveniences, but no householder can supply them for himself.

The corporations that render these public services are called public service corporations. They receive from the public certain privileges, such as the use

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

of the streets, alleys, and highways. Each corporation is given a monopoly on its business, one to supply gas or electricity, one to supply water, another transportation, another the telephone. There can be no competition, as it is not desirable that two companies should be tearing up the streets and erecting their poles. It is better for a city to have one telephone company rather than two. No one wishes two telephones in his home or his office, with two systems of numbers. 

One company with a monopoly is better. But if this monopoly is not owned by the public (as is often the case with the water works or the lighting system) it should be regulated by the public.

The cities or the states have a right to regulate these corporations to see that they fulfill their obligations to the people. The prices they charge should be fixed by law and a good quality of service should be required. In a street railway company's charter it may be provided that a certain percentage of the street car fares shall be paid into the city treasury for the maintenance of the city parks. This seems only fair, as thousands of people use the street car lines in order to get to the parks.

They work in poorly ventilated "sweat shops," at times thirty or forty families living in a single tenement house. grants have settled. These have to be taught in day schools and night schools, so they may become "Americanized" and prepared for citizenship. Every year charity has to provide for thousands of people who are out of work or without means of support.
