Two thousand or more people live on this street. Some conduct their business or trade right here. They all do their shopping at the curb-stone push-carts whose owners sell everything from food to wearing apparel at astonishingly low prices.

Governing the City. In addition to all these things which a city must manage, it must be governed. Law and order must be maintained, the city courts kept open, justice administered, criminals arrested and restrained and crime prevented, so far as is possible. 

A system of city taxation must be devised, the city receipts and expenses must be accounted for, and the

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

public money must be used in the best possible way to promote the public welfare. 

This city government involves a police system for the protection of life and property. The police force exists for the peace of the community. The bluecoated policeman who guards our streets is generally the friend of all who do well. He is the friend of little children and the ever-ready helper of the stranger in the city. (See "Protection of the Community," p. 206.)

With all these problems and burdens does it not seem that a city has enough to do?

Zoning a City. To zone a city is to divide it into districts, reserving certain neighborhoods for certain purposes. A good residence neighborhood should not be spoiled by the intrusion of stores, warehouses, factories, cheap eating houses and other buildings that tend to cheapen the value of residence property. By a zoning plan provision is made for growth and certain districts are set aside for homes, others for apartment houses, others for manufacturing plants, public service stations, warehouses, offices and business rooms. This helps to make the city more symmetrical and beautiful. It is good housekeeping. The majority of our principal cities have now adopted zoning ordinances. 

A city without zoning has been compared to "a house in which the book case is in the bath room and the kitchen stove in the parlor." Do you think this is a good comparison? Why?

It has been said that millions of dollars are lost every year by property owners in American cities from lack of zoning. Can you explain why this is so?

In a Connecticut city a man owned a fine old colonial

residence shaded by beautiful elms. He had an offer for it but postponed selling until he came back from a trip to Europe. When he got back he sold his house-was glad to sell - but he took $10,000 less than he had been offered. During his absence a public garage and gasoline filling station had been built next door. Had the community done him a wrong?

See if you can get information on good zoning laws for your city by writing to the Department of Commerce 

(Division of Building and Housing), Washington, D.C. 

This Department issues a Zoning Primer. 

City Government. Usually cities are governed by a mayor and council elected by the people. The mayor represents the whole city, the councilmen represent the various wards, though some of the councilmen may be elected by the city at large. The terms are from one to four years. In a few cities the city council is divided into two bodies like the two houses of a legislature.

The council passes city ordinances, levies the city taxes, creates city departments, and provides for such matters as the public health, street traffic, garbage disposal, city lighting, parks and amusements.

It is the mayor's duty to enforce the city laws. He presides over the meetings of the Council. In many cities the mayor may veto the ordinances passed by the Council. When he does so the Council may pass the measure over his head either by another majority vote, or, as in some cities, by a larger vote than a bare majority. The mayor appoints certain heads of departments and he may remove these when he chooses. 

The mayor sometimes holds a court to try offenders against the city laws.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

The mayor-council form of city government provides for a City Clerk to keep the records, and a City Treasurer, to keep the funds, and, in larger cities, a City Judge for the trial of the more important criminal cases.

The business of the city is carried on largely by Boards or Departments, such as the Departments of 

Health, Finance, Police, Public Works, etc. The 

Board of Education which manages the city schools is in some cities elected by the Council, in some by a vote of the people. As a rule it is independent of the rest of the city government.

Failures and Reforms in City Government. There have been many defects and failures in the government of American cities. This failure has been called the "shame of American cities." Citizens have failed to attend to their civic duties. In many large cities bad men have secured election to the city council from several wards. They can then legislate for the whole city. They make corrupt deals in selling city franchises and they make agreements with criminals for pay. Party managers and bosses control ward politics and get their "heelers," or agents, into the city council, who seek to control city appointments and police officers for personal and party purposes. These ward politicians are willing to betray their city for the "spoils of office." There has been a long struggle against these forces of evil in American city government, and there are many signs of improvement and progress. 

The Commission Plan and the City Manager. In 

1901 a hurricane and tidal wave swept over the city of 

Galveston, Texas. There was a great emergency. The

corrupt and wasteful city government had no credit; it could not borrow money, nor be trusted in any way to build up the waste places. Public-spirited citizens and business men of Galveston went to the Legislature and asked that body to set aside the old form of city government and put the powers that had been exercised by the mayor and council into the hands of a commission of five men. So Galveston received a new charter providing for the election of five commissioners by the people of the city, two to be elected one year and three the next, all to be chosen not by wards but by the city at large. In this way "ward managers could be defeated. 

One of these commissioners the one receiving highest popular vote - became the President of the Commission. He was called "Mayor," and while he had a general supervision over the affairs of the city, he had no veto power, nor any more power than the other commissioners. The city was divided into four ad ministration departments: (1) Finance and Revenue; 

(2) Water and Sewerage; (3) Police and Fire Protection; (4) Streets and Public Property. Each commissioner supervises one of these departments, and is thus a business manager of an important branch of the city's business. Expert engineers and business heads are appointed by the whole commission which has power also to levy city taxes and pass such ordinances as it may think the city needs. Thus law making and law enforcing are combined in one body and the citizens can know very readily who is responsible for the kind of government the city has.

The City Manager. As one great flood brought re-

ACTIVE. CITIZENSHIP 

form to Galveston, so did another bring a change to 

Dayton, Ohio. In March, 1913, heavy rains flooded the city of Dayton. Business management was necessary to get the city on its feet. The people of Dayton adopted for themselves a self-governing charter, as they were allowed to do under the laws of Ohio. They adopted the "city-manager plan" of city government, sometimes called the "commission-manager plan." It had already been tried in a few small towns. 

The people of Dayton, like those of Galveston, elected a commission of five members, but the Dayton commission placed the business of the city in the hands of one person who was an expert business manager. This plan looks upon city government not as politics but as business. The citizens are stockholders; they choose a board of directors (the commission) and these directors appoint a foreman, or business manager, to manage the city's business, just as the directors of a bank appoint a cashier to care for the business of the bank, or as a School Board, elected by the people, choose a Superintendent to direct the management of the schools.

The city manager is chosen, not because of religion, politics, or "pull," but because he is an experienced business expert in managing a city's business. The manager may be a citizen of the city, but he is more likely to be brought from some other city where he has made a good manager. The manager is well paid, he has charge of the city departments, and becomes responsible for the honest and efficient conduct of the city's affairs. He prepares the city budget, advises the commission on the needs of the city, investigates

and corrects abuses and appoints and dismisses city employees, or heads of departments - fire chief, chief of police, etc. This gives a single head to the city, and when things go wrong the people know whom to blame. The manager is responsible to the commission and he may be removed by that body. 

More than four hundred cities have now adopted the city-manager plan, among them Akron (O.), Norfolk (Va.), and Sacramento (Cal.). Thousands of dollars have been saved in city revenues by better business management, and city improvements have been greatly extended.

The State makes laws governing its cities. How much ought a State to interfere in a city's government? 

Should a city have full “home rule" as to controlling its streets and schools, as to its taxes and how they shall be raised? As to managing its public utilities? Do the country members of a legislature know the problems of the city? Should a city be allowed to define its own crimes regardless of what the State or the Nation makes a crime? As in reference to the liquor traffic? What are the objections to electing councilmen by wards? Why is it important to be able to fix responsibility for failures and abuses? 

Explain: "Eternal vigilance is the price of good city government. 

City Planning: The City Beautiful. In 1800 Wash ington City became the Capital of America. The site was chosen ten years before on the muddy banks of the Potomac amid woods and swamps. Major L'Enfant, a French engineer, was engaged to draw up the plan for the new city. Streets were laid out, running east and west, north and south, intersecting at right

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

angles, after the usual checkerboard plan. Then long avenues were provided running on the diagonal, like spokes from the hub of a wheel. These avenues radiated from a center, the hub of the city, where the beautiful and majestic Capitol Building has since come to stand. Where these avenues intersect the streets, "Circles," or little parks, were planned, which add open spaces and beauty to the city.

On this plan America's Capital City has developed from a few wooden huts to a "city of magnificent distances," of about half a million people, and it is one of the most beautiful cities in the world. It had a plan at the start. Very few of our cities were so planned.

Like Topsy, they just grew, without any plan, in a commercial way, or from manufacturing demands. 

Their streets were narrow, irregular and crooked as if they were following cow paths as, for example, in 

Boston. Their growth has been haphazard, without plan or foresight. No one ever had the vision of their possible future and planned for their beauty. 

Most of our cities are laid out in squares, or rectangular blocks. The houses, or lots, are numbered 100 to the block on a plan which Philadelphia first started. 

This makes it easy to find any address. If you know a family lives at 1843 North Walnut Street, you know that it is in the 18th block north of the central dividing street running through the city east and west. Number 2580 East Washington Street may be found 25 blocks from the central street running north and south.

To a foreign traveler American cities seem much the same. They are monotonous, with too few curves in

the streets, with no artistic plans, no unique features. 

All main streets are alike. How can this be remedied?

How could your city become noted for some unique attraction peculiar to itself?

Beautifying the City. In planning for the growth of towns and cities attention should be given to both health and beauty. Narrow streets should be avoided, as they may become congested with traffic. Swampy places should be drained, and if a stream runs through the town it should be kept free from pollution and its banks kept free from weeds, dumps, and garbage piles. 

From the stream the city could have a children's swimming pool, and its banks instead of being places that are waste and ugly may be turned into places of rest and beauty. The "city beautiful" will have its avenues and boulevards with grass plots and flowers between the driveways. Avenues of beautiful trees may be cultivated. The "slums" may be cleaned out.

The railway stations may be made attractive with lawns and flowers. Monuments and fountains and stately public buildings may be erected that will add dignity and charm to a city.

If such a public spirit of enterprise and planning exists, and if the city keeps its streets and alleys clean, and if citizens keep their houses in order and their lawns and yards clean and trimmed, the people may have a "city beautiful" which they will be proud to have strangers visit.

The people of our cities have come to see that mere money making, mere dollars and cents, are not the chief consideration in life. The city is to be not merely a place for workshops and trading houses and machin-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

ery and smokestacks, but a place where people can be comfortable and live in pleasant surroundings. Even factories and shops are being made attractive and pleasant places in which to work. Smoke nuisances are being abated and the screeches of noisy and earsplitting whistles are being prevented. The banks of rivers are being beautified; water fronts are being redeemed from trackage and factories and turned into attractive resorts. The wonderful lake front in Chicago, instead of being given up to railway tracks, freight cars, and puffing and noisy engines is to be turned into fine beaches, parkways and drives. Chicago is carrying out a general plan of beautification and development which will make it one of the most beautiful cities in America.

All this is in response to the love of the beautiful, which is natural in man. As a pioneer people conquering the wilderness we may have neglected too much the lovelier and more artistic side of life. But after our cities have become established and wealthy their people are willing to plan and pay for more beautiful abodes, even at a great price. If the city is well governed and its people can have comfortable and wellordered homes and beautiful surroundings under the reign of law, one will then write, not of the shame of the cities, but of their glory.

There are Rules for Walkers as well as Drivers. 

Accidents are not always the fault of the drivers. 

Traffic managers have announced some sensible rules for the walkers: 

Keep to the right on sidewalk, crosswalk, roadway and passageway, but on a highway without sidewalk, such as a country road or any road intended for auto traffic, keep to the left so as to have a clear view of approaching traffic. Observe traffic before stepping from curb "look both ways" - and keep off roadway except when crossing. 

Keep out of traffic whirlpools.

Cross roadway at right angle, not diagonally. Only "jay walkers" try to cross two streets at a time. On a crosswalk watch for the traffic officer's signals and heed traffic signs.

Stand on the sidewalk or within safety zones while waiting for street cars. 

Watch out for traffic when obliged to pass from behind a car.

Face and step toward the front of a car while alighting. 

Observe traffic when alighting from a car. 

Enter and leave car stop safety zone only at crosswalk. 

Do not loiter on a crosswalk, or before an entrance.

Do not walk more than two abreast on a crosswalk or on a busy thoroughfare.¹

CHAPTER VI  

From seed-time to harvest, from dawn to dark, the farmer toiled in the field in the ever-recurring struggle against flood and drought, devastating storm and withering sun, blight, scale, pest, and all the manifold enemies of nature, to bring his crops to fruition and his stock to fertility. 

- DAVID SAVILLE MUZZEY 

We learn in this Chapter of improvements on the farm, of organizations among farmers, of country schools and education, of farming clubs for boys and girls. We see, too, how the 

Department of Agriculture helps the farmers through the work of the county agents, and how opportunities are offered for a more prosperous farm life.

We have seen that in Washington's time nine-tenths of the people of America lived in the country. Now only about a third live in the country. Yet farming is still our principal industry, and one-third of our workers are living and working on the farms. These workers are doing the most important work in the world, they are producing our food. City and country are dependent on one another but the city is the more dependent. The country could get on without the city; but if the farmers ceased to produce their crops how could the people in the cities get food? 

The cities would cease to be. 

75

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Washington and Jefferson always thought that the chief strength of our country was to be found in an honest yeomanry, sturdy farmers who cultivated their own farms. Nothing is more important than that we should have an intelligent, industrious, contented farming population who are well rewarded for the work which they do for the community. 

Modern Improvements in Life on the Farm. In former times the farmer and the farmer's wife had a much more lonely life than they have now. They were left more to themselves. Now the telephone enables the dwellers in the farm home to keep in touch with their neighbors and with their friends in town. 

They may call the doctor or order something by parcel post from the store in town. The farmer may have a car and the good roads enable him to travel long distances. A team of horses can draw twice as heavy a load over the smooth roads as could be drawn over the rough or muddy roads of the days gone by. Electric lights are reaching the farmer's home. Modern inventions have brought him improved machinery, the cultivator, the steam thresher, the mowing machine, the tractor plow. The free postal delivery brings him his mail and the daily paper; the parcels post carries his packages to and from the city. He shares in the benefits of the radio program, with its news, educational, and entertainment features. The schools are improved and there are central or consolidated graded schools and high schools to which the children are carried in the school bus over good roads. The farmer, if he chooses, may even live in the village or town and by quick transportation, on the trolley or by automo-

FARM METHODS, THEN AND NOW 

The horse plow of a few years ago has to a large extent given way to the modern tractor. Note the wide swath cut by the tractor.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

bile, he may go to the farm every day to superintend its management. So the farmers are sharing modern improvements with others.

The Farmers are Organizing to Work Together. 

The farmers live so far apart over so wide an extent of territory that it is difficult for them to organize and act together. The labor men in the cities can form their unions more easily. Still the farmers are getting together for co-operation. Farmers in the past have organized the "Grange" and the "Farmers Alliance" for mutual help and education. Now they have the "Farm Bureau." A County Farm Bureau is organized to secure co-operation throughout the county. A Township branch of the Bureau is formed so the farmers of an immediate neighborhood may meet together to discuss agricultural problems or to listen to instruction from some expert on rural problems. An agent or manager of the County Bureau is appointed who acts as agent for selling the stock and grains from the farms and for buying farm machinery. So they practice co-operative selling and buying and thereby secure better prices.

A National Organization of Farmers. The farm bureaus of the different counties unite to form a State farm bureau, and the State bureaus have formed a national body called the "American Farm Bureau

Federation." This is a nation-wide organization with millions of members. It deals with farm matters in which the farmers of all the country are interested and it tries to secure the legislation which they desire. The 

Federation is trying to bring a knowledge of scientific agriculture into every farming community. It is

trying to get the same government aid for its industry as is allowed to manufacturing, banking, business and labor interests. When the farmers stand together in a "farm bloc" in their politics and voting, their representatives in Congress do so too, and they become very powerful in securing favorable legislation. 

Farm Credit and Farm Loans. When a farmer wishes to add some improvements on his farm, or buy some expensive machinery he may need to borrow money. 

Or he may need a loan "to tide himself over" till his crops can be sold in the fall. For a good part of the year he has much outgo and no income. So he needs credit. The local banks may take advantage of his need and charge him too high a rate of interest. To help the farmer out in this difficulty Congress established a Federal Farm Loan System in 1916. Farm

Loan Banks are maintained in twelve different districts throughout the country. Ten or more farmers by combining together may form a Farm Loan Association and this Association may apply for loans to the 

Federal Farm Loan Bank. The borrowing farmer offers a mortgage on his land. No loan is made for more than 50 per cent of the assessed value of the farmer's land. Only land may be given as security. 

The Emergency Farm Mortgage Act of May, 1933, and subsequent farm credit acts liberalized the loan system of 1916, enabling the twelve Federal land banks to issue loans to provide for the purchase of land equipment, fertilizer and live stock; to improve buildings and land; to liquidate indebtedness incurred prior to January, 1933; and to provide the owner of mortgaged land with funds for general agricultural uses.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Education for the Farmer. Much has been done to train the farmer for his own work. In every State there is an Agricultural College. These are sometimes called "Land Grant Colleges," because the United

States Government granted large amounts of land to each State for establishing these colleges, by the Act of 1862. These colleges have trained scientists and teachers who are studying in their laboratories to find out what causes disease in plants and animals and how these diseases may be cured. They seek to make known the best means of producing the largest crops. These colleges have their extension work, answering many inquiries, sending out lecturers, instructors and bulletins of information to farmers and farmers' institutes in all the counties in their States. 

There are Experiment Stations or experimental farms under these colleges where intelligent experts are trying out various plans in plant and animal husbandry. Any farm boy can come and see how farming is done by those who know. Farm boys go to these schools sometimes for only a short course of four or six weeks. They are called "short horns" by the other college boys, but, as a rule these "short horns" show that they have long heads as they often turn out to be intelligent and progressive farmers.

The United States Department of Agriculture cooperates with these colleges and the Farm Bureaus in sending demonstration agents to show farmers how to care for their land, their live stock, their crops, and to attend to business accounting on the farm. Women teachers from the Domestic Science Departments of the State colleges go to the local institutes to instruct

the farmers' wives and daughters in poultry raising, fruit raising, butter making, breadmaking, caring for bees and squabs, and in the fine art of putting up fruits, jellies, and jams. All this educational instruction is practical work and "practical farmers" have learned its benefits. At times exhibit cars are sent out and these stop long enough at county seats to enable expert lecturers to give a good message to assembled farmers and their wives on some phase of agriculture or household economics.

Better Schools for the Country. Country schools have changed for the better along with other things. 

Better life on the farm demands better education for the boys and girls of the farm. The country schools are not now so much neglected as in former years. (1) They have better supervision. All the schools of a township, perhaps of a county, are brought under a common plan and each school may have its needs supplied and have a course of study as good as that of the others. The County Superintendent oversees the country schools and seeks to keep them up to the standard. (2) They have better physical improvements. The old log school houses or tumble-down frame buildings have given way to comfortable school buildings, with playgrounds and hygienic provisions. (3) There are better teachers. The best are none too good for the country. The country schools are now demanding teachers not only of high character but also of special training and education. (4) Consolidated Schools. The little country school of one room, ungraded, with pupils of all ages under

one teacher, has given way to the larger graded school at some central point. These consolidated schools are graded, well equipped, with one teacher to one grade, or with "departmental teachers," one teacher to one subject in all the grades. The good roads help the children to get to these good schools, and thus the 

AT THE COUNTY AGENT'S OFFICE 

The farmer has called to ask the county agent's advice. Note the specimens of farm products which are exhibited in the office. children in the country may fare as well in their schools as the children in the city.

The County Agent Helps the Farmers. A number of years ago the United States Department of Agriculture sent out some agents, who had been trained for agricultural work, into various counties of the South and West. These agents went to live in the local coun-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

ties. They sought to get groups of farmers together so as to show them how to fight animal diseases and the pests of the farm, like the boll weevil, the chinch bugs, the scale on the fruit trees. They were trying in every way to bring about success in farming. 

Now there are thousands of these county agents living and working with the farmers in our agricultural communities. Every progressive county where farming is an important industry has one of these agents. 

He is generally a graduate of the State Agricultural College and he works as the local field agent of the college. The college sends him help as, also, does the 

United States Department of Agriculture. The agent may be supported by the County, or partly by the State, and sometimes the local Chamber of Commerce helps out. The business man in the city is interested in the welfare of the farmer, or ought to be, since he depends so largely on the farmer for his success in business.

Clubs for Boys and Girls on the Farm. Bright boys and girls on the farm are learning how to make farm life pleasant and profitable. They are growing up to be better farmers than their fathers. Boys' Corn

Clubs and Pig Clubs are organized. The boys are taught how to raise hogs and to make corn land yield more bushels to the acre. Contests are held and premiums are earned by the industrious and intelligent boys who can grow 100 bushels of corn to the acre. 

The girls of the farms are organized into Canning Clubs and are taught how to can small fruits, berries, and tomatoes. They learn how to select the seed, to plant and cultivate it, and in this way they add to the yield of garden vegetables. The jellies and jams and canned fruits made by these girls often receive the first premium at the county fair.

The boys and girls are learning that farm work is not merely "beef" and muscle and back work, any more than football is. Brain work and team work count on the farm as well as anywhere else, perhaps more.

Learning to Care for the Soil. Farmers need to study the soil. There is a science of the soil. It is called agronomy. There are professors who make the soil their special field of study. A farmer's field cannot play like a boy, but it needs rest and recreation and the proper foods to make it grow just as much as a boy does. If the soil is used for the same crop year in and year out, with no letup, it will wear out. The onecrop product of the South, tobacco or cotton, has worn out many rich Southern plantations. The land should "lie fallow" for a year or be sown to other crops that

Changing from a crop which takes richness from the soil to one which renews it is called "crop rotation." 

The good farmer divides his farm into a number of fields and nearly every year he changes the crop which he raises in any one field, sowing wheat in one field. this year and clover in that field the next year. Thus he varies his crops and calls nature to his aid in fertilizing the land. A one-crop farm, like a one-crop section, is not very profitable in the long run.

Needs of the Farm Community and How They are Supplied. There are many agencies in the States to protect the farming industry and to promote its interests:

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

There are boards of agriculture, to study conditions in country life, to spread knowledge on farming, to provide for agricultural exhibits and State Fairs, where farmers may see farming machinery and methods and also note what other farmers are doing.

There are dairy commissioners, to aid in the production of milk, cream, and cheese, and to prevent infection

There are horticul tural boards, to promote fruit culture and enforce laws against plant diseases. The San José scale is a great pest and whole orchards at times fall before its blight. 

There are sanitary boards, to study livestock and enforce quarantine regulations against diseased animals. There are fish and game commissioners, to stock the rivers and lakes with fish and to prevent their destruction by dynamiting and seining, and to save the wild game on the farm. The "bob white," the rabbits, the squirrels, the mink and opossums, and other fur-bearing animals should be protected from ruthless hunting and trapping out of season.

There are state entomologists (special students of insects) who look after insect pests that may destroy vegetables, fruits, and grains.

Prosperous Farming. Prosperous farming is necessary to a prosperous community. When the farm decays the country decays. If the farmer cannot receive a fair profit for his toil; if his crops will not pay the expense of raising them and a good living for his family, some remedy must be found and applied. The farmer is serving the

whole country. He and his family have a right not only to a good living but a good deal to spare for improvements and comforts, for pleasure, for culture and education. The prosperity of the manufacturer and the business man depends very largely on the buying power of the farmer. If he should be unable to buy, all business would be injured, factories would close down, traveling men could not get orders from the country merchants. Then hard times would come, and the whole nation would be in distress. For these reasons our statesmen need to study the problem of the farmer and to do all they can to see to it that the working farmer is permitted to retain the ownership of his land and to receive a due reward for his services.

CHAPTER VII MAKING A LIVING IN THE COMMUNITY 

To be honest, to be kind, to earn a little and spend a little less, to make a family happier for his presence here, this is a task for all a man has of fortitude and delicacy. 

ROBERT LOUIS STEVENSON 

We should all realize the need of self-support, the blessing of honest work, and the conditions necessary for earning a living. 

Good health, a useful occupation, thrift, self-denial, economy, and an honest character are essential. In this Chapter we see how the honest worker deserves both the support of the community and the protection of a just government. 

Boys and girls wish to be able to earn a living and to do some useful work when they grow up. School days will soon be over and they will be "out in the world" making their way. The boy in school looks forward to success. If he is diligent, orderly, and successful there, so he will be in after life. 

Living to Work and Working to Live. Some men seem to live to work. They love their work and they would be miserable if they had nothing to do. Only the lazy and good-for-nothing like having "nothing to do." Most of us have to work in order to live, and every honest and willing worker ought to be able to find some good work to do and to have a chance to 92

MAKING A LIVING IN THE COMMUNITY 93 

earn a living. Every person worth while in the world, no matter how poor or how well-to-do, wishes to contribute his share to the community life about him. 

Earning a Living. Some men act as if they believe "the world owes them a living." The world owes no able-bodied man a living. There are some who have to be cared for by others. The old and the feeble have done their work. Their children or their relatives will care for them. If they have no relatives the community will provide. The blind, the crippled, the sick, the insane, these have to be helped by the community. 

We have homes and schools and hospitals where these unfortunates may be cared for and restored to health. it

In what ways can boys and girls earn money while they are in school? Do you know any children who have left school to go to work? 

Have you known blind people who earn their own living, or part of it? What can they do? How can a blind child learn to read?

Let us notice the means by which success is won in the world. 

1. Honest Work. The best way in the world for getting money or wealth or position in the world is to work for it, the good old-fashioned way of honest toil. 

There is no better way. One may inherit money, but may do him more harm than good. If it leads him. to think he has a right to live without work, or without rendering any service to society, an inheritance may be a real misfortune. One must show a manly independence, industry, and perseverance, and be ready to make his own way if he expects to be a real force in the community.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Gambling is not honest work. The gambler has consented in his own mind to put into his pocket gains for which he has rendered no service. Boys and girls who are preparing to earn a living ought to know that the only honorable means by which an able-bodied person may get money in the world is to do something worth the money. Is there any other principle by which to live? 

How does this principle apply to school life? Are there dishonorable ways of doing work, or getting credits, in school? Can you name dishonest acts you have seen in school? 

2. Good Health. Next to honest work in earning a living is good health. Earning without health is up-hill work. It is of prime importance to develop, or preserve, a good, strong, bodily constitution. The child who wishes to do this should eat good, wholesome, health-producing food; should take good bodily exercise; should breathe fresh air and drink pure water; and should keep his body clean. If he observes these simple rules of health good food, good water, good exercise, pure air, and a clean body the child is likely to grow up, not only with a sound body, but with a sound mind, and this will go a long way toward his ability to make an honest living. 

Child Labor Injurious to Health. Long hours of forced toil will undermine the health of children. There are places beet fields, cotton fields, tenements, factories where little children, only six or seven years old, have to work long hours for pitiful wages. Such child labor stunts the bodies of children and seems cruel, almost inhuman. Can this wrong be made

MAKING A LIVING IN THE COMMUNITY 95 

right? Since the States compete in industries it has been found almost impossible to make the matter right in one State till it has been made right in all. Recently a national law forbidding such child labor was declared unconstitutional by the Supreme Court. An Amend-

3. An Occupation. In earning a living in order to apply honest toil and good health there is need of an occupation, - some trade, business, profession, labor, - for which one is prepared and to which he can apply himself. In America a boy does not need to follow the

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

occupation of his father. There is a wide field and the boy may choose for himself. Sometimes boys and girls decide very early what they wish to do in life and they take such work in school or college as will fit them for their calling.

It is never safe to be a "Jack of all trades and a 

BUILDING A HOME 

Home-building is a part of active citizenship. Usually it is the result of hard work, thrift, family co-operation, and self-sacrifice. 

"of master of none.' If one is taught to do something well, if he acquires the habit of accuracy, energy and industry, you may be sure he will be able to earn a livelihood. This will give him honest independence, which has been called the mother of virtues courage, perseverance, self-reliance, thrift, cheerfulness, and hope." A man is likely to be a poor citizen, perhaps a dangerous one, if he has no occupation, no business, no home, nor property, no means of earning

MAKING A LIVING IN THE COMMUNITY 97 

a living. If a man is a "roustabout," or street loafer, who does not know where his next day's living is to come from, or whether his family will have a shelter over their heads the coming winter, he will have very little interest in the community, and he will be ready to listen to messages of discontent and revolution. Such a man can add nothing to the stability of law and order and good government. He will lend himself readily to any lawless, destructive movement in the community since he has nothing to lose and he may feel that he stands to gain by the losses of others in time of violence. Having an occupation, a living, and a home is one of the first essentials of good citizenship. 

4. Thrift. In working for a living one should be thrifty. Thrift is "good management in the business of living." It is the opposite of waste. 

A thrifty person will save his money and save his time, and use his money and time to good advantage. "Thrifty” does not mean stingy. A thrifty person may be very generous and he is likely to have something to be generous with. An idle, lazy, shiftless person never gets on in the world and he is never able to be generous. One who works and saves may soon be able to buy a home or start a business and he then becomes of some weight and importance in the community. 

While one is in health and is earning money he needs to "save for a rainy day," because sickness may come or he may be thrown out of employment and he may then need what thrift led him to save. 

Waste is the enemy of thrift. To be lazy or idle is to waste your energy. To work hard at something not

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

worth while is also waste. To dig a hole in the ground and fill it up again may require hard work, but what is the use? It is waste of time and labor. Old King 

Sisyphus, of Corinth, because of his avarice, was condemned in the lower world (according to the myth) to roll a huge

SAVINGS DEPT 

APINE

BANKS ENCOURAGE SAVING 

stone to the top of a hill and to fix it there; he no sooner got the stone to the top than it rolled down again into the valley below.

So he had to work on, all the time rolling the stone up the hill but never getting his task accomplished. What harder punishment could one have than to be compelled to work hard without ever accomplishing anything? Some people at the end of a year, or ten years, are just about where they were when they started. They have no will power to save what they get, or they spend their money foolishly in buying what they cannot afford. If one is thrifty he gets ahead; he wins the respect of his neighbors; he can back public enterprises; he can borrow money at the bank if he needs to; he is looked up to as amounting to something. In short, he is a substantial citizen who pays taxes and is able to support worthy causes.

MAKING A LIVING IN THE COMMUNITY 99 

Keep an account for a month, or a year, of what you receive and what you spend. Are you earning money?

If so, are you saving any of it, or do you spend it as fast as you make it?

Franklin and National Thrift Week. Benjamin 

Franklin was one of the wisest of men. In his famous "Poor Richard's Almanac" he taught his countrymen the principles of thrift. His birthday is January 17 (1706) and the week of 

January 15 to 21 is now "National Thrift Week."

During that week the children in school and at home ought to be reminded of the thrift and sound common sense of Dr. Franklin as expressed in his fa 

BENJAMIN FRANKLIN 

miliar maxims. Here are some of them: 

No man e'er was glorious 

Who was not laborious.

Laziness travels so slowly that Poverty soon overtakes him.

Early to bed, early to rise, makes a man healthy, wealthy, and wise.

God helps them that help themselves. 

The early bird catches the worm.

Be up and doing; He that lives on Hope will die fasting.

At the working-man's house, Hunger looks in but dares not enter.

Diligence is the mother of Good Luck.

Poverty often deprives a man of all spirit and virtue. 

It is hard for an empty bag to stand upright. 

Franklin meant that a man must own something to be able to stand up well in the community. 

Have you a Thrift Club in your school? How much can the children save in their little banks in a year, if they do without candy, gum, or some of the "movies"? 

Have your local banks "Christmas Funds" to encourage Christmas Saving?

Is it not true that many persons who are now poor or are living on small wages are those who have failed to practice thrift or have failed to improve the opportunities that have come to them? 

5. Wise Spending Habits: Self-Denial and Econ omy. Economy consists in choosing what we shall have, within what we can afford. It is giving up less important things in order to have more important things. Practicing thrift consists largely in choosing well and spending wisely. A boy may have a chance to take a job and earn wages. Is it not wiser to stay in school at a sacrifice and prepare himself for a larger job?

If the average wage earner gets $800 a year and the average high school graduate receives $1,300 a year how well would it pay a boy financially in twenty years to stay in school four years longer?

A poor boy's bare feet were on the cold ground in winter. A good woman gave him a pair of shoes. The next day the boy swapped the shoes for an air gun.

What can be done for such a boy? How can people be taught to buy what they really need instead of wasting money on foolish desires? 

Honesty. Behind one's occupation, behind industry and thrift and spending habits, stands honesty. Straightforward honesty is the foundation of true character. 

It is so important to success in life that it needs more attention in our daily lessons. Honest work, honest play, honest study, honest words, honest conduct, honest service these are at the basis of life. 

Honesty means honor, uprightness, truth-telling. The honest man will not cheat nor steal nor lie; he will respect the property rights of others; he will be faithful to his trust, and "may look the whole world in the face." He is not afraid. He has nothing to conceal. 

He deals openly and frankly. He inspires confidence.

Men trust him and are ready to promote him to places of honor and responsibility. He is not honest merely for policy's sake, but because his life is based on truth and honor. Verily, "an honest man is the noblest work of God."

The bad effect of dishonesty is certain whether it be cheating at play or in examination, or loafing on the job, or getting unearned credit, or deceiving mother, teacher or friend. The result will be weakness of character and failure in the end. There will be loss of self respect, of reputation or good name, the surrender of noble qualities to an ignoble life. You are being tested day by day, at home, in school, at work or play. Your habits are revealing your character, as to your studies, your school spirit, your truthtelling, your whole life. When you go out into the

MAKING A LIVING IN THE COMMUNITY 103 

business world you will have to stand the same kind of tests and examinations, as you meet in school. 

The business firm that employs you will want to know all about you, how you care for your work, whether you have the right spirit, whether you are honest.

School life and business life are one life. If you are to succeed at all you must succeed now. If you keep on failing now there will be no place nor use for you later. 

A boy at West Point cheated in examination. When the upper classmen learned of it, a committee of cadets visited him and said: "You might as well pack your things. You cannot stay here. You do not understand the spirit of West Point and you don't belong here."

The cheat left the next day. His life was out of harmony with the spirit of the place. 

How does a student who passes an examination dishonestly hurt the school? How does he hurt himself?

The pupil who cheats in school lies when he hands in work as his own which he did not do; he steals when he takes as his own information which does not belong to him. Why is this practice likely to get him into trouble in his adult life? What are you doing in your school to check such practices? Are you leaving it to your teachers to do? 

What is the "honor system" in a school? Do you think it would work in your school? Why? How can we make our school a school of honor? Some have said: "Let each pupil look only to his own work." "Let each pupil refuse to let any one copy his work." "If any pupil takes what does not belong to him let us get other pupils to go to the offender and tell him that this sort of thing cannot go on in our school." What do you think of these answers? Will you give others?

It is usually a wise plan to put some of your savings into life insurance. Good Government for the Honest Citizen.

Any one when earning a living or saving for a home or to establish himself in business depends upon the good government of the community every day of his life. He must know what he can rely upon. There must be order in the community; his right to work must be recognized; his business and property must be protected. 

If mobs and riots and robberies were allowed there would be no encouragement to work and save. The savings of years might be swept away in an hour. No one could plan for any time ahead. So the thrifty citizen is especially interested in the preservation of law and order in the community. 

He wants a stable government that can be depended upon to secure these blessings and to protect the equal rights of all its citizens under just and equal laws.

MAKING A LIVING IN THE COMMUNITY 105  

1. Are the American people extravagant or thrifty? Have you heard that a French family can live on what an American family throws away? Can this be so? 

2. One man said he had bought an automobile with the money he had saved by not using tobacco for twenty-five years. 

Could that be so? 

3. What wasteful habits have come under your observation? 

4. What uses can be made of the family garbage? How is it disposed of? 

5. Will honesty ever cause loss or sacrifice? How do you feel toward a boy who cheats in a game? Do you like him? 

Can you trust him at all? 

6. What are debts of honor? Did you ever know of a man who paid large sums of money on debts which the law did not compel him to pay? Why should he do so? See the life of Mark Twain. 

7. If a man gives up all his property to pay his debts and the property is not enough, is he then released from further obligation? Why do you think so? What is it to be a bankrupt? What is meant by "taking advantage of the bankruptcy act"? 

8. Is it honest to contract a debt that you are not reasonably sure of being able to pay? Should debts ever be incurred for the purchase of pleasures? How would this be "a dishonest abuse of credit"? 

9. Is there much borrowing among the pupils in your school? 

Is this a dangerous practice? Would you be a good friend by refusing to lend to an habitual borrower?

CHAPTER VIII : 

COMMUNITY: PROBLEMS OF LABOR AND CAPITAL 

Blessed are the horny hands of toil. 

LOWELL 

The discussion on making a living leads us to a consideration of our industrial life. Here we turn to the reasons why men work, and to the three sources of wealth: natural resources, labor and capital. In the conflicts between labor and capital, we learn what causes strikes, the lockout, the open and closed shops, collective bargaining, and the real purpose of the labor unions. An understanding of all of these is necessary to the boy and the girl citizen before entering the industrial community. 

The industrial community of workers is the millions of men and women who live by their bodily toil. If you will look around you in the community you will see that most men and women are working for a living. 

They go to work in the morning and come home from work in the evening, while the housekeepers at home work nearly all day. There are a few who do not work the loafers who are too lazy, the rich who are too idle, the sports who are too trifling, the men and women of leisure who give themselves to travel, pleasure and trying to find amusement; and the old and the feeble who are rightly excused. All these are but few 

107

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

compared to the great body of workers who make up the industrial community of the world. wants. 

Why do Men Work? Men work to satisfy their

The more advanced they are in civilization the more they want and the harder they work. Half civilized people have very few wants. The Eskimo in a cold climate stays in a snow dug-out, lives on fish and blubber and sleeps most of the time. He has about all he wants. In some warm countries the backward inhabitants grow slothful and lazy. They can satisfy their few wants without much work. As the climate is warm they need few clothes. They are satisfied with a rude hut for shelter. Their food comes handy, from fruits and fish and game. They never think of trying to invent anything or of changing the world for the better. It is only when their wants increase that men begin to bestir themselves and set to work. 

Wants of Civilized Men. When the civilized man has satisfied the bare needs of his body food, shelter, clothing he begins to think of other needs. He thinks of better food, more clothes, finer homes, costlier furniture. Then social and cultural wants arise, the desire for books, pictures, education, automobiles, leisure, travel, recreation and amusement.

Working for Wealth. So men begin to work for wealth, to satisfy their wants. Now there are three chief sources of wealth natural resources, labor, capital. Let us think briefly of these three. 

1. Natural Resources. What are natural resources?

Such things as land and the soil, water and water power, mines of gold and silver, coal and iron, zinc

and lead, oil, forests of timber, and quarries of granite and stone. Man did not make these sources of wealth; nature made them. Man can destroy or waste them, or leave them unused, but he can do very little to add to them. He can enrich the soil, he can drain the swamp, at some places he can recover some land from lake or sea; but little can he do to add to land or water. These great resources on sea and land and under the land are the bounties of nature, and it would seem that God has intended them for the benefit of all. 

America is a rich land because it has such an abundance of natural resources. 

2. Labor. These great resources of nature cannot be made useful to man without labor. They must be developed, worked up, and brought out. The soil may be rich but it will not produce full crops nor abundant food without the labor of man. These great resources will go to waste if man does not work with hand, body, and brain. 

3. Capital. In applying his labor to the land man needs some form of capital. We often think of money as capital, and we believe that if a man has money he can hire some one, or buy some seed or machine to use on the land. But money is only one form of capital. 

Capital existed before money, and may exist without it. It may be in the form of a tool or implement knife, a gun, or net, or spade or wooden plow, anything a man may make with which to work. It may be some domestic animal, a horse, a cow, a dog which man has tamed for his use. 

Can you imagine a man going on the land emptyhanded and bare-handed, to make his living? Could he

IIO ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

CAPITAL: THE FARMER'S AND THE MANUFACTURER'S 

The stock, machinery and buildings on a farm are capital, just as surely as are the factories and equipment of the modern industrial plant.

III live by gathering nuts from trees and berries from bushes? Or by catching fish with his hands or spearing them with a sharp stick? Or by killing and skinning wild animals without any tool? Man will use his wits and begin to invent things. Out of a flint he will make a knife, out of a stone a hammer, out of a sapling a spear with which to hunt, and out of some wood fiber he will make a net with which to catch his fish. He will set snares and traps for his game. In his tent or cabin home he will devise some kind of utensils to add to his comfort. He will hitch a dog to a rough sled to transport his food or his goods. 

The First Forms of Capital. These early tools, inventions, and animals were man's first capital. With whatever he found at hand he labored on the land and produced some form of wealth. If he did not consume this wealth nor waste it, but used it to produce more wealth, he then had capital. Capital is saved wealth used in producing more wealth. Every time a man invented a tool he increased the productive power of his labor. Now his inventions are so wonderful, his progress so great, his saved-up wealth so enormous that society finds itself in possession of capital in multiplied forms, not only in money and credit, but in all kinds of factories, mills, improved farms, live stock, mines, railroads, rolling stock, cars and trucks and buildings of all kinds.

Changing Conditions of Labor. A little over a century ago nearly everything was made by hand. The shoemakers, blacksmiths, carpenters, cabinetmakers, masons, had their own tools, and many had their own shops. A boy would become an apprentice to a master workman to be taught a trade. The master workman

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

was generally his own employer, his own boss. Some men worked for others, but the employers worked, too. 

They were all of the same social class, working, eating, and having their frolics together. Often the hired workmen married into the families of their employers. 

Their tools were simple and old-fashioned the flail, the ox-cart, the wooden plow, the spinning wheel, the hand loom. 

This has all been changed. Inventions and laborsaving machinery have brought about a new world of labor. Water and wind, steam and electricity have been harnessed to powerful machines in great mills and factories. The workers have left their home industries, their little neighborhood shops, and have gone into great factories to handle machines, or parts of machines by which one man can produce a thousand times as much as he could before. The great modern machines, like the cotton gin, the spinning jenny, the power loom, the steam engine, the wonderful printing presses, increased enormously the output of manufactured goods. The wealth of the world has increased by leaps and bounds. Such was the industrial revolution. 

Conflicts Between Capital and Labor. This new wealth came largely into the hands of comparatively few men. The laborer found himself but little better off, in some ways not so well off. He was not so independent nor so free. Now the laborers had to go into mines, mills, factories, railroad shops, and on railroad trains all belonging to the owners, the capitalists. 

Thousands of workmen were employed in one industrial plant. They no longer owned the tools with which they worked. They had nothing but their wages. They

worked at machines, and under monotony and drudgery they became almost like machines themselves. 

The owners of the factories wished to make as much money as possible. They wanted cheap labor; they wanted men to work long hours for low wages. These capitalists owned not only the factory, but the machinery and the tools with which the workmen had to earn their living. The owners fixed the wages and the hours of labor, paying no more than they were compelled to pay. They imported cheap labor from Europe, and sometimes cheaper labor from Asia. In Europe the laborer worked for a mere pittance, fifty cents a day or less. As the immigrants entered, the labor market became "glutted" - thousands of men seeking employment at any price. If the American workman objected when his wages were lowered he was told he could leave his job, and if he left it the owners could find plenty of workmen ready to take it. 

Under these circumstances the workmen had ceased to know their employers; there was no contact or sympathy or understanding between them. Foremen were employed with power to "hire and fire" the men, and these foremen had too little regard for the welfare of the workers. All this came from the "industrial revolution."

Standard of Living. It had come to pass that the workmen were living almost at the mercy of the capitalist owners. Their wages were low, about at the "edge of existence"; their hours were long; their conditions of labor in many ways unhealthful; and the company houses, or shacks, in which they lived were miserable and poor. The bare necessities of life did

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

not satisfy the American standard of living. The workman very rightly believed that he should be able to own a home of his own, support his family in decent comfort, and be able to send his children to school and provide for their needs, with books, good food and warm clothing. The laborer, like other men, is entitled to rest and recreation. He should be able to lay 

KEYSTONE WORKMEN'S HOMES 

These neat houses have been built for the use of workers in one of Michigan's large automobile plants. aside something "for a rainy day," and to carry some life insurance for the protection of his family if he should be called away.

Labor Unions. The factory system and the wage system were not giving the workman this standard of living. How could he better his condition? How could he keep his head above the poverty line and maintain a standard of life that every self-respecting head of a household ought to have? Intelligent laboring men concluded that they must organize and look out for themselves. If laboring men could all act

together they could better protect their own interests and make better bargains with their employers. So

Labor Unions were organized. They were first trade unions; that is, the men were organized according to different lines of work carpenters, miners, bricklayers, stone cutters, railway engineers, etc., each group of laborers having a Union of its own. These trade unions co-operated, or were merged, in one large federation, which is known as the American Federation of Labor. The laborers by their Union seek to protect one another against unfair treatment, lowered wages or unjust discharge. The members pay dues which go to make up a fund to insure them against sickness or unemployment, or to be used in case of a strike.

The main purpose of the Union is to unite all laboring men and have them act together for their common interest, on the principle, "united we stand, divided we fall," or, "an injury to one is an injury to all."

The Strike. A strike may come when the workmen feel they have a grievance. If in some industry wages should be reduced, or the conditions of labor are not good, the Unions may vote to call a strike in that industry. Then all the men (if they obey the strike order) lay down their tools and walk out, or quit work in a body. If all workmen were unionized and acted together there would be no other workers to take the place of the strikers; the industry would then be 'paralyzed" or "tied up," and the owners would have to come to terms or quit business. 

But labor is not so perfectly organized. There are non-union workers, men who have not joined the Unions, and during a strike many of these are ready to

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

take the place of the strikers. The Union strikers call these men "scabs" and "strike-breakers." While a strike is going on the Union men sometimes seek to prevent these so-called "scabs" from working, sometimes by persuasion, sometimes by forms of violence. 

The strikers resort to picketing; that is, they send out 

INTERN STRIKING WORKMEN ON PARADE 

These Rhode Island strikers want to show their strength and to win sympathy for their cause. some of their men called pickets, who stand near the gates of the factory, or along the approaches to it, and try to persuade the non-union men coming to work to leave their jobs and join the strikers. They are offered strike pay, instead of their wages, to leave their "unfair" employer. If these methods fail, the strikers sometimes resort to harder arguments, in abuse, threats, or some form of coercion.

Employers' Unions. The employers have their

unions, too. There is a National Association of Employers. Labor Unions charge that these employers keep "strike-breakers" in reserve, ready to be transported to any place where a strike occurs. The employers do not like to have their workmen controlled by Union leaders, or the "walking delegate," the paid agent of the Unions who goes from place to place trying to unionize the workmen. Some employers try to prevent their men from organizing, sometimes discharging them if they join a Union. This leads to a conflict between the closed shop and the open shop. 

The Open and the Closed Shop. The closed shop is one closed to all laborers who are not members of the Union. The open shop is one open to non-union as well as Union men, though usually in an open shop Union men are not employed. The Union men, as a rule, do not wish to work in shops along with nonunion men. They want all the workmen in the shops to be ready to stand together for the protection of labor interests under the direction of chosen leaders. 

Collective Bargaining. The Unions stand for collective bargaining; that is, instead of having the foreman or employer make a bargain as to wages and hours with each workman, the Union men wish to bargain as a group, through some leader or committee. Instead of each carpenter making his contract with some boss building contractor, a thousand carpenters in the Carpenters' Union give to their officers the power to make a contract for all. If each workman had to stand alone, his employer might be able to make harder terms with him. But if none of the men will agree to work unless all do, the men can come nearer to getting

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

what they want. The contractor can get no carpenter unless he agrees to the terms laid down for all. If the employer agrees to collective bargaining, then a wage scale is drawn up in that industry and agreed to by both sides, to last for a year or more. 

The wage scale 

COAL MINERS AT WORK 

When these men stop work for a period, in protest against working conditions or low wages, the price of coal is increased and the public is greatly inconvenienced. may be renewed from time to time. If one side attempts to change the scale a new labor struggle may arise. The disputes are usually about how the profits of industry shall be divided. The Labor Unions have been a strong factor in securing a larger share for labor. 

Strikes in Basic Industries. There are certain basic industries like mining and railroading. Society can-

not well exist unless these keep going. If coal mining ceases and the railroads stop running the factories would have to close from lack of fuel and shipping facilities. Every one is dependent on these industries. 

There is always some uneasiness lest the coal miners. and the operators cannot agree and a strike will come and the factories and homes will be left without fuel. 

There have been a number of disastrous coal strikes with great loss to workers and operators and to the general public. In the great coal strike of 1902 the price of hard coal (anthracite) went as high as $30 a ton. Industries closed down and people suffered. The coal managers were trying to starve the coal miners out. 

The miners were ready to arbitrate but the managers refused, and President Roosevelt, backed by public opinion, demanded a settlement. He let it be understood that the people must have coal and if the operators would not agree to reasonable terms the Government would take charge of the mines and put the miners to work. When Roosevelt's intention became known the coal mine owners came to terms. In 1925 and 1926 there was another long coal strike which caused much suffering. President Coolidge did not interfere to bring the strike to an end, but pressure was brought to bear by legislatures, business organizations and members of Congress, and the strike was brought to a close by an adjustment which left the coal industry pretty much as it was at the start of the trouble.

Inter-dependence. The truth is that in industrial life one class depends upon another. The laborer depends upon the capitalist as well as the capitalist

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

upon the laborer. The capitalist who starts a factory or an enterprise may be a great benefactor to the community. He may be a man of vision, seeing an opportunity better than others. He may have organizing 

JUSTICE PAYS BIG DIVIDENDS EWING CALLOW AN INDUSTRIAL DINING ROOM 

This Ohio factory furnishes lunch with music to its employees at a minimum price. Such consideration makes the employees loyal, happy workers. capacity and brains to contribute to the work. He is capable of planning and directing, and he is willing to risk his money in the enterprise. His skill and foresight and energy may be the means of building up a great industrial enterprise which may afford employment to thousands of self-supporting and home-owning laborers. Such a capitalist should have his reward. 

Many such capitalists are anxious for the welfare of the workers. They seek to co-operate with their em-

ployees, to provide for pension systems, profit sharing, recreational opportunities, and as short hours and as high wages as the business will permit. In industrial conflicts the capitalists are not always to blame. The 

Labor Unions and their leaders may be as unreasonable and as unfair as the capitalists. There may be blame on both sides. The object is to co-operate, to respect the rights of one another, and by working together to promote the interest of all.

1. How can labor wars be prevented? Should the two contending sides - labor and capital - be compelled to submit their quarrel to the decision of some fair board of arbitration? 

2. Would profit sharing be a good thing, by which if profits are large the laborers are paid more, if small they are paid less? If there were losses do you think the laborers would be willing to share them?

CHAPTER IX BUYING AND SELLING IN THE COMMUNITY: 

HOW BANKS SERVE THE COMMUNITY 

Money is a good servant, but a bad master. BACON

Money is needed in buying and selling; it is called a measure of value. Its value depends upon the supply and demand. Following the nature and uses of money, we come to the kinds of money and to the Federal Reserve System with its Regional Banks. We see how banks serve the community; how the 

Postal Savings Banks function and how the government seeks to prevent bad banking. We shall also see why weights and measures must conform to some standard.

In our modern communities we all are dependent on one another. Most of us cannot raise our own food, or build our own houses, or teach our own children, or make our own books, furniture, and dishes. Nearly everything we have is produced by some one else. One man works at one thing, another at another, and each man exchanges what he makes for what he wants that some one else makes, or for some service that another can render. It is our system of exchange, of buying and selling, that provides the comforts and luxuries of our civilized life. This exchange of goods we call trade. 

The Use of Money. Money is an invention to make 123

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

TYPES OF SERVICE WE APPRECIATE 

We are daily dependent upon the postal service for communication, upon the market gardener for fresh food, upon the road worker for safety and comfort upon the streets and highways, and upon mechanics for necessary repairs.

trade easier. If there were no money, trade would have to be by barter, that is, exchanging one thing for another. If a man wants a horse he can offer two cows, and perhaps a man who wants two cows will be ready to give a horse for them. Barter still goes on in places where there may be a scarcity of money. 

Can you explain how trading by barter would be difficult and inconvenient? How would you make change? 

Money as a Medium of Exchange. If there were no money men would have to invent some. Without it there would be no end of trouble. In our modern life money seems to be a necessity. It is the means by which exchanges are made. The man with the pig, or the hat, or the shoes, or the horse - each can sell what he has for money and with the money buy what he wants. This instrument of exchange, which we call money, passes freely from hand to hand for goods; everybody is willing to accept it because he knows he can exchange it for what he wants. 

Explain the use of money. Tell why nearly everybody is so eager to get money. A great scientist was once offered $1000 for a lecture. He refused and said, “I have no time to make money." How do you explain this? Is not money-making the very thing to which most people give up their time? Name some things money cannot buy. 

Money is a Measure of Value. Money is called "a measure of value.' This means that we express values in terms of money, or we price things in money. We say a horse is worth $100, a hat $5, a bushel of wheat

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

AUFMANN & MAY CO. 

CHICAGO SCHMENBURG FORVICE 

WHOLESALE PRODUCE MARKET, CHICAGO

$1, a suit of clothes $40, an acre of land $200, a house and lot $10,000. But do not make the mistake of thinking that the value of the dollar as a measure of value is steady and unchanging. The value of money is what it will buy. A hundred dollars will not always buy the same amount of goods; sometimes more, sometimes less. If money in circulation is plentiful, it is cheap, it will not buy so much; if scarce it is dear, it will then buy more. At one time a dollar has bought two bushels of wheat, at another only half a bushel. 

During the business depression that began in 1929, people found that $100 would buy as much as $150 or more would have bought in 1928. Thus money varies in its buying power, or value. 

Money Is Made by Law: Supply Regulated by Gov ernment. To coin money is the function of the nation, and that which is made into money must be money for all alike. So Congress is given power "to coin money and regulate the value thereof." No State may do so.

Congress determines what metals may be coined, the weight and fineness of each coin, which paper money shall be acceptable for taxes, and it regulates the value of all kinds of money by fixing the amount of metal in the coin or the quantity of money in circulation. Nothing can be made into money except what the law decrees. If an individual attempts to coin money on his own account he is a counterfeiter, and the law officers will hunt him down. 

Paper Money Must be Limited and Backed by the

Government. No gold is just now in circulation. It has given way to paper, which is more convenient. 

The value of paper money depends not only on its

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

quantity, but also on the government that issues it. credit and stability of the 

If it is uncertain whether the government will endure; if it is not strong enough to collect taxes or honest enough to pay its debts; if it cannot preserve order so trade and industry can go
