on;

UNITED STATES MINT IN PHILADELPHIA 

Most of our silver coins and millions of pennies are made here. if it cannot enforce contracts and say how debts are to be paid and force them to be paid, then the paper money which the government issues will be worthless.

The Gold Standard Abandoned. The Government stands behind its paper money, limits the quantity of it and provides that it shall be accepted for debts and taxes in city, county, state and nation. In 1900 Congress adopted the gold standard by providing for re-

demption of paper money in gold. This had long been the custom though the law did not require it. 

By an act of January 30, 1934, Congress abandoned. the gold standard. The act authorized the President to take into the U. S. Treasury all the gold in the country and to devalue the gold dollar from 40 to 50 per cent. 

The President revalued the gold dollar at a little less than 60 cents. Instead of having 25.8 grains of gold (23.2 pure gold) it would have about 

15.25 grains. 

Kinds of Money. The money in general use most familiar to us is of silver, gold, and paper. Small change, of course, is of copper and nickel. Some paper money is in the form of gold and silver certificates, for every dollar of which there is in the United States 

Treasury a gold or silver dollar. Then there are United

States Notes, National Bank Notes, and Federal Re serve Notes, each of which bears the Government's promise to pay to the holder the amount on the face of the note.

Federal Reserve Act. By the Federal Reserve Act the country is divided into twelve districts, and in a principal city of each district a Federal Reserve Bank is located. The cities are Boston, New York, Philadelphia, Richmond, Atlanta, Cleveland, Minneapolis, Chicago, Dallas, St. Louis, Kansas City, San Francisco.

All the National Banks must, and State Banks may, become members of the Federal Reserve Bank for their respective regions. The Reserve Bank for each region is called the Regional Bank. The Regional Bank does business only with its member banks. Its stock is not owned by individuals but by the member

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

banks. It is a bank for banks and does business only with banks.

The Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System. This Board consists of seven Governors appointed by the President, each with a 14-year term at a salary of $15,000. The Secretary of the Treasury 

KEYSTONE MAKING PENNIES 

Metal sheets coming from the machine after the pennies have been stamped out. and the Comptroller of the Currency are omitted as ex officio members. This board was created by the 

Banking Act of 1935 which continues Federal insurance for bank deposits and provides for more liberal credit when needed by allowing safe loans on reasonable security. If credit is cut down, or fewer Federal

Reserve notes are issued, the local banks cannot make so many loans to borrowers who apply for money. 

Checks, Credit, and Credit Instruments. Very little business is done on a cash basis. Most debts and bills are paid by credit instruments such as drafts, checks, money orders. A draft is an order drawn by one bank on another for the payment of money. When one wishes to pay a debt or a bill in some distant city, he is likely to send a bank draft or a post office money order, or a personal check on his home bank. A check is merely an order on a bank for so much money. It is not good unless the one who writes it, or gives it, has money in the bank to the amount of the check. If one writes a check on a bank in which he has no money he is guilty of a crime and may be punished. That is an attempt to get money under false pretenses.

How the Bank Serves the Community. 1. The bank is a place for the safe keeping of money or valuables. 

Your money is safer in a good bank than in an old stocking or in a trunk or in some hidden place about the house. In the bank are vaults and great steel safes which can be opened only by those who know the combination of the lock. There are also safe deposit boxes for valuable papers stocks, bonds and diamonds and jewelry. 

2. The bank is a place for loans and discounts. 

Formerly men loaned money to one another more than they do now. In these days if a man wishes to borrow money he goes to his bank. Since many persons deposit their money in the bank for safe keeping the bank will have on hand a great amount of money. A large part of this the bank can safely lend

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

to farmers and business men to help them carry on their business. 

3. The community bank usually seeks to encourage thrift. It advises its customers about investments, and by its checks and drafts it makes easier and safer the transfer of money or credit, from place to place. 

EDWIN LEVICK N. Y. 

A SAFE DEPOSIT VAULT 

The walls are lined with small steel safes or boxes where valuable jewels and documents may be kept secure against burglars and fire. 

Postal Savings Banks. To encourage thrift and provide a safe place for savings Congress established a system of Postal Savings Banks. By an Act of 1910 

every post office may be used as a savings bank. 

Uncle Sam can be trusted. If a person places money in a post office, a certificate of deposit will be given him, he will receive a small rate of interest (2%) and he will know that his money is absolutely safe. The

Postmaster deposits this money in reliable local banks and the banks pay the Government as much interest as the Government pays the owner. The banks lend this money for business purposes. Many people who distrust private banks trust the Government. Therefore much money is brought out of hiding into active. use, and at the same time thrifty saving is encouraged. 

Protection Against Bad Banking. Banking laws pro tect the people against fraudulent banking. The Government issues a charter to a bank and requires it to make regular public reports of its condition, Government bank examiners inspect the banks and require them to obey the law. The New Banking Act of 1935 allows banks which are members of the Reserve System to make loans on farm, home, and business properties up to one-half of their value. Money deposited in banks insured with the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation. is insured up to $5,000 for any one depositor in any one bank. These two features will benefit farmers, home owners, and bank depositors.

Weights and Measures. As stated, money is a measure of value, the manufacture of which is controlled by the Government, that it may conform to certain standards. After rising above the savage state men felt the need of some kind of measurement of quantity and quality in order to buy and sell. We have to measure time, distance, weight, area. Nature has fixed our time-piece the earth's rotation on its axis makes a day. Other measuring things come by man's device.

Because the weights and measures used by individuals were changing and inaccurate, governments found it necessary to fix certain standards and impose penal-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

ties for false weights and measures. The standard is some weight or measure or instrument by which we compare the weights and measures of other things by which things must stand while being measured. The standard is now very accurately determined. Our 

KADEL & HERBERT, N. Y. 

TESTING FRAUDULENT WEIGHTS AND MEASURES 

Punishment awaits the merchant who cheats his customers by the use of false bottoms in his basket, or by weighted scales. 

Bureau of Standards at Washington has an official platinum measure of length and weight kept carefully guarded. By these, merchants' scales and yardsticks are tested from time to time, and if they are not up to the standard the offenders may be punished. Congress has power to regulate weights and measures and it is the business of government to protect the people against false standards in trade.

Has your city an inspector of weights and measures? 

Are your coal dealer's scales tested occasionally? If the milk man or vegetable man or coal dealer is not giving fair weight and measure how can it be corrected?

CHAPTER X TAXES: HOW THE COMMUNITY PAYS ITS EXPENSES 

In this world nothing is sure but death and taxes.

-BENJAMIN FRANKLIN 

The expenses and benefits of government are shared by the citizens of a community and must be paid for by means of taxes. 

Every citizen should understand the principles of taxation. In this Chapter we shall become acquainted with the different kinds of taxes, and see how they are collected, how the taxing power is limited, and why taxes increase with the growing expenses of government.

It takes money to run the Government. Take the 

National Government, State Government, city and local governments, and then think of all their expenses. 

Think of the money required to have good schools, to erect school buildings, to pay the superintendents, teachers, janitors; to build good roads and pay road engineers; to buy the cement and road material; to build the bridges and culverts. In the cities there must be good streets and sidewalks; there must be policemen, firemen, judges, sheriffs and other officers; there must be jails, court houses and poor-houses, libraries and playgrounds. There are State Colleges and Universities and all have expenses for laboratories and teachers. Then there are the salaries of the Presi- 136

dent, Vice-President, Congressmen and Judges, and a half million soldiers and sailors; and over 500,000 civil service office-holders. Great sums are paid for public buildings and improvements.

See if you can find out from the World Almanac the total expense of government in the United States, how much money is raised in taxes for all purposes, national, State, and local. How much apiece for every man, woman and child?

The Nature of a Tax. All these sums are paid in taxes. They are not voluntary offerings of whatever amount the citizen chooses to give. A tax is a compulsory contribution to the community for public purposes. Nobody agrees upon the amount of his own taxes; they are levied on him and collected from him without agreement on his part. People cannot be left to decide for themselves whether they will pay or how much they will pay. If they were, some people would not pay at all; they would not be willing to pay their fair share of the expenses and burdens of the community.

How a Tax is Collected. If a citizen refuses to pay his taxes the court may order his land or his house to be sold, or may order the sheriff to seize his movable personal property and sell it for taxes. Thus the tax may be collected.

If a tax is not paid in due time on real property (land and houses) the property is advertised as delinquent and then after the owner has had a reasonable time in which to pay the tax the property may be sold at a sheriff's sale. The sheriff is the county officer who is

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

charged with collecting the tax or enforcing the law. 

A man with no property cannot be forced to pay a tax, unless he has an income which can be taxed. 

A man without property may have a poll tax, or voting tax imposed upon him. Is this a fair tax? Why? 

Can a man be jailed for not paying a tax? It 

Taxes are not Paid for Services Received. should be noticed that taxes, besides being compulsory, are not paid in proportion to services received. When you buy postage stamps you are not paying a tax, you are paying for a service, to have your letters, papers or parcels carried just as you would pay an Express Company for the same service. When you ride on a railway train you pay for transportation, and if you travel 200 miles you must expect to pay twice as much fare as for only 100 miles. But if one pays $200 in taxes he does not expect to get twice as much service from the Government as if he paid only $100. It has been claimed that a citizen should pay in taxes according to the benefits received. This would be an unsound and unfair plan. It would mean that a rich man with no children should pay no school tax, as he would get no benefit from the schools, while a poor man with many children in school should pay a high tax. It would be impossible to determine the benefit each individual receives from the Government. It is not true to say that a rich man without children receives no benefit from the public school.

Every citizen whether rich or poor, is interested in good government, in law and order, in peace and justice, in the training of good citizens, in the cleanliness

of the streets, and in the restraint of crime. All these are promoted by enlightenment and education. These benefits come to all alike and each citizen should pay for them according to his means. 

Principles of Taxation. I. That each citizen should pay according to his ability is one of the first principles of taxation. Some have much, some have little; let them pay accordingly. A man's ability to pay may not depend upon the amount of property he has, but upon the income it produces or the profits from his business.

There are two pieces of land. One is laid out in forest trees with no expectation of a crop for fifty years; the other is used to raise corn and hogs. Which should pay the higher tax? 

2. All should be treated fairly without favors to any class. There may justly be some exemptions. Some may have so little that it may seem hardly fair or wise to tax them at all. The tax gatherer should leave the poor with at least a decent living. 

3. The tax should be assessed publicly, collected by public officers, should always be for public purposes, and no assessment should be greater than the amount needed. A surplus in the treasury is likely to lead to extravagances, waste and corruption. 

Sometimes tax "ferrets" are employed to ferret out, or find out, property that is escaping taxation. The owners have not made their property known to the assessor. 

These "ferrets" are paid a certain per cent, perhaps half, of all the taxes they can bring into the treasury by discovering this hidden property. The tax "ferrets" are not known to the public. Is this a good system?

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

4. The taxes should be levied and collected with the least possible trouble and annoyance to the people. 

A French statesman once said that the art of taxing was like that of plucking a goose, the object being "to get the largest amount of feathers with the least amount of squawking." There would be less squawking, or 

A BAD ROAD 

A road like this is expensive because it slows up business, besides causing discomfort and inconvenience. We are dependent upon the highways for the necessities of life. complaining, about the taxes if it could be known that the tax money would not be wasted, and that the people would really get value received for what they pay, and that everybody was paying his fair share of the public expenses. It is hard to bring this about, but that is the goal toward which we should strive. 

Kinds of Taxes. Taxes are levied for town, city, county, state, and nation. Some are direct, some indirect. 

1 Cited in Munro and Ozanne's "Social Civics," p. 255.

A direct tax is one that is placed on a man's lands, houses, personal property, such as household goods, bonds, notes, mortgages, crops, etc.

This is the general property tax. This seeks to take in everything a man owns, on the principle "if you see an article, tax it." It has been said that this kind of tax cannot 

A GOOD ROAD 

The State's program of highway improvement is producing roads like this. 

The taxes in the community are put to good use when they are spent to improve roads. be shifted to any one else, but this is a mistake. For instance, a man owns a house or a farm which he rents to some one else. If the taxes are raised the owner raises the rent, so the renter pays the tax. If a man owns a mortgage on a man's land and has to pay taxes on the mortgage, he charges more interest from the borrower; so the borrower has to pay the tax on the mortgage. Every one who rents, or hires, or buys anything is charged more because of taxes. So taxes are constantly entering into the cost of living.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Most taxes are indirect, that is, a tax that is shifted and paid by some other person than the one from whom it is collected. The tariff, which is a tax on imports, is a good illustration. An importer of silk may pay 50 per cent of its value to the United States Government as a tariff tax, but he immediately charges the merchant 

EWING GALLOWAY SUBJECT TO THE GENERAL PROPERTY TAX 

The owner of this farm pays taxes on his land and buildings and on his personal property which includes furniture, tools, livestock, securities, etc. and the merchant charges the buyer 50 per cent more for the silks. So the buyer actually pays the tax. A cigar maker puts up a box of cigars on which he has to pay to the Government a tax for a stamp on every box. 

Of course he adds the amount of the tax to the price of the cigars. The one who buys the cigars pays the tax, indirectly. So almost all the taxes collected by the 

U. S. Government are indirect, except the income tax. 

A tariff is specific or ad valorem. It is specific if it

is a certain amount per yard, per pound, or per dozen, of the goods. It is ad valorem if it is a certain per cent of the value of the goods. 

A tariff is protective if it is levied not merely to get revenue but to protect the manufacturer or producer from foreign competition in the home market. If a tax is placed on foreign goods when they are imported, the American manufacturer can put a higher price on his goods than he otherwise could. 

A tariff is prohibitive if it is so high that the owners or importers of foreign goods cannot afford to bring them into the country at all. To have a protective tariff is to use the taxing power for industrial purposes, to promote business, manufacturing and industry. 

From the very beginning of our government there have been those who have contended that such use of the taxing power is unconstitutional, or improper, and that all tariffs and taxes should be "for revenue only," not to promote one business or another but only to get enough money to run the Government. Nowadays we do not frequently hear this argument. 

An external tax is one levied on goods coming from outside our country, like the tariff. An internal tax, or an excise, is one levied on the production, consumption or exchange of goods within the country, or on some business transaction. The stamp tax was an excise tax which helped to cause the American Revolution. The whisky tax was an excise tax, which caused the "Whisky Rebellion." The farmers of Western

Pennsylvania did not like to have to pay a tax for turning their grains into liquors. A tax on tickets to the theater or the "movie" is an excise tax. The

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

showman adds the tax to the price of his tickets and those who go to the show have to pay it. Thus, nearly all excise taxes are indirect. 

Is there a gasoline tax in your State? How much per gallon? Who pays it? What is the money used for? 

KEYSTONE CUSTOMS INSPECTORS AT WORK 

These men are examining and appraising imported goods as they are unloaded from the incoming vessels. The importer must pay the tax, or tariff.

Income and Inheritance Taxes. These are what their names indicate, taxes on inheritances and incomes. 

The 16th Amendment (1913) permits Congress to impose an income tax among the States regardless of population. Congress has done this, and now the largest single source of national revenue is from the income tax, about $3,000,000,000 a year. In taxing an

income, all income is counted from whatever source salaries, rents, interest, royalties on books, plays, etc., and profits from business.

Licenses and Other Forms of Taxes. Money comes to the public from various kinds of licenses. There are marriage licenses, and automobile licenses, taxi licenses, licenses for ferry boats, auctioning, peddling and other 

KEYSTONE INDIVIDUAL INCOME TAX GEPORTS RECEIVED HERE PAYING THEIR INCOME TAX 

occupations. An insurance company pays a license for doing business within the State. There are licenses for recording deeds, surveying lands, administering wills. 

So the citizen is paying partly for the services he receives. Public service enterprises give a portion of their earnings to city or State, because the public gives them privileges which enable them to earn. 

Some revenue comes from poll taxes for the privilege of voting.

There are fines and forfeits, as penalties for mis-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

demeanors; and escheats, that is, when property comes to the State because no heirs can be found. 

Limits on the Taxing Power. Under our Constitution there are some things the taxing authority cannot do. 

Congress may not levy any tax on exports. The States 

OBTAINING AUTOMOBILE LICENSES 

The owner of a car is obliged to get an automobile license, the fee for which goes to the state. did not wish to be hampered or burdened in shipping out their products.

A State may not tax the lawful means by which the National Government chooses to carry on its work. 

If that were allowed then a State could prevent the United States from doing anything the State disliked. 

Likewise the National Government cannot tax the lawful means which the State chooses to carry on its affairs.

Therefore the national income tax does not apply to State salaries or to salaries paid in State institutions. 

If the nation could tax a State institution it could kill that institution, if it wished to do so. Neither State nor Nation may prevent the lawful living and working of the other. "The power to tax involves the power to destroy," the Supreme Court said.

Congress has imposed a tax of 10 per cent on all bank notes that may be issued by State Banks. This prevents their issue, as it would not pay. A State Bank is a lawful State institution and Congress may not prevent its doing business; but issuing bank notes to be used as money does not belong to a State or one of its agencies. 

What would be the effect of allowing all the States to issue money? 

Can you name some securities (bonds) that are exempt from taxation?

If people put their money into these securities and thus escape taxation, would the taxes on other forms of property be higher? Why?

If the Constitution should be so amended (as is possible) as to make these tax-exempt bonds subject to taxation, would the national and local government not have to pay a higher rate of interest on their bonds in order to sell them? If so, would there be any gain?

The Growing Expense of Government. There was a time when government was a very simple thing with few expenses. It is so no longer. In 1789 America had only 4,000,000 people. Now we have about 130,000,000 stretching across the continent and from the Great Lakes to the Gulf. The people are demanding more of their Government than formerly and the Government is appropriating money in a thousand ways at the

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

demand of the people — in agriculture, labor, industry, commerce, education. Apart from the vast and wasteful expenses for war the people are demanding more for peaceful purposes - for better roads, better schools,

WHERE THE STATE DOLLAR Went 

This is a simple picture of how the money paid in taxes in one year was spent. 

The circle represents one dollar. Less than ten cents of each dollar spent by the State went for "All other purposes" or the general functions of Government. finer school buildings, greater universities, better health provisions, better streets, better lighting, better policing, veterans' bonus, relief of the unemployed, and more commissions to safeguard the public interests. 

Aside from the expenses of war, the greater part of the tax money goes for local purposes for roads and schools and local improvements. For these benefits

the people are ready to pay if they are sure to get the worth of their money. If the money is not misused and wasted, and if the people actually receive what they pay for, the main ground of complaint about taxes will be removed. The people will then merely have to decide whether they want these public benefits and improvements enough to pay for them.

CHAPTER XI THE IMMIGRANT. 

THE COMMUNITY AND THE MELTING POT: 

A MIXTURE OF RACES AND NATIONALITIES 

The public school has done its best for us foreigners, and for the country, when it has made us into good Americans. 

I could not pronounce the name of George Washington without a pause. 

MARY ANTIN 

The original Americans were red Indians. The English settlers in Virginia and Massachusetts were the first real immigrants. 

In the following pages we shall see the difference between the old immigration and the new; we shall come to understand why 

America is called "the melting pot." In order to appreciate the benefits of immigration, we here learn of the reasons for its restriction, the naturalization process by which the stranger becomes a citizen, and the part played by the schools in Americanizing the foreigner.

America is a strange mixture of races and peoples. 

We had the red Indians at the very beginning, and we have about the same number of them now. 

The Spaniards settled in Florida, New Mexico, California, and the far west. 

Then came the English. They were the first real immigrants to land in America, when they settled in 

Virginia and Massachusetts. 151

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Very soon a few negroes were brought over from Africa to be slaves for the whites. There were four millions of them by 1863 when Lincoln set the black man free. There are fully twelve million negroes in 

America now.

The Blood of the Nation in 1776. Before the War of the Revolution there were in America (besides the 

English), Dutch, Danes, Swedes, Germans, French, Scotch, Welsh, Irish, and Scotch-Irish. These all helped to gain American Independence. The Sons and 

Daughters of the American Revolution came from many kinds of stock.

Early Immigration. Most of the people who were here when the Constitution was adopted were native Americans. Immigrants did not come in great numbers for nearly sixty years. In 1846 the potato rot in 

Ireland caused a food famine there and thousands of 

Irishmen came to America for the sake of a living.

In 1848 there were political troubles and an unsuccessful revolution in Germany and thousands of Germans left that land for America. Carl Schurz, who became a great American, was among them. By 1854, in a single year as many as 400,000 foreigners came to America, mostly Irish and Germans, though there were some Swedes and Danes and Norwegians. They all came to better their condition, to escape oppression and hard times at home and to find greater opportunity and greater freedom. Our hospitable doors were open and America was looked upon as "the asylum for the oppressed of all lands." There was much vacant land. There was coal to be dug, farms to be tilled, cities and railroads to be built. America needed la-

THE IMMIGRANT 

153 borers and there seemed to be land for all and work for all to do.

A New Kind of Immigration. Some time after the 

Civil War a new kind of immigration began and in greater volume. That which had come before was nearly all from northern and western Europe Germans, French, Scotch, English, Welsh, Scandinavians. 

EDWIN LEVICK NY, AMERICANS TO BE 

These immigrants have just passed the inspection at Ellis Island, New York, and are now waiting for trains to take them to their new homes. 

Those who cannot read or speak English have their names and destinations written on the tags they are wearing. 

These were a good deal like the people who were already here. But later immigrants began to pour in from southern and eastern Europe, Italians, Sicilians, Greeks, Russians, Jugo-Slavs, Slovaks, Austrians, Poles, Hungarians, Armenians, Croatians, Czechs, Bohemians, Lithuanians, Finns, and others. There were about forty nationalities mixed into our population. There was a regular babel of tongues, men

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

and women of all languages and religions. Taking all the newcomers, together with our negroes and some Mexicans, Portuguese, Japanese and Chinese, it seemed that America was becoming a mass of all the races on earth "a polyglot boarding house," as Theodore 

Roosevelt expressed it. 

What has the Melting Pot to do? Now how could any common life or national unity come out of such a population? For a while (1905 to 1914) immigrants were coming to America at the rate of a million a year. 

Could the "Melting Pot" of American citizenship melt all these people down into good Americans? Your mother may be able to put many different things into a pot or pan - some water, some bread crumbs, flour, raisins, "sugar and spice and everything nice," and after boiling and stirring and and cooking, out will come a pudding fit for a queen! Mother will know how to use the right ingredients and not too much or too little of any one kind.

But could good, intelligent citizenship result from all the kinds of people who were coming to live in our country? America began to think that the melting pot of our citizenship seemed to be getting too much and too many different kinds of mixtures. The pot was boiling but it could not boil fast enough to fuse all these different kinds of people into one. The question was: Can all these immigrants be merged in one people, speaking one language, having one allegiance to one government and one flag? If you think of taking ten different kinds of food into your body at once you must consider whether your stomach can digest them all, and assimilate or work them together into a mass of life-giv-

THE IMMIGRANT 

155 ing food that will make blood and bone and sinew and muscle. So the nation has to consider whether all these different kinds of immigrants can be made into intelligent, true, patriotic citizens. Let us notice how fast we have been receiving the many kinds of immigrants. 

UNDERWOOD & UNDERWOOD THE WORK OF THE MELTING POT 

A typical group of several nationalities in a Washington school. In the upper row from left to right are natives of Japan, Honduras, Cuba, Russia; in the lower row, Poland, China, Lithuania, Roumania. 

Great Increase in Immigration. In twenty years before the World War over 15,000,000 immigrants came to America. In 1914, the year the World War began, 1,218,418 immigrants came to America. The effect of the war was to cut this down, almost to nothing, to 110,000 in 1917. But as soon as the war was over the stream of immigrants began to rise again.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

It was seen that the pioneer days were over; our free land in the West was gone; the cities were crowded where the immigrants gathered and the city slums presented a hard problem; wages would be pressed down with so many unskilled laborers coming in. In all the large cities there were colonies of foreigners — 

Italians, Poles, Jews, Russians-speaking their own language, reading their own newspapers. In New 

York City, for instance, there were two million people who were not born in America. For all these reasons it was thought wise to restrict or check immigration. 

Restricting Immigration. Before 1882 almost nothing had been done to keep aliens from coming to the United States. In that year Congress passed a law to keep out paupers, criminals, the insane, and all laborers of the Mongolian race. In 1885 the Alien 

Contract Labor Law was passed by Congress which prevented corporations from bringing over laborers under contract. In 1910 an act put a tax of $4 on each immigrant and also forbade the admission of persons having epilepsy, tuberculosis, or contagious diseases, as well as polygamists, anarchists, and Mongolian laborers, and those who might become public charges. In 1917 another act increased the head tax to $8 and applied a literacy test. This required that all persons over 16 years of age (if not blind or dumb) should be able to read in some language. Although not a severe test yet many failed to pass it.

This was quite a check, but immigration was soon rising again and Congress in 1921 passed an act limiting the number of immigrants which any country could send to the United States to 3% of its people already

THE IMMIGRANT 

157 living here according to the census of 1910. Only about 350,000 aliens could come in under this act. 

In 1924 another act was passed still further cutting down the number. The new law allowed to each country only 2% of the number of its people living in the United States in 1890. The year 1890 was before

The immigration law of 1924 limits immigration from any country to 2% of those of that nationality in this country in 1890. This limits total immigration to about 151,000 a year. As the map shows, over two-thirds of these are allotted to Great Britain, Germany, Norway, and Sweden. the immigration from southern and eastern Europe amounted to much.

During the business depression of 1929-, immigration was greatly reduced. Only 97,139 entered the 

United States between July 1, 1930 and June 30, 1931.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

It should be noted also that more than 61,800 immigrants left America that year, and the actual increase, therefore, was only about 35,000.

Why fix the year 1890 instead of 1910 in restricting immigration? 

Testing Immigrants Abroad. Immigrants who are not acceptable should not be allowed to leave their own country for America. American consular officers in European cities now give visas or passports to those who are thought to be admissible. There are now immigration officers and public health officers ("technical advisers") to pass on such cases in the countries that send over 90 per cent of our immigrants. There are now 21 of these advisers at consulates abroad. 

Heretofore, immigrants had been landed at Ellis Island who could not be admitted and it caused them disappointment and suffering to be turned back. 

Benefits of Immigration. Honest and industrious laborers bring to a country one of the greatest benefits it can have. The millions of immigrants have been good laborers. They have worked in our steel mills, mines, shops and factories. They have done most of the heavy and dangerous work on our railroads, bridges, buildings, ditches, and sewers. The foreign-born laborers who were working in our country fifteen or twenty years ago were adding over a half billion dollars of wealth to the country every year. Many of our talented musicians and artists were born abroad, as also scientists and statesmen, men like Alexander 

Hamilton, Albert Gallatin, Robert Owen, Jacob A. 

Riis, Louis Agassiz, Oscar Straus, James J. Davis and others.

THE IMMIGRANT 

159 What are the chief influences that make the foreignborn into good Americans?

Are the native Americans sufficiently kind and considerate toward the foreign-born? If there are recent immigrants in your neighborhood how can you help them to like America and to become good Americans? 

What do you suppose our foreign-born workers think of our country and our ways? Why should we care what they think? Are they ever treated like beasts of burden?

A Cosmopolitan Nation. It may be a benefit that the American people are not local or provincial, but are made up of people from widely scattered nations of the world. New traits and qualities have come into the national life. Mixed races are often the strongest. 

The English are a mixture of Germanic stock, made up of Danes, Angles, Saxons, Jutes, Normans, with surviving traits of British and Celtic. So the American population is more vigorous than it would be if it were made up of a single strain. These foreign-born soon become American citizens of their own free will. They mingle with Americans, become accustomed to American ways. Their children go to the public schools, learn the English language and soon occupy positions of influence and importance. And when trouble comes and they are called to war they are ready to defend their adopted country as loyally and as bravely as the native born. The dead in the World War will show thousands of these immigrant names. And if you look over the field of American athletic sports you will find the same thing is true. 

Read the names of an "All American" Football team, or of the leading college teams — what will you find? Among them will be Siano, Macaluso, Martinez,

Americanizing the Newcomers. To lead the immigrants to understand America and to become good

161 American citizens is to Americanize them. It is not necessary to change all their customs and habits, or to make them conform to one standard of living. But it is well for them to learn the language of the country, which is English, and to know about the Constitution and principles of our Government. There are agencies like the Immigrants' Protective League and Church

Missions to help them to get work and to find an opportunity to make a living. The churches have missions. in the foreign sections of our cities, and the mission teachers and college settlements seek to train the children of the foreign-born and to prevent them from breaking away from the restraints of the religion of their parents.

The Public School and the Foreign-Born. The greatest help in Americanizing the immigrants comes from the public schools. These schools take the children of the foreign-born and teach them the English language, which is difficult for their fathers and mothers to learn. In the schools the children learn some American history and the principles of our American government and to honor the American flag. tion or two it is impossible to tell them from Americans whose ancestors have been in America from the first. 

The children in the schools and at play teach one another, and they teach their parents at home. So the melting pot does its work and will continue to do it if not filled too fast or with material that will not mix. 

Naturalization. Naturalization is the process of making an alien into a citizen. An alien in America is one born in another country; a citizen is one who owes allegiance to America. If a citizen, or subject of another country who has come to America, wishes to become an American citizen he may apply for citizenship after reaching the age of 18. He must show that he has a good character, that he is not an anarchist, that he can read and write and has some knowledge of American principles and institutions of government. 

He applies for citizenship by going before a court and declaring his intention" to become a citizen. He must do this at least two full years before full citizenship can be acquired. He must renounce all allegiance to any foreign country of which he has been a subject; give up all claim to any title of nobility, if he had any; take the oath of allegiance to the United States and swear loyalty to our constitution and our laws. The alien is then given his "first papers," and two years later, if he has resided in the United States for five years, he is given a certificate of naturalization, if he again applies. He then becomes a full citizen and has all the privileges of native-born citizens, except that he may not become President or Vice-President of the 

United States. (Why not?)

When we receive these newcomers into the American

THE IMMIGRANT 

165 community, into our national family, as it were, we should strive in every way to foster good will and a spirit of unity among all classes in America. All citizens, old and new, should understand the importance of what is done. We should receive only those whom we are willing to have as our fellow citizens, and only those should take upon themselves the responsibilities of the new citizenship who are willing to become good Americans and who will not fail in patriotism and loyalty to the land of their adoption.

CHAPTER XII TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION IN THE COMMUNITY 

Necessity is the mother of invention.

RICHARD FRANCK 

Let observation with extensive view, Survey mankind from China to Peru; 

Remark each anxious toil, each eager strife,

And watch the busy scenes of crowded life. 

-SAMUEL JOHNSON 

Civilization has advanced through the benefits of transportation and communication. Modern inventions have brought changes in the modes of travel; good roads have lessened distances and have improved mail service and cheapened postal rates. In this Chapter we not only follow the history and benefits of transportation, but we also see how the telegraph, the telephone, the wireless, and the radio have transformed the world. 

One midnight in a little village of 800 people a fire broke out. There was no fire company nor enough fire-fighting machinery at hand. The volunteer citizens did all they could to put out the fire, but in vain. The fire was spreading to other buildings and it seemed that the whole business section of the little village would be burned. What could they do? They sent a message for help to a near-by city, twelve miles away. 167

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

A STUDY IN AMERICAN TRANSPORTATION 

The airplane is by far the newest and swiftest. The ox-team has not yet given way to the automobile in some districts. The railroads, however, still furnish the bulk of our transportation.

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 169 

In a minute the telephone had carried the message. 

In fifteen minutes the city firemen were there, with their engine, trucks, hose, ladders, grappling hooks, and all. Within an hour the fire was out and other buildings were saved. 

What would your great-grandfathers have thought of sending twelve miles for men to come to put out a fire, over hills and streams and rugged roads? In those days the fire company could not have got across with its load. And if it could, it would have taken two hours at any rate, and in that time what would the fire have done? What saved the little village? The telephone, good roads, and the automobile - modern improvements in communication and transportation.

A Western Journey a Hundred Years Ago. A little over a hundred years ago a young teacher and his wife started from Philadelphia to Indiana. They went from Philadelphia to Pittsburgh by stage coach, a crazy, rattling, rickety, old machine, with its cramming and jamming, its lurches and plunges, swaying and jolting, until its passengers were bruised and sore. 

They went along at about three miles an hour, stopping at watering places, letting-out places, letting-in places, grog shops, and Post Offices. 

From Pittsburgh our western travelers went down the Ohio to Louisville in a slow steamboat. If they had gone just a few years before they would have traveled by a flat boat, carried by the current, as thousands of the westward "movers" had gone before.

The flat-boat men kept their boat from bumping the shore, or a raft of logs, by means of long poles. Some-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

times the poles broke and the current drove the boat on a sand bar where it stuck. There were many dangers on the river. 

From Louisville our travelers started overland in a two-horse cart, for a hundred miles in to Indiana. Here they had a lesson in forest traveling, in "spouty land," marsh-land," 

rooty land," and snaggy land,” with mud holes and no bridges, and the fords of streams almost without bottoms.

TRAVEL BY STAGE COACH 

They found some corduroy" roads made by the trunks of trees laid side by side, which for bumps and jolts always took the prize. It was spring-time and traveling by land became traveling by mud and water. Small brooks bubbled up where no brooks should have been; creeks were turned into rivers and rivers into lakes. There was no road to follow, but only a blazed trail, around stumps and over roots and snags. But by sunset of the second day, by hard driving, our travelers had gone only twenty miles from Louisville, about a mile and a half an hour. Such were the days before good roads! 

Compare those days with conditions and modes of travel now. 

Benefits of Good Roads. You can easily name many benefits that come from good roads. Travel is a great

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 171 

means of education. Good roads, highways or railways promote travel. Trucks can carry goods and busses can carry passengers over long distances. We have told of the hard journey of the young professor and his wife from Philadelphia to Indiana in 1823. Less than a hundred years later a successor of his went from Indiana to Philadelphia. This professor and his wife went in a Ford sedan and their household goods were carried in a motor truck over hard-surface roads all the way. Such were the changes of less than a hundred years made by good roads.

Farmers can get to town and market their products more easily. They can haul larger loads, saving time and money. They can get their daily newspaper and their mail, as the free-delivery carrier will go wherever the roads are good. The country children can be carried to a city or central high school. Medical aid or relief in times of danger can be obtained quickly when needed. The farmers by the use of the automobile can go to social gatherings and see more of one another. They can travel long distances to their capital cities, to conventions, to different parts of the State and to other States. The city people can get into the country or take excursions to distant cities. 

Thus good roads help to unify the country; they help the people of different communities to know one another, to keep them from being provincial, or ignorant of other parts of the country than their own. In fact, one of the best tests of the civilization of a community is to be found in its roads. 

Name some of the benefits of travel, of “seeing the world." Show that good roads go along with civilization.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Once two Americans were traveling in a donkey cart in China. They were anxious to reach a certain village by night. As night was coming on they stopped at a farmer's hut to ask how far it was beyond the hill to the village. "I do not know," said the Chinese farmer. "How should I know? I live here." The village was only three miles away, but that peasant farmer had lived all his life without ever having seen the village over the hill. Would such living account for the backwardness of a people? "FOX FILMS"

TRAVEL BY RIVER Early Modes of Travel. Modern modes of travel began not much over a hundred years ago. In Washington's time, if a man wanted to go a long distance, with light luggage, he went on horseback, with his saddle-bags. If he wished to travel in style, it was by the

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 173 

heavy stage coach. If he went by water, it would be by the sail boat or by use of the paddle and the oars. 

The Indian used the birch canoe and paddle. He could carry a deer across the lake in that way. 

People also traveled by the canal boat. A mule, or a team on the tow path slowly pulled the boat along. 

WESTWARD Ho! BY COVERED WAGONS 

They traveled also by carts and sledges and covered wagons, with ox-teams. Even as late as 1849, when the gold rush for California occurred, a caravan of travelers in prairie-schooners, or canvas-topped covered wagons, would start out from Missouri to go over the plains and mountains. The journey meant three or four months of hardship and suffering. Only the hardiest and strongest could survive. Thousands died from exposure and starvation and were buried on the way. The bones of cattle and horses were left to bleach

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

upon the prairies. Later the trail of these hardy travelers and adventurers became the track of the transcontinental railway, and now travelers go in comfort and luxury in three and a half days from Chicago to 

Los Angeles. 

Did you ever see a set of saddle-bags? or an ox-team? or a covered wagon like the old prairie-schooner? What discomforts accompanied such modes of travel?

Clermont first steamed up the 

Hudson from New 

York to Albany.

Ohio and over the 

Great Lakes to the 

West. In 1836 the 

Great Western crossed the Atlantic under her own steam. There were croakers who said it could never be done; they said no vessel could ever be big enough to carry the necessary fuel for the voyage.

Modern hard roads began with the work of McAdam, a Scotchman. He saw roads that were muddy or rough, tedious and dangerous to travel on, and costly to repair. He urged that roads should be built of broken stone, raised above the roadside and properly drained. He built a piece of such a road in 1815 and

Compare the old modes of travel with the "Sunset 

Limited," or the "lightning express" trains that carry passengers across the continent, or with the automobile sedan that carries a family or friends in comfort over cement roads 40 or 50 miles an hour; or with the luxurious 30,000 to 50,000 ton steamships, the great

"floating hotels," that plow the deep carrying 3000 passengers at one time across the 3000 miles of the Atlantic Ocean within less than five days; or with the more wonderful airplane which flies through the air at 

100 or 150 miles an hour. What wonders man has wrought!

What are macadamized roads and how did they get that name?

What are the most common types of roads in your vicinity? 

Which are the best for heavy traffic?

The Locomotive. For carrying things overland, the greatest achievement of man is the locomotive. It is a wonderful thing to make steam do the work in pulling a long train of loaded freight cars across the continent. 

For carrying big loads on long distances, the horse and the ox are now "back numbers." 

Before the days of steam and railroad building and the locomotive, our freight traffic followed the water routes on canals, rivers, and great lakes. Big wagons drawn by four-horse teams carried heavy goods over bad roads, but it was a slow, tedious, and expensive process. The railway and the locomotive brought a revolution in transportation.

Importance of Transportation. Carrying goods from place to place is more important than travel. We see the need of getting the products of our country distributed from place to place. The cotton of the Southern plantations must be taken to the factory or the seaboard. The wheat of the West must be shipped to the city mills or to the seaports for shipment abroad. The

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 177 

fruits of California and Florida must be carried to Eastern and Northern markets and homes. Coal and lumber are carried from mines and forests to all parts of the country. Potatoes, apples, berries, peaches, machinery, furniture, and many other things are transported from the sections of the country that produce them to other sections that want them. And from far distant parts of the earth goods are transported which are wanted in America and which America cannot produce, such as coffee, tea, silks, spices, mahogany, dates and figs, drugs and rugs, fine fabrics, and precious stones.

No community lives to itself; we all depend on others for things to eat or wear, for books to read, for materials with which to build our houses. Famines have been known to occur, not because there was not enough food in the world, but because it was not in the right place. There were no means of transporting it from provinces over the mountains not far away. 

So civilized man, as he lives today, especially in the city, depends for his life upon means of transportation. 

Changes in Transportation. It is amusing to think of the many ways by which men have transported their goods. You have heard of "Shank's Pony." That is the pony a man rides when he uses his legs to carry himself and his load. If his load were too heavy for him to get it on his back, he would drag it along the ground by main strength. Men and women carried heavy loads on their heads. The blacks in Africa and in other parts of the world still carry burdens in this way. Hundreds of black porters in Africa carry on their heads and shoulders the ivory, rubber, and other

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

freight from the interior of the continent to the coast for shipment to other parts of the world. The American Indians let the women carry the burdens; the women tore down the tepee and carried the tent to the next camping place while the men did the hunting, the fighting, or the loafing. At a more advanced stage of civilization man got various animals to carry his load - the 

ILONG LAKE RES POST OFFICE A RURAL POST OFFICE 

donkey, the camel, the elephant, the mule, the ox, the horse, the dog. Men used poles, carrying burdens between them, or drew them by skids and rough sleds. 

When some one thought of the wheel, there was a great advance. We are accus- tomed to think that the time never was when men did not have things rolling on wheels, but whole races of men have lived and died without ever having seen a wheel. With wheels there was no need of skids or long logs to be used for sliding. It would seem that anyone might have thought of the wheel if he had ever seen a log roll down a hill. The first wheel was probably a piece of a trunk of a tree, smoothed with some kind of ax. When two wheels, joined by an axle, were placed under the load, the going was much easier. 

Improving the Mail Service. Think of how good roads and modern inventions have improved the mail service. They have helped to bring cheap postage,

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 179 

one of the great blessings of our age. Postage stamps were introduced into America in 1847. In 1851 the letter rate was reduced to three cents for distances not over 3000 miles, and ten cents for greater distances. 

Charges varied according to weight, as now. In 1863, a uniform rate of 3 cents for all distances was provided. 

HARRIS & EWING THE CITY POST OFFICE, WASHINGTON, D. C. 

In 1883 the 2-cent rate was enacted, and this rate continued, except for a brief period in the World War, until 1933. Local postage remains at the 2-cent rate.

For 3 cents one can send a letter from any part of this country to another part, and by agreement of the International Postal Union, to many other civilized countries. of the world. For one cent a post card may be sent from New York to the Philippines, a distance of nearly 

9000 miles. 

Advantages of Cheap Postage. The cheaper postal

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

rates led to an immense increase in correspondence and to the spread of knowledge and information. A still cheaper rate was applied to magazines and newspapers, and there was a great increase in the spread of news, literature, and intelligence. All this led to the cultivation of common ideas and purposes. It helped to unify and nationalize the country. Letters and magazines can be carried across the continent in less than a week, and one part of the country can be kept in touch with another part.

Now the telegraph makes it possible for the Government to communicate instantly with all parts of the country and widely-separated districts can co-operate and act together. In times of war or disaster, troops and provisions can be moved quickly from one section of the country to another. 

Free Delivery and Parcel Post. The free delivery of mail began for large cities in America in 1863. Before this, everyone was expected to go to the Post Office for his mail. Letter carriers were rapidly increased, and now every city of any size has free mail delivery. Uncle Sam's postman, or letter-carrier, is the best known government official of the country. 

He brings his welcome message day by day, or two or three times a day, throughout the year. At Christmas time his job is that of a good Santa Claus who brings presents to the family from distant parts of the country. 

Rural free delivery was begun in 1896, and now Uncle Sam sends his drivers with the mail to all country homes and mail boxes where the roads are good enough. 

Influence of Newspapers. The newspapers gather the news and print it for the information of their

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 181 

readers. Their columns send out market reports and weather reports to the farmers and financial reports to business men. They tell about the proceedings in Congress and the State legislatures, seeking to keep their readers informed on all matters relating to national, EDWIN LEVICK, NY. 

PRINTING A NEW YORK DAILY NEWSPAPER 

The printing is done from curved metal plates fastened to revolving cylinders. Ink is spread on them as the heavy cylinders roll over the paper. state, and city affairs. When the newspapers do their duty in these respects, public opinion is enlightened and public officials can be held to a strict performance of their duty. If the people can know the truth about public affairs, if they can know what is going on, then, if things are wrong, they can soon set them right. 

Thomas Jefferson once said that he would rather have one good free newspaper in the land than a standing

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

army for the defense of the Republic. He thought that a people could not be ignorant and free at the same time and he wanted a newspaper to inform the people, to appeal to their intelligence and sense of the right, and to tell them the truth so they would vote intelligently and choose the right kind of people to govern.

Changes in Communication. The changes in communication have been even more wonderful than those in transportation. In 1844 came the electric telegraph. 

In 1867 the telegraph wires were laid under the sea and we had the Atlantic cable. A dispatch could then be sent around the world in a few minutes. Thousands of words come every day to the newspapers, enabling them to spread before their readers what happens all over the world.

In 1876 came the telephone, which has revolutionized the whole field of communication. There are now enough telephone wires to reach perhaps a thousand times around the world. These wires are found overhead and underground in all our cities and throughout all parts of the country. Nearly every home has its telephone. All kinds of messages are sent over the wires by the human voice — business is transacted, doctors summoned, “visits" are made and orders are given.

People call up distant cities and their conversation is as distinct as if they were speaking over the wire to the neighbors across the street. And now the telephone communication is carried across the Atlantic and New York and London have talked with one another in ordinary tones of the human voice. 

The Wireless and the Radio. The wireless seems

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 183 

more wonderful still. Without lines or wires stretching through space receivers catch messages out of the air. 

A person in Baltimore may send a message to a friend on an ocean steamer a thousand miles at sea. A concert in New York may be heard in New Orleans and by an amplifier a large audience may hear it. By the radio ten thousand homes throughout the country may 

KEYSTONE HELLO, LONDON, THIS IS NEW YORK! 

It took only one minute to make the telephone connection. be listening to the same concert. Millions of people from Maine to California may hear the President speaking to Congress or delivering a Memorial Day address at Mount Vernon. No passenger vessel goes to sea without its wireless apparatus, and any vessel can now give its signals of danger and distress (S. O. S. call) and bring other vessels to the rescue. 

Someone has said that "science has made a whisper-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

ing gallery of the skies." Instantly knowledge may be sent around the world, the speaker hardly raising his voice above a whisper. There are lanes of the air as there are lanes of the sea and highways on land. There are about 600 broadcasting stations in America, 18,500,000 radio sets in use, and 55,000,000 radio listeners. These numbers are increasing. The air has become valuable and men are seeking to control it, or own it, or become "masters of the air." One radio station is now stealing the wave length that belongs to another. 

So Uncle Sam will have to regulate the air traffic, like a traffic cop, to prevent piracy and collisions and quarrels over rights of way. More general use of the air will make regulations necessary, and a radio bill for regulation has been passed by Congress. 

On the Eve of Progress. Wonderful things have been done, but more wonderful things are coming. 

Television is coming, so that a man may sit at his telephone and see the person with whom he is talking far away. Flying over two miles in air, a plane by means of its rays will be able to blow up ammunition dumps miles away. It is claimed that giant dirigibles carrying heavy bombs will make regular trans-Atlantic trips in "wo days' time. A German passenger dirigible, the

Von Hindenburg, has already done this. War's weapons will be so destructive that wars must be prevented to save civilization. These new discoveries and inventions may be used to enrich the world and to save life rather than to destroy it.

Chemicals poured from planes are scattered over cotton fields by rapid revolution of the propeller blades and bring death to the boll weevil.

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 185 

The Postmaster-General reports that the air service will use but a single day in the transmission of mail from the Atlantic to the Pacific. So today's letter from New York will arrive in San Francisco tomorrow.

Herbert Hoover is here shown making a speech in Washington, D. C., while a New York audience, 200 miles away, both saw and heard him.

New York, while the loud speaker reproduced the speech. phone in an ordinary tone of voice may be heard. throughout a vast auditorium, and through the air to distant places. The human voice is magnified and is made to reach the most remote corners of the earth. 

It seems we are living in an age of miracles.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP Public Control of Public Utilities. The people live and do business by means of the telegraph, the telephone, the railway, the trolley line, the public bus, the street car, the subway. These are necessary to the life of the community. In their use and success we are all concerned. The whole nation has an interest in the way the telegraph and railways and air waves are managed, and the nation should have some control over them, just as a city should have some control over its street car lines and other public utilities, such as those engaged in furnishing water, gas, and electricity.¹ In such a line of business there is a natural monopoly, with no competition. If prices are too high at a business. man's store, or if his goods are shoddy or the service is bad, the customer may go to another store to buy what he wants. Competition serves to keep up the quality of the goods and the service. But with the telephone company or the telegraph company, what can a patron do if the charges are too high or the service is poor? It is not possible nor desirable to start other telephone and telegraph companies.

Public Service Corporations. The companies that furnish these benefits to the public are called public service corporations. They are private companies rendering public services. If they give good service they are entitled to fair pay and good treatment. 

Sometimes these companies water the stock and claim more pay than they are entitled to. For instance, a street railway company may have actually invested in its system, say $5,000,000, but it issues stock to the amount of $10,000,000. So $5,000,000 of the stock is 

1 See the "City Community," pp. 59, 60.

TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION 187 

water — no substance to it, no real value in it, just certificates of stock. Then the company may claim that its plant is actually worth $10,000,000 while it has cost only five millions, and it claims the right to fix wages, rates, and fares in such a way as to earn a "reasonable income" of five or six per cent on ten millions. The same thing is sometimes done by Gas and 

Electric Light Companies. Does this seem fair?

To prevent these abuses and to protect the people's interest, public service commissions are created by the State. These are to investigate, oversee, and control charges and service, seeking to bring about fair wages for the men employed, fair return for the capital invested, and good service for the public. The trouble is, these public service commissions often fall under the control of the public utilities corporations with the result that the commissions look out more for the interests of the corporations than they do for the interests of the people whom they are supposed to represent. 

Whether the Commissions do any good or not depends entirely on the kind of men who are appointed to them. 

The appointments are usually made by the Governor to a State Commission, by the President to a National Commission.

The Inter-State Commerce Commission was created by Congress in 1887, to oversee and regulate the railroads, canals, steamship lines, express companies, telegraph and telephone companies, so far as these companies carry on commerce between the States. All changes of rates and financial operations of these companies must have the approval of the Commission, which is supposed to look out for the interest of pas-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

sengers, shippers, holders of the companies' bonds, and of the general public.

The country is always facing the great problem of production, transportation and distribution of the necessaries of life. The farmer, the railroad owner, the railroad laborer, the worker in the cities, the merchant, and the "ultimate consumer" are all interested in its fair solution.
