CHAPTER XIII  

A burden well borne becomes light. - OVID No community is free from burdens. We see them in orphan children and aged parents, in defectives, blind, deaf, dumb, feeble-minded and insane. We learn here how the vocational schools help the crippled and disabled so they can become selfsupporting; how the burdens from accident and disaster and unemployment are to be borne, and how the awful burden of crime and its costs may be reduced.

There are people in every community who cannot carry their own weight. That is, they cannot support themselves, but have to be helped along by others. 

They may be old and feeble; they may be young and helpless; they may be sick or unfortunate; they may be crippled and maimed; they may be good-fornothing loafers and beggars; they may be defective in mind and body, like the idiot, the feeble-minded, and the insane; or the blind, the deaf, and the dumb; or they may be criminals who are preying upon the community and have to be restrained. Business depressions, like the one that began in 1929, can by loss of job or by wage cuts, reduce thousands of otherwise independent families to dire need. 189

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 1. Orphan Children and Aged Parents. The parents of the family generally take care of the children, teach and guide them and bring them up, so they will be able to support families of their own. 

And when the parents are old and feeble and cannot work any more, if they are left without means, the 

PLEASANTLY EMPLOYED, IN SPITE OF OLD AGE 

So long as they are able to make gay toys for little children, these old gentlemen can support themselves. They find the work very pleasant, too. children love them, provide a home for them, and care for them. As long as the family is able to provide, little children and aged parents will not become a burden on the community. But if the parents die while the children are too young to support themselves; or if parents, or others, grow old and helpless without children to care for them, then the community must make some provision.

How does the community care for the helpless? Is there an Orphans' Home in your community? Do you know of any Old Ladies' Home? Or a Home for Old 

Men? Sometimes a Board of Charity, whose officers are looking out for such cases, will find a good family where there are no children and induce the family to adopt a child from an Orphans' Home. Is this a better way to help an orphan child than to leave him in the "Home" with other children? Should an adopted child feel that he was especially wanted by his foster parents? 

KEYSTONE LEARNING A USEFUL TRADE 

These students in a school for the blind are taught to make very beautiful baskets which are sold at a good price. They find the work fascinating and they become very skillful. 

2. The Defectives. There are those in the community who may be handicapped in some way, not having the same chance as others in making a living. 

They are bodily or mentally defective. If the trouble

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

is in the body, they may be lame or blind, deaf or dumb, or suffering from some long standing disease like epilepsy or tuberculosis. A civilized community never feels like blaming these unfortunates, but is always ready to support them, if need be; or, to teach them to help themselves as much as possible. It is always the better way, to help the weak to grow strong and become self-supporting and independent. These classes deserve our sympathy and aid. The State is under obligation, in kindness and mercy, to provide for their comfort and education. 

Education of the Blind and of the Deaf and Dumb. 

Only a little over a century ago nothing was done for the education of these unfortunate classes. It was supposed that nothing could be done to improve their condition. If a blind person could not be cared for in his own family he was left on the street or roadside to beggary and want. He was left to the charity and mercy of the passers-by. Now in every civilized land and in all our States there are schools for the blind. Since the blind are deprived of the sense of sight, their hearing becomes very keen and their touch very sensitive and their fingers nimble. 

READING BRAILLE 

The system of raised dots by which the blind may read was invented by Louis 

Braille. With the fingertips of the right hand they feel the words, while the left hand keeps the place at the beginning of the line.

They quickly learn to guide themselves about the streets of crowded cities. They take up certain handicrafts sewing, knitting, crocheting, carving, wood-work, and the making of fancy articles. There is a system (Braille) for teaching them to read and write by raised points, or dots on the page. Many books are printed in this form. Many blind boys and girls go through high school and some, by employing readers and helpers, make their way through college. They overcome their handicap and become self-supporting and helpful members of the community, thereby teaching all of us a lesson in persevering and overcoming our difficulties. 

Like provision is made for the deaf and dumb.

Schools are established for them and institutions where they can be trained for some self-supporting trade. Do of blindness that has come know of you any case from accident? Was the accident preventable?

One authority says that not half of the deaf are so from birth but two-thirds of the cases are the result of disease scarlet fever, measles, diseased adenoids, catarrh in the head. How can deafness be prevented if a child contracts one of these diseases? 

Where is the Institute for the Blind in your State?

For the Deaf and Dumb? Learn how many there are in these institutions and what is being done for them. 

Mental Defectives. There are two kinds of mental defectives, the feeble-minded and the insane.

A feeble-minded person is one whose mind is undeveloped or is not capable of much growth. The body may grow up while the mind remains weak and childish. 

The idiot is one who has no mind at all; so there is no

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

hope for any change or growth. An imbecile is weakminded, only little better than an idiot. A moron is a stupid person whose mental growth has stopped at about ten or twelve years of age. It is a case of “arrested development." The moron seems unable to use his mind or to learn anything after a certain age. All these classes of the feeble-minded have to be cared for. 

Some are kept in county houses, without any special attention or teaching. But now there are State Schools for the feeble-minded, where they are given special treatment. The public schools provide special classes and grade rooms for them where special and skillful teachers devote themselves to a roomful of the slow pupils. The teaching is adapted to their needs with remarkable results.

The Insane. An insane person is one who has lost his reason, one who suffers from a mental illness. His insanity may come from a disordered nervous system, from some great shock or sorrow, or from being overburdened or distressed. The brain is diseased, which may happen to the most brilliant of men or women. 

Some of the insane become delirious and violent and therefore dangerous. They have no idea what they are doing and are not responsible for their conduct. No one would think of punishing an insane person for what he does. But the dangerously insane must be kept in close confinement, sometimes in a padded cell, to keep them from injuring themselves or others. 

By a change in living conditions, by rest, by gentle and soothing treatment, the insane may sometimes be cured.

In early times it was thought the insane were "possessed of devils," and they were chained in dismal dungeons

and cruelly treated. Our age is more enlightened and humane. Now these sick ones are cared for in State hospitals, under doctors and nurses who are specialists in the diseases of the mind. Some of the patients are but slightly ill, and are easily controlled. Some are trusted with work to do, and some are allowed to work out of doors upon the hospital farm, and thus by living a normal, healthy, untroubled life they recover their mental health and balance. 

These are

Vocational Schools and Rehabilitation. 

big words that need explanation. A vocation is a calling in life or some occupation or trade by which one makes a living. A vocational school is one which teaches not merely what may be found in books, but some calling or practical trade by which the student may afterwards earn his livelihood, such as carpentry, cabinet-making, work with electric and plumbing fixtures, and machine work of various kinds. 

Rehabilitation means many accidents in life. making men over. There are 

Men and women are disabled and have to be changed from the work and conditions to which they have been accustomed. Crippled children have to have special training if they are going to be fitted for making a living and not be a burden to their families or the communities. So the maimed and crippled, like the blind and the deaf, have need of vocational schools.

A young boy in Los Angeles just out of the grammar school was suffering from the results of infantile paralysis. He had to wear braces on both legs. Wherever he turned he could find no work to do. Finally he was referred to the State Board of Education which has a

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Division for making men over (Rehabilitation). He was advised that he would probably make a good telegraph operator. He was placed in a technical school to learn that trade. Today, although he still wears his braces, he is a telegrapher telegrapher employed by the Postal Union and is happily earning his own livelihood. 

An older man, nearly 50, was a longshoreman for years. An accident resulted in the loss of his right leg above the knee. He was almost in despair, as he could not work at his old job. He saw only dependency for himself and children. Shoe-repairing was suggested as a suitable trade. He was put in a shop to learn the trade. Today he has a shop of his own and is prospering. His children are not "eating the bread of charity."1

So by linking education and industry those who are maimed and might be a burden to the community are turned into producers and self-supporting helpers. If properly trained the cripple can do many kinds of work as well as regular workers. The handicap from injuries may always be partially overcome. 

Disabled Soldiers. This service (vocational rehabilitation) has been extended to disabled soldiers. During the World War 276,000 casualties occurred the wounded and diseased. Many of these "ex-service men" of the World War have been disabled by their wounds. One may have lost a leg, or an arm, or an eye. Others have been shell-shocked or gassed or permanently deafened by explosions. Some have contracted tuberculosis. All these have a claim that special provision be made for them, to restore them, so far as 1 These stories of human experiences are taken from an article by 

H. D. Hicker, in the Survey for January 15, 1927.

possible, to their former selves. The Government has made provision for these men by vocational schools to help them again to earn their living. 

Automobile Accidents. Every year there are 

3,000,000 accidents reported in industry, and many are not reported. In the United States during 1935, UNDERWOOD & UNDERWOOD IN THE TORNADO'S PATH 

Many St. Louis families were driven from their storm-torn homes to seek shelter until the waste could be cleared away and the work of rebuilding undertaken. Help came from local, state, and national agencies. automobiles caused 36,000 deaths and 450,000 accidents and disabilities, twice as many as the World War cost America. All these are losses and burdens to the community. The community is interesting in preventing them and in repairing the losses.

The Burden of Disasters. There are special burdens from time to time that the community has to bear. 

The scourge of war sometimes comes. It always

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

causes suffering, woe and loss of life and health and property. Its burden of taxation rests heavily on the nation long after the war is over. The crippled soldiers, the widows and orphans of the slain have to be cared for, and the pension burden goes on through two generations. More than four-fifths of our national taxes are caused by the expenses of past wars and preparations for future wars.

Besides the losses and horrors of war, we have fires and floods, hurricanes and earthquakes. Most of the fires and floods could be prevented, but no one can foresee nor prevent the hurricane or the earthquake. 

The suffering from these disasters cannot be allowed to fall only upon their victims. All our communities the whole nation, must help bear the burden. So the 

Red Cross sends out appeals for money and sends nurses and food and help to the suffering in any stricken region. 

Do you know how rapidly Chicago was restored after the great fire of 1871, or San Francisco after the earthquake and fire of 1906? What do you know about the destruction caused by the tornado in St. Louis in 1927 or the Mississippi flood in 1927? Can men do anything to prevent earthquakes? What precautions are taken in earthquake regions to avoid danger and damage from earthquakes? What precautions had been taken along the Mississippi River to avoid destruction by flood?

How is the work of the Red Cross carried on in your community? 

Can you give examples to show that the American people are usually generous in their response to appeals for help in times of special disaster?

The Burden of Unemployment. Hard times come in industry. The factories close down, and men and

women are thrown out of employment. Thousands who are willing to work are out of a job. The community must help them. Those who still have work must pay out of their wages to help the others. Or there may be a labor war and a strike occurs. Then the owners lose trade, the workmen lose wages, and the savings of the workmen have to be drawn upon to pay the expenses of the strike and to support the workers. 

This condition of idleness is a condition of wastethe idle are consuming what the workers are producing. 

The problem is to reduce this unemployment as much as possible.

The Social Security Act and Unemployment Insur ance.

In recent years our Government has recognized that unemployment, poverty, and insecurity in old age, to which many people are subject, hinder happiness and social justice. The Social Security Act of 

1935 provides for Federal contributions to the States for needy mothers, for crippled children, and the blind, and it offers aid to the States for an unemployment insurance system.

The Act is based on the idea that when a citizen has become too old to work or has reached old age without means of support, it is then the duty of the Government to see that he has some continuing income. 

Workers and employers will be taxed, and in return the citizen who has reached the age of 65 and who is unable to work, will receive a small pension, the amount of which may vary from ten dollars to eighty-five dollars a month. Federal contributions equal to the 

State contributions are authorized, but in no case is the Federal Government's share to exceed fifteen

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

dollars a month. It is hoped that this will remove from the shoulders of the young people the burden of caring for old people and prevent the great hardships which come from unemployment and old age.

The Burden of Crime. A crime is a violation of the law. If one does what the law forbids or fails to do what the law requires, he is a criminal. There are many kinds of crime. A felony is a serious crime, such as theft, embezzlement, robbery, burglary, forgery, arson. These are usually penitentiary offenses. A capital crime is one punishable by death, such as murder and treason.

Reducing Crime. What can we do to put a stop to crime in our country? Is there much hope that crime can be reduced and the record improved? Several plans have been proposed: 

1. Try to reform the young offenders. There is hope for a boy of 20 who has gone astray and has committed his first offense. For a bad man of 40, confirmed in crime, there is less hope. These two classes ought not to be kept together in our prisons. They should be separated and the young offenders should be given physical training and be taught some useful trade. 

They can be shown a better way than the paths of crime. 

2. Try the suspended sentence. Sometimes men who are convicted of small offenses, especially if they are first offenders, are not sent to jail, or to the penitentiary at all, which would be a disgrace, but are released on a "suspended sentence." That is, they are allowed to go to their homes and keep on working and providing for their families. As long as they behave well the

sentence is not enforced. Trusting men and putting them on their good behavior often works well. 

3. Try releasing men on parole. This can be done under the "indeterminate sentence," which means that one who commits a crime should not be "sent up" for a certain term of years but the time should be determined by his conduct and state of mind. We send a patient to a hospital until he gets well, not for a fixed term of years. Can a criminal be treated in this way? 

Let him be kept only until he shows good signs of being morally well. Then he can be put on parole" and if he goes straight he

PRESS ILLUSTRATING SERVICE Rows OF PRISON CELLS 

is free; if he breaks the law again he is brought back. 

Under this system one who is convicted is not sent to prison for a fixed term; it may be "from two to fourteen years." He must be kept for at least two years, but cannot be kept longer than fourteen.

Whether he is allowed to go at the end of two years, or three, or ten, depends upon his good behavior and how he proposes to live. Maybe a job will be found. for him, and he becomes again a useful member of society. 

4. Try prison reform. A prison like a hospital or a

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

school should be placed under the management of an educated man, one who knows the ways and minds of men, and how they should be treated to call out the best that is in them. There is no need of treating prisoners cruelly, of crushing the heart out of them or destroying their personality. It is shocking to our humanity to read of the "cruel and unusual punishments," now forbidden by our Constitution, which in former times were inflicted upon criminals, sometimes for very slight offenses. A little over 100 years ago in 

England there were 200 crimes punishable by death, some of them very trivial. If a poor man shot a rabbit on a nobleman's hunting preserve, or stole a piece of clothing off a wash-line, he was sent to the gallows. 

The Cost of Crime. The cost of crime is so great it can hardly be estimated. It has been placed at over a billion dollars a year. Police expenses are greatly increased. Judges, courts, jails and prisons, watchmen, locks, vaults, bolts and bars and hundreds of officers have to be provided for on account of crime. 

Running down criminals, catching and trying them, cause a vast expense. The cost is more than a matter of dollars and cents. Not only is property destroyed but lives are destroyed, and people live in uneasiness and fear because of the robber, the burglar, or the footpad. People do not feel safe on the streets at night.

The danger in the home or on the street interferes with orderly and efficient ways of living. No problem is more serious for society than that of preventing crime. 

This is the work not only of the state but of the home, the church, and the school, that children may learn self-government, good behavior, and how to "play

the game of life" according to the rules of human society.

CHAPTER XIV THE PROTECTION OF THE COMMUNITY 

The first duty of a government is to protect life and property. 

This is a paramount obligation. For this governments are instituted, and governments neglecting or failing to perform it become worse than useless.

WILLIAM M. EVARTS 

This Chapter shows us how the community is protected against crime and disease, fire and flood, accident and disaster. The more we know of the duties and powers of the Police and the 

Fire Departments, the better able are we to appreciate our security under their protection. The following pages give us an understanding of the constant struggle of the community to protect itself.

The community needs to protect itself against crime and disease, against fire and flood, against accident and disaster. If every one were peaceful and law abiding, careful and thoughtful of others, there would be much less to do and our communities would all be happier. Crime would be gone and there would certainly be fewer fires. Still, in spite of carefulness, there might be explosions and fires, floods might come, and accidents and disasters occur. The community must be ready for self-preservation and protection.

Police Protection. In every city there is a police force, a body of men employed to enforce the law and 204

THE PROTECTION OF THE COMMUNITY 205 

to protect the community against wrong-doing. When on duty the police wear uniforms. Their job is suppressing crime and trying to make the streets and homes and business houses of the city safer for life and property. The country districts are not so well provided with police, though some states have provided for mounted officers who seek to do for the countrypeace side what the city police do for the cities. 

Police Corruption. No doubt there are some bad policemen, who "wink" at crime, or ignore it and refuse to enforce the law. Instead of preventing crime they are willing to encourage it for pay. Men and women who wish to run gambling places or low dance halls, or bootleggers who want to ply their trade in bad liquors, may pay the police to let them alone. Such policemen are not true to their trust, but are traitors to their fellow policemen and to the community. 

A Good Police Force. However, the great body of policemen are true men who try to do their duty and protect the law-abiding citizens who employ them. 

They are community benefactors in many ways. Their work is not merely confined to arresting criminals, fighting rioters, or clubbing people in a disorderly crowd. They see that city ordinances are carried out; they prevent loiterers, and crowds, peddlers, fakers, and speech-makers from blocking the streets and sidewalks. They help to enforce the city health rules.

They are present at fires to help guard against accidents. They see that cars are properly parked and are not parked at forbidden places. They slow down the automobile traffic and run after the speeding car on their motorcycles. They try to keep the sidewalks in

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

good condition, and the traffic policeman stands at the crowded street corner to direct the passing of automobiles and pedestrians in such a way as to avoid delay, confusion, and danger. The school children going to school or coming home find in the policeman a friend and helper at busy street crossings. 

So the good policeman is the friend of every law- 

BROWN BROS THE PROTECTOR OF THE COMMUNITY 

Timid school children lose their fear of busy corners when the police officer clears a path for them through the heavy traffic. abiding citizen. He tries to break up the criminal "joint" or hidden gambling room; he patrols the street, warding off danger; he sometimes risks his life to save people from burning buildings or speeding automobiles, and he is always ready to give information to strangers in the city.

Police Women. There are police women, too, to

THE PROTECTION OF THE COMMUNITY 

207 look after cases in which women are especially concerned. These police matrons are helpful in preventing wayward girls from going wrong or bringing them back to useful lives if they have fallen into bad ways. They protect women and girls who may be alone in public places, in parks, railway stations, or on the streets. 

Police Courts and Detectives. In the cities police courts and jails are often over-crowded. Vagrants and tramps have been known to commit petty crimes in order to get into a comfortable jail on a cold night. 

The policy now is to discourage numerous arrests, and to help those in need of food and lodging. The police are instructed to warn first offenders, to disperse disorderly crowds without arrest; to save men from the disgrace of arrest and from loss of time in cases where the milder and kinder policy will have the better effect. 

So the intelligent policeman tries not only to punish but to prevent crime and to keep people out of trouble. 

Some of the police are detectives, whose business it is to run down crimes and criminals. They may be "plain clothes men," who dress like other citizens with the police badge concealed, to be revealed when the time comes for an arrest. To resist an officer of the law is a special offense and the officer has a right to compel obedience. When the officer by his badge shows that he represents the majesty of the law, it is the duty of the citizen to submit to his authority. If a policeman makes a mistake and arrests an innocent citizen, he may be sued for false arrest and punished for misconduct. To "impersonate a policeman" by the use of a false or stolen badge subjects the offender to serious punishment. The "plain clothes" detectives

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

mingle with the criminals themselves and learn the ways and plans of lawbreakers, as the uniformed police could not do.

Police Powers. The police powers of the state are those used not only to protect the community from crime and disorder, but to do good things for the community, any lawful thing that may be for the public welfare. Let us name some of the "police powers" that have been used for public protection: 

Cleaning up tenement houses. 

Prohibiting the liquor traffic.

Inspecting mines and factories to make sure they are safe and healthful places in which to work. 

Keeping women and little children from laboring too early, too long, and too hard. 

Preventing profiteering in rents.

Regulating the location of powder mills and slaughter houses.

Requiring elevated tracks for railway trains. 

Inspecting hotel elevators. 

Fixing the speed limit for automobiles. 

Regulating the height of buildings.

Requiring rest rooms and rest hours for women and girls who toil. pro- Laws requiring all these things and many others are passed in the exercise of "police powers" for the tection of life, health and property in the community. 

Fire Protection. Do you have any idea how much valuable property is destroyed by fire in America every year? It has been estimated at nearly half a billion dollars. It may be more. This means nearly a thousand dollars going up in smoke every minute! If it were 900 dollars a minute, count up what that would

THE PROTECTION OF THE COMMUNITY 209 

come to in a year. This causes a great expense for insurance. What is worse, lives are lost in fighting fire and in burning buildings-over fifteen thousand lives every year, with as many injured. What a loss that is! What can be done to prevent such losses?

Most of the fires are caused by sheer carelessness, by thoughtless disregard of rules and regulations. 

COMMON SENSE COULD HAVE PREVENTED THIS ACCIDENT 

It is never safe to start a fire with kerosene. The flames leap up at the touch of the match, and blindness, disfigurement, or death may result. 

What are some of the careless ways in which fires are caused? 

Using kerosene to start a fire, where there may be smoldering coals. 

Throwing cigar stubs about the careless smokers seldom look to see where the stubs go. 

Throwing a lighted match among the shavings.

Over-heating the furnace or having a defective flue. 

Little children playing with matches. 

Letting soot accumulate in the chimney. 

Careless lighting of the Christmas tree.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Leaving the current on in an electric iron. 

Going away from a bonfire before it goes out wind comes up and spreads the sparks. 

the

Leaving oily rags where the heat may cause combustion. 

Emptying hot ashes in wooden boxes. 

Leaving shavings, excelsior, pasteboard, and pine boxes or other inflammable material in the cellar - if a fire starts there it will go fast and be hard to stop. 

Cleaning gloves and clothes with benzine or gasoline near an open flame. 

Using popping wood in an open fireplace without a screen. 

Having faulty electric wiring. 

Letting sparks fall on a shingle roof. 

Do you think of other ways by which fires are caused? How could most of the fires be prevented?

All these dangers make it necessary for cities to have fire companies made up of expert and professional firefighters, equipped with fire engines and all necessary machinery for preventing and fighting fires. 

Fires and Police Powers. What are some of the "police powers" that are used for protection against fires?

Preventing the erection of frame buildings, which may easily catch fire, in the crowded parts of a city. 

Keeping inflammable materials away from danger. 

Requiring fire drills in schools.

Requiring asbestos curtains and numerous exits in theaters.

Requiring fire escapes in hotels, in office buildings and other high buildings.

Providing fire alarm boxes and giving the fire company the right of way on streets.

Do you know of other provisions against fire? Do you have fire drills in your school building? How long does it take to get everybody out? How can the pupils best co-operate in a fire drill? If you were to detect a fire in your school building which should you do first, send in the alarm to the Fire Department, or give the signal for a fire drill? Do you know how to do both? 

Fire Insurance. If a man has his house insured against fire, does that mean it will not burn down? No, it only means that if it does burn down the insurance company will pay him some money, perhaps enough to rebuild the house. Insurance does not prevent fires, except as the insurance companies busy themselves in securing good fire regulations and enforcing them. It is to their interest to prevent fires. When a house burns, or a stock of goods, its money value is gone. 

The money paid by the insurance company to a man whose house burns really comes from the owner of the house, and other owners, who for some time have been paying money called "premiums" to the company. 

Paying for insurance is like paying a tax. It has to be

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

done or one runs the risk of losing the value of his buildings. Insurance rates go up as danger from fire increases. Shingle roofs, frame buildings crowded together, inflammable materials, increase the risks from fire and the cost of insurance. Insurance consists of many house-holders co-operating through the company in raising a fund to pay those who lose. 

Why do people set fire to houses? Did you ever hear of a man who set fire to his store in order to collect the insurance money? Has one a right to set fire to his own house?

Does a fire loss usually fall on the people who caused the fire? How do fires add to the cost of living for everybody? 

If a grocer or a merchant has to insure against fire, will he not have to charge more for his goods? 

Co-operation of State and Local Communities. State authorities seek to co-operate with local authorities in preventing fires. There are state fire marshals with assistants who investigate fires and prosecute those who may be guilty of causing them. They make known to the people the fire regulations, and send out information as to how fires may be avoided. 

Have you a fire marshal in your state? What are his duties? Some states have once a year a “Fire Prevention Day," set apart by a proclamation of the governor. 

Does your school celebrate such a day? If so, how?

Can you think of a reason for having such a day on 

October 9? 

How far from your home is a fire-call box? What small fire extinguishers may be kept about a house that would be helpful in putting out a fire before it gets well under way?

THE PROTECTION OF THE COMMUNITY 213 Fire Companies and Fire Fighting. Cities now have fire companies well organized and well trained for fighting fires. There is a fire chief, who has his men stationed at the various engine houses located at suitable places throughout the city. There are modern equipments for fire fighting-engines, hose, trucks, EDWIN LEVICK, N. Y. 

FIRE FIGHTING 

grappling hooks, ladders, and chemical apparatus.

Some chemicals will destroy the oxygen in the air and thus put out the fire. Sometimes it is necessary to tear down or blow up buildings in order to prevent a fire from spreading. In smoke helmets to prevent suffocation, the brave firemen go into burning buildings to rescue people who have been entrapped. For successful fire fighting the city should have a plentiful supply

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

of water, with strong pressure and strong hose so several streams of water may be played on the fire at once with a force that will reach the highest windows. The firemen have water towers on wheels from which with their water hose they may reach the roofs or topmost windows of the tallest buildings.

Forest Fires. Some of the most wasteful and terrible fires in America are in the forests. Great trees in the timber country, or on the mountain sides are annually destroyed by fires. This is a woeful loss when the country needs so much to preserve its lumber supply. These raging forest fires burn up the standing timber, and the wind sometimes carries the fire to the villages and towns and wipes them out of existence. 

These fires are often started by sparks from a passing locomotive, or by the carelessness of campers and hunters. This has led to strict regulations on camping in certain forest regions and fire officers post strict directions which campers are required to follow. 

Thousands of square miles of forest lands in the West are now patrolled by airplanes to report fires. 

Protection From Floods. Floods may come because the forests are destroyed. Where trees and vegetation are growing, the rainfall is more readily absorbed and the fertility of the soil is preserved. If the uplands are bare, the water runs into the valleys below. The soil is carried off and the lower lands are flooded, with a great damage to property and danger to life. Flood-prevention becomes a problem. The State and National governments seek to protect the homes and farms of the people by erecting dikes and levees and raising the banks of the streams.

Protection Against Many Evils. The citizen needs. 

protection against all sorts of evils and pests. Protection against the usual crimes is not enough. Murder, robbery, thievery, burglary, forgery, bootlegging all these should be prevented. But we need protection also against riots and mobs, nuisances and various disorders. There are swindlers and crooks who go about selling people worthless stocks and bonds. "Blue Sky laws" are to protect people from being swindled by the false pretenses of these agents by making them show whether there is real value behind their claims. Using the mails to defraud, and getting money under false pretenses, or by forged checks, are penitentiary crimes.

Apart from these evils, think of all the pests the farmers have to fight continually. The smut and the weevil destroy his grain; the cholera carries off his hogs; worms and insects eat up his fruit; the hoofand-mouth disease kills the cattle; the "borer" is getting after his corn; prairie dogs and rats, field mice, ground squirrels, and gophers damage his crops; and flood or drouth may sweep away all the fruits of a season's toil. A war is always going on against these

destructive evils and all the powers of the State and National Governments have to be called on to help. 

Millions of dollars are spent on irrigation to prevent drouths. Millions more are used in drainage and dikes to prevent floods. The Department of Agriculture and many State Colleges are sending out information on diseases of animals and plants and how they can be prevented. Expert scientists and teachers are constantly working in their laboratories, studying plant and animal life, trying to find out how this war for protection can best be conducted against these enemies of human welfare. It is not so much against an invading army of men with guns and bayonets in their hands that we need protection, but rather against these foes that would destroy our means of living. Men have to fight the insects all the time and it sometimes looks as if the insects would win. This is the reason we need science and education to learn how to protect society in the struggle for existence.

CHAPTER XV  IN THE COMMUNITY 

Health is a blessing that money cannot buy. -IZAAK WALTON We all realize the need of a sound mind and a sound body for our own comfort and convenience, and we ought to know how healthy bodies may be preserved by means of exercise, pure air and water, good food, and suitable recreation. Besides touching on these advantages to the individual and to the community, this Chapter shows how disease and epidemics are resisted and prevented, and how the community is benefited by playgrounds and parks.

The ancient Greeks had a maxim, "a sound mind in a sound body." They were the most highly civilized people in the world. They were intellectual, refined, artistic. They sought to cultivate their minds but they also sought to cultivate their bodies, to make their bodies strong and graceful. They had Olympic games, or national sports, to which young men came from all over Greece to compete. 

The Greek boys jumped and wrestled and pitched quoits and played many games in order to develop strong athletic bodies. They believed the mind could work better in a healthy body, and of course they were right about that. The English, too, another great 220

people, have always been fond of outdoor sports, cricket, hockey, rowing, riding, football and other vigorous games. Some one said that England won the great battle of Waterloo on the football fields of Rugby and Eaton, two famous English schools. The boys while at their games were getting ready for the hard tests that were to come. 

Keeping Our Bodies Well. How can we keep our bodies well and strong?

Once a man in Indiana lived to be over a hundred years old and he said he had "never been sick a day in his life." That was remarkable. When he was asked how he had done it he gave some simple directions for preserving the health: "Breathe good air, eat good food, drink pure water, take vigorous daily exercise, keep clean, and get plenty of refreshing sleep." We may be pretty sure that if we follow these directions we shall not have to take much medicine. It is better to prevent disease than to have to cure it. 

1. Good Exercise. We should do as the Greeks did, get a good deal of outdoor work and outdoor play. If our muscles are not exercised, they will grow weak and soft and flabby. It will then be easier for us to catch disease and we shall not have the strength to resist it or throw it off. Almost all the schools now provide for classes in physical training, and have gymnasiums where the students can have regular and interesting bodily exercises. It is more beneficial to play games than to sit in the bleachers. 

2. Pure Air and Ventilation. If we breathe foul air our lungs become poisoned. Smoke, grime, dust, and damp, cold air are harmful to health. The temperature

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

of a room must be watched and dazzling lights avoided, as colds may be contracted and eyes injured. All schools, theaters, and public buildings where large numbers of people may gather are now usually required by law to provide for good ventilation and plenty of fresh air. 

Are coal miners and house painters and stone cutters sometimes injured by the nature of their work? Why? 

3. Pure Food and Pure Water. Our bodies tend to wear out and waste away. We need regular food to

Fermentation is prevented, and the milk is safe to drink. sustain the body and repair the waste. The food we take into our bodies should be nourishing and healthproducing. Much of our sickness comes from the improper things we take into our stomachs from eating

things we ought not, too much candy, or green apples, or rich mince pie or uncooked things the stomach cannot digest, instead of good, wholesome well-cooked food that is suitable for us. The water or the milk we drink may have the germs of typhoid fever or malaria. Con-

In many places provision has to be made to purify the water. If garbage is thrown into a lake or river from which city water is drawn filtration plants have to be built; or the water may be treated chemically and thus be made clean and pure.

In some cities the water supply has to be brought from great distances. The people cannot have wells and cisterns. Chicago and Cleveland have the Great 

Lakes at hand. Other cities bore deep artesian wells and pump the water to big water towers from which it is distributed by pressure to the homes of the city. 

Los Angeles gets its water from a mountain stream by means of a reservoir 250 miles away. New York has a reservoir in the Catskill Mountains and pure water is brought from miles away through huge pipes laid under the Hudson River. It requires the wealth of great cities to finance such enterprises. 

What reasons can you see why the health of the city may be better than that of the country? What advantages do you see in the country in this respect? 

Under modern conditions, especially in our cities, no family can take care of its own health conditions. The community (national, state, or city) seeks to safeguard the food and drink of the people. Chemists analyze the city water regularly to see that it is pure. The dairies where the cows are fed and milked are inspected by public authorities and are required to be kept clean. 

The Pure Food and Drug Act, which Congress passed in 1906, is intended to provide that people shall not be deceived in their foods and medicines. When one pays for butter he has a right to get butter and not something that merely looks and smells like butter, like oleomargarine. If diseased meats, decaying fruits and vegetables are offered in market for sale there are market detectives and inspectors who may find it out and the salesman may be punished. Stockyards,

slaughterhouses and meat-packing places are inspected by officers of the United States Department of Agriculture. 

4. Recreation. Recreation means "creating again," or making new and fresh the tired muscles and tissues of the body. The laborer needs relaxation and change. 

If life were only one toilsome grind there would not be

Where street cleaning is done regularly, the health of the community is improved, and the convenience of the pedestrians increased. all the time, or is always seeking amusement he becomes stupid and fed up, and it is hard to find anything new to interest him. He becomes worn out and dissipated and needs the recreation of down-right, hard, honest, useful work. The idle rich are usually suffering from this disease.

Fighting Disease and Epidemics. There are many enemies to our health and we have to be constantly on our guard. Sometimes epidemics break out in a com-

munity and bring a frightful loss of life. It used to be thought that cholera and smallpox, the black plague, typhoid fever, diphtheria, malaria, and the "flu" and other diseases were sent at times as visitations of Providence to punish people for their sins. It was thought in such cases that there was nothing man could do. The sin consisted in disregarding the laws of life and health. All these diseases can be prevented, if people will only live according to sanitary law, and if the community will protect itself from conditions which are sure to bring disease and death. It has been shown by science that the typhoid germ may be brought in drinking water or in milk; that filthy flies on the food may bear germs of disease; that mosquitoes carry malarial fever. So we must "swat the fly." Even better than swatting the fly is to clean up the dirt and filth which breed the flies. 

We must make war on mosquitoes by draining the swamps and stagnant pools where they breed. Rats, too, are dirty and dangerous vermin which may bring a deadly plague. It is estimated that every rat costs the country $2.00 by fire and disease. They ought to be exterminated from every house and barn in the land. 

EDWIN LEVICK N TO FIGHT MOSQUITOES, DRAIN YOUR SWAMPS 

Still, stagnant water is their breeding place. A common way of killing them is to drain the swamp by means of ditches, thus keeping the water moving.

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

Preventing Disease. Medical men are now concerned about preventing disease. The doctors have led in establishing Boards of Health in state, county, and city. These Boards give out information about disease and they make health rules for the community 

A STATE BOARD OF HEALTH LABORATORY 

Tests of water supplies throughout the state of New York are made here. to observe. They need the co-operation of all. If a contagious disease breaks out in a home it is the duty of the home, or of its physician, to report the case to the health officer. As soon as possible the home is quarantined" to prevent the spread of the disease. 

Other members of the family are kept apart from the sick one, and only the doctor and the nurse may go in or out. By vaccination and similar precautions, others may be prevented from catching the disease.

If there is danger of the disease spreading to the community, the churches, schools, movies, and all places for public gatherings may be closed, and the people may be urged to stay at home or apart from one another as much as possible.

Explain the services rendered to a community by hospitals. How do people get along in countries where there are no hospitals? What are medical missionaries doing for other lands?

Life Extension. 

Men are living longer than they used to live. The death rate is declining. Medical science has made such advance that it is overcoming disease.

In 1900 nearly 18 persons out of every 1000 died in a year; now fewer than 12 out of 1000. People are becoming more intelligent and are learning how to live and take care of themselves. 

The whole community 

Parks and Playgrounds. needs its recreation. Great public parks are provided for this purpose, which are like breathing places in the city. There the people who live in apartment houses on crowded streets may find out-of-door rest and recreation. Here in their family picnics they may enjoy the flowers and trees and the beauties of nature, as well as the animals in the "Zoo."

In these parks in the cities, all kinds of provision may be made for games and recreation — baseball, tennis, races, track sports, skating, swimming, band concerts in the open air; bars and swings, coasting slides, and all kinds of gymnastic exercises; and many cities have laid out many acres of land for municipal golf. Not

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

only these things out-of-doors are needed, but also libraries, museums, art galleries, amusement rooms where young people may come together for a good time under good influences.

All this rightfully recognizes the importance of amusement and play in life and education. Organized play has now become a part of the work of the school, and, of course, there must be provision for it. The boys and girls of the city cannot be left to play in the street; it is too dangerous in these days of the automobile and the crowded traffic. The children on the farm who may live far apart should have their social centers for play and amusement. There are play directors for children of different ages, and children of all ages are urged to get into the games. 

Benefits of Play. Any one who invents a good game is a benefactor of his fellow men. Play is a great factor in the education of children. Boys and girls ought to be allowed to play, indeed they ought to be encouraged and taught to play. Play gives children good exercise and helps their bodily growth. It teaches them co-operation and respect for others. It teaches them to obey directions and follow the rules. 

A boy who plays well on a ball team is learning loyalty, comradship, leadership, self-denial, courage, faithfulness, friendly rivalry, the spirit of fairness, respect for his opponent, and the power to overcome difficulties. 

He comes to know the need of doing his best, and the joy that comes from achievement. These are the very qualities that he will need for his success in life, and these he acquires from his work and his play.

to cultivate the outdoor life. The automobile has been a great influence in this direction. People can get into the country for picnics and excursions. The States are establishing parks and the roads to them are made attractive to tourists. 

Beautiful scenery and natural wonders are being preserved. If we can keep the beauty of our countryside free from litter, defacement, and destruction, and from the annoyance of billboards and advertisements, the appeal to out-of-doors will be stronger and the cause of health and recreation will be promoted. 

The National Parks. The National Government has turned many of the great battlefields of the Civil War into beautiful National Parks-Gettysburg, Vicksburg, Chickamauga, Chattanooga, and the historic National

Cemetery at Arlington, across the Potomac from Washington, the former home of General Robert E. Lee.

The National Government has also done much to preserve the natural wonders of the continent. The 

Yellowstone Park and Glacier Park in Wyoming and 

Montana, the Yosemite Valley in California, the Grand

Canyon in Arizona, are among the wonders of the world. In the Yellowstone Park, with its millions of acres, the National Government has preserved the scenery and primitive wildness of mountains and lakes, waterfalls and geysers. In this great park are native wild animals of the North American continent, the bear, the buffalo, the bob cat, the mountain lion,

CHAPTER XVI THE AMERICAN STATE AND ITS GOVERNMENT 

The State attracts less attention than the Nation, both at home and abroad. But it is just as important; indeed, in the daily life of the average man and woman it is vastly more important. 

OGG AND RAY 

This Chapter shows the way in which an American State is governed. It also shows the relation of a State to the National 

Government. It explains the way in which the original thirteen 

States came into being and the process by which a Territory becomes a State. It is important because the State Government is very close to the citizen.

The American State is very peculiar. There is nothing like it either "in Modern Europe or in the Ancient World."

The people of Europe find it very difficult to understand the working of our government. It confuses them. We are governed by the Nation and also by the 

State. We have two governments and are subject to two sets of laws. It is very hard for Europeans to understand this double government. For those of us who have lived under the two governments all our lives, the matter is easy enough. 235

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

The State is very important to all of us. It is closer to us than the Nation and does more for us. It protects our lives and property in a very direct way. It also protects our health, provides for our education, cares for the poor, and does a thousand other useful things. In our daily life we have much more to do with the government of the State than with that of the Nation.

The Origin of the American State. Let us see how the State came into being. We already know how our forefathers came to this country and made settlements at Jamestown, Plymouth and other places. In the course of time many other settlers came. They cleared new spots in the forests and made new settlements. 

These scattered settlements made up the various colonies and each of these colonial communities, as we have already seen (pp. 43, 44), had a charter and a form of government of its own. Each separate colony continued to manage its own affairs, under the supervision of Great Britain, until the American Revolution in 1776. At that time the colonies revolted against the mother country and declared themselves to be "free and independent States."

Delegates from these "free and independent States" met in Philadelphia in the summer of 1787 and drew up the Constitution under which we are now living. 

They set up a stronger and a better National Government than we had before, but the States themselves did not go out of business. The States still remain and they constitute a very important part of the government of the American people. The National Government is also important. The fact is both the Nation and

the State are necessary to the safety and the happiness of the people.

How the Territories Became States. In the beginning there were only thirteen States; now there are forty-eight. Thirty-five new States have been admitted into the Union since the Constitution was adopted. 

The last of these was Arizona.

These new States were carved out of territory which the United States owned at the time, such as the Northwest Territory, or from land since acquired, such as the Louisiana Purchase of 1803, or the Mexican 

Cession of 1848.

Now all of the land from the Atlantic to the Pacific, in what is usually called "Continental United States," has been made into States; Arizona, admitted to the 

Union in 1912, was the forty-eighth State. In case we should wish to establish any more new States we should have to turn to our outlying possessions such as Porto Rico, the Philippine Islands and Alaska; or divide some of our larger States like Texas and California.

Most of the States were Territories before they became States. While Territories they were under the direct control of Congress. Congress defined their boundaries and set up the territorial government. 

This government usually consisted of a Governor appointed by the President and a legislature elected by the people. There were also territorial courts presided over by judges appointed by the President. 

When a Territory had a government of this kind it was said to be organized. All of the Territories were organized before becoming States with the exception

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

of Texas and California. Do you see why these two exceptions were made?

The Petition and Enabling Act. The Constitution says that "New States may be admitted by the Congress into this Union." There is no way to compel 

Congress to act and that body has sometimes been very slow about taking the necessary steps. Territories have often been left waiting out in the cold for a long time after they were ready for statehood. This was due to politics. It has always been considered that a Territory should be made a State when it had a population large enough to entitle it to one Representative in Congress. This number has varied from 30,000 in 1789 to 280,000 at the present time.

When a Territory wishes to become a State it makes a petition to Congress. The first step taken by Congress is the passage of an Enabling Act. This act enables, or authorizes, the people of the Territory to elect delegates to a constitutional convention. This convention then proceeds to draft a constitution for the proposed State. The constitution is then submitted to a vote of the people of the Territory for acceptance or rejection. If the people accept the constitution it is submitted to Congress for the approval of that body. If Congress approves the document the

President is notified and through his Secretary of State issues a proclamation stating that the Territory has been admitted to the Union and is now a State. 

The new State then stands upon an equality with all the other States and proceeds to elect its State officers. 

In some cases the State officers were elected before Congress took final action and were all ready to take

up the reins of the government as soon as the President's proclamation was issued. 

Differences Among the States. The States are not uniform either in area or in population. They vary greatly. Some States are large, like Texas and California; some are small, like Rhode Island and Delaware. New York has the largest population and 

Nevada the smallest. Two hundred and twelve States the size of Rhode Island could be carved out of Texas, or seven the size of Indiana. Even then there would be enough land left over in each case to make a string of large farms. California is seven hundred and eighty miles long, while Delaware, with its three counties, is only seventy miles in length. The western States, as a rule, are much larger than the eastern. 

THE GOVERNMENT OF A STATE 

But whether large or small, it should be remembered that all States have essentially the same kind of government.

The State Constitution. In the first place every 

State has a constitution. The constitution is document drawn up by a convention of delegates elected for that purpose. It is usually ratified by a vote of the people before going into effect. It is sometimes called the "fundamental" or the "basic" law of the State because the whole framework of the State's government is based upon it. 

The constitution sets forth the form and the nature of the government and lays down certain principles for the guidance of the officers. It tells what laws may

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

and may not be passed. It provides for a list of State officers and sets forth their powers, duties, and terms of service. The laws passed by the State legislature must be in harmony with the State constitution and must not conflict with the Constitution of the United States. Any law passed by a State legislature which 

UNDERWOOD & UNDERWOOD ILLINOIS STATE CAPITOL, SPRINGFIELD 

does conflict with either of these constitutions is null and void. That is to say, it is no law at all and no one is bound by it. 

All State officers must take an oath, or make an affirmation, to obey the constitution of the State and also the Constitution of the United States.

In a general way it may be said that the State constitutions are very much alike. All of them provide

the framework for the government of the State. In details they differ. Some are long and some are short. 

Some provide a general outline of the State government while others contain a great deal of detail. As a rule the shorter constitutions are the better ones. Why is this true?

The Three Great Departments of Government. 

Every State constitution provides for a government divided into three great Departments - the Legislative, the Executive, and the Judicial. The Legislative makes, or passes the laws; the Executive Department executes, or enforces the laws; and the Judicial interprets the laws, or decides what they mean. 

The Bill of Rights. In addition to those parts already mentioned, a State constitution usually con tains a "Bill of Rights." The Bill of Rights guarantees freedom and civil liberty to the people. It provides for free speech, a free press, the right to assemble and to petition, the right of trial by jury, the protection of life and property, and freedom from illegal arrest and from "cruel and unusual punishments." In a general way, it may be said, the Bill of Rights safeguards and protects the lives and liberties of the people. 

Amending the Constitution. No constitution, however good, will last for all time. It must change.

Every constitution gets out of date- some of them very rapidly. Change and growth must be provided for. So every constitution provides a mode of amendment. 

Was your State ever a Territory? If so, when and in what circumstances did it become a State? What are the essential provisions of your State constitution?

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP THE EXECUTIVE DEPARTMENT — THE POWERS OF THE GOVERNOR 

Every State has a Governor. He is sometimes called the Chief Executive because he is the chief man to execute or carry out the laws. He is nominated for the office by his party, either in a nominating convention or a primary, and is elected by the people, usually for a term of two or four years.

It is his duty to see that all the laws of the State are faithfully enforced. To enable him to do this the State police force is placed under his control. He is also commander-in-chief of the State troops. He sometimes uses the police power or the military power to suppress riots or to disperse mobs.

The Governor also has certain legislative powers. 

He gives information and makes suggestions to the legislature from time to time. He does this by means of his message.

He may also veto bills passed by the legislature and sent to him for his signature. If he is an able man and a real leader he will exercise a great influence over the legislature and induce that body to pass measures which he favors.

Many important laws now originate in the Governor's office. The best results for the welfare of the people are obtained when the Governor and the legislature work together for the passage of good laws. 

Team work is necessary.

The Governor is often the leader of his party in the State. A strong Governor is often able to control the politicians. A weak Governor, on the other hand,

is likely to be a mere tool in the hands of the politicians who are looking after their own interests rather than those of the people.

The Governor also has some judicial power. He may pardon a man convicted of crime under the State 

OEWING GALLOWAY, N Y A STATE LEGISLATURE IN SESSION 

laws. He may commute the sentence of a convicted man; that is, for example, he may change it from the death penalty to imprisonment for life. He may postpone the date of a man's execution in order to give time to obtain new evidence.

It should also be said that the Governor usually exercises his pardoning power upon the advice of a Board of Pardons. This Board investigates each

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

case and advises the Governor as to what he ought to do. 

Other State Officers. The Governor is the chief executive officer of the State but he is by no means the only one. There are at least six or eight other officers who have important executive duties to perform. 

Next to the Governor, comes the Lieutenant Governor, or the Deputy Governor, as he is sometimes called. He is elected in the same way the Governor is and usually presides over the upper house (Senate) of the State legislature. If the Governor should die or resign the Lieutenant Governor would become Governor.

The Secretary of State has charge of the papers and records of the State. He publishes the laws passed by the legislature and makes up the official ballot for the primaries and also for the election. Automobile license plates are often issued from his office. The duties of the State's Secretary of State are very different from those of the Secretary of State in the President's Cabinet.

The State Treasurer has charge of all the moneys of the State. He receives State taxes collected for him by the various county treasurers and pays all bills due from the State. When the legislature appropriates money for the support of a State University the Treasurer pays over the money at specified times to the treasurer of the institution.

The State Auditor is the bookkeeper and accountant for the State. He checks up the accounts of the Treasurer and sees to it that no money is paid out which

has not been voted by the legislature. He issues warrants or orders for paying out money. 

The Attorney-General is the lawyer for the State. 

He gives legal advice to the State officers and represents the State in lawsuits in which the State is interested.

The Superintendent of Public Instruction, sometimes called the Commissioner of Education, has general charge of the public schools of the State. He has his office in the capitol building and is assisted in his work by a large staff of deputies and clerks. He gathers statistics, makes courses of study, and publishes important reports and pamphlets. 

The list of officers varies somewhat in the different 

States. If you look up the list for your own State, you will probably find several whom we have not mentioned here.

We should remember that these officers are independent of the Governor. He does not appoint them nor can he remove them, except for very unusual causes. They are not like the President's Cabinet whom the President appoints and can remove. They are elected by the people and often on the same ticket with the Governor. They are not his official advisers, but merely perform the duties of their offices as defined by law and the constitution. Some reformers would like to have them under the direct control of the Governor and have them constitute a sort of Governor's Cabinet. It would require changes in our laws and constitutions to bring this about. 

In addition to 

State Boards and Commissions. these officers most of the States have Boards or Com-

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

missions of various kinds to care for certain interests. 

There are usually a Tax Board, a Board of Agriculture, a Board of Charities, and a Board of Public Health. 

Some States have boards to deal with mines, labor, and public works.

The State Board of Education is made up of leading school men and women of the State and is usually presided over by the State Superintendent. This Board prescribes the course of study, inspects the schools, grants licenses to teachers, and "commissions" High

Schools. This means that the Board makes a list of the High Schools which are entitled to send their graduates to college without entrance examinations.

In the State of New York the Board of Regents adopts courses of study for the public schools and sets Regents examinations to be taken by the pupils of the State.

In some States, as in Indiana, the State Board of Education selects the text-books which are used in all the public schools.

Most of the States have Public Service Commissions, or Public Utilities Commissions, as they are sometimes called. These commissions have charge of the public utilities of the State - the gas, water, and electric plants, the electric lines, and, in some instances, the bus lines. These commissions tell the public utilities what rates they may charge for their gas, electricity, or bus rides.

Since the automobile has become so important most States have State Highway Commissions. These comsions oversee the building of the more important highways in the State and keep them in repair. Some States

have borrowed large sums of money for this purpose, while others have derived their revenue from gasoline taxes and from the sale of license plates. 

The business of a State is a large and an expensive one. It requires many officers and much money to

Their job is to build good roads and to keep them in repair. carry it on. The business is constantly growing and the taxes are going higher. 

Who is the Governor of your State? What is his term of office? What do you consider his most important duty? What noted men have held the office of Governor in your State?

State Police more necessary now than they were twentyfive years ago? Is crime increasing in the rural districts?

In what ways does it protect the people? In what ways might this Commission do a vast amount of harm?

What are their duties? Do these boards perform their duties regardless of politics?

THE LEGISLATIVE DEPARTMENT LEGISLATURE 

- THE STATE 

The State Legislature is the dominant part of the State Government. It makes the laws which govern the people of the State.

The Legislature (sometimes called the Assembly) is made up of two houses in all the States except Nebraska which now has only one house. These two houses are usually called the Senate and the House of Representatives, as in the National Government. The Senate, or the upper house, is the smaller of the two bodies. The 

House of Representatives usually contains two or three times as many members as the Senate. 

The State is divided up into Senatorial districts and also into Representative districts. The Senators and the Representatives are then elected by a vote of the people living in these districts.

The term of office of the Representative is usually

two years. In some States it is only one year and in a very few the term is four years. 

The term of the Senator is usually longer than that of the Representative. In a number of States the 

Senators serve for four years and the Representatives for two. In such cases one-half of the Senators are elected every two years. 

The remaining half are called "hold-over" Senators. 

The length of the session of the legislature varies in the different States. In some States it is limited to sixty or ninety days; in others there is no limit at all. 

The legislature simply continues in session until it finishes its business. It then adjourns. 

When the session is limited the members are usually paid a certain amount per day for their services. If the session is not limited they are usually paid a yearly salary. 

In case of an emergency, or where the regular session adjourns without having finished its important business, the Governor may call a special session. This is usually called an "extra session." Why are extra sessions. rarely called?

How many members are there in each house of your State legislature? What is the length of the term of office in each house? How long does your legislature remain in session? How is this determined? Do you have "hold-over" Senators? How are the members paid? Do you think the length of the session should be limited or unlimited? What are the arguments in each case? Turn to your State constitution and read what it says about the legislature. Have you ever attended a session of either house of your legislature? Have you ever talked to a member of your legislature about the business of that body?

How a Law is Made. A State legislature is made up of all sorts of men and women. You will find there lawyers, farmers, merchants, editors, manufacturers, real estate men, and men of no regular occupation at all. There are usually more lawyers in a law-making body than men of any other occupation. 

The main business of the legislature is to draft bills and to enact them into laws. The drafting of a bill is a very important matter. It is a difficult task too if well done. It is often very poorly done. Some bills are drafted in such a slovenly way that it is impossible to tell what they mean. Very few members of a State legislature are able to draft a bill in a correct and scientific manner. As a result of this fact 

As a result of this fact many States have established Legislative Bureaus to furnish information to the members and to draft their bills for them. 

Sometimes bills are drafted by persons or organizations entirely outside of the legislature and then given to members to introduce.

Again, important measures are sometimes drafted in the Governor's office and advocated by him. These are called "administration measures."

The Lobby. The law-makers receive all kinds of advice from many sources. Men and women crowd the passage ways or "lobbies" of the capitol in order to "button-hole" the members as they pass by. People swarm into the capitol from all parts of the State in order to urge the legislators to pass this bill or to defeat that one. They lie in wait for them at the door of the chamber and seek to waylay them at their hotels. 

Failing in this, they call them on the telephone, or send them telegrams, and finally turn hordes of their

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

friends loose upon them. They are neither modest nor sensitive about their methods of approach. 

These outsiders are known as the Lobby, sometimes called the Third House. Lobbyists at times have been so numerous, bold, and offensive that they have been excluded from the capitol building. Some States require them to register and to make known the particular kind of legislation which they are trying to pass or to defeat. The experienced lobbyist, however, is very skillful in "getting around" these restrictions. 

In this same connection we should remember that all lobbyists are not bad or selfish people. Many of them are honest and high-minded men and women. It all depends upon what the lobbyist is trying to do and what methods he employs. Some of them work unselfishly for the passage of bills which ought to pass bills which are for the public good. Others work for bills which are contrary to the public welfare bills from which they or their employers hope to reap a rich profit at the expense of the people. Some lobbyists are entirely open, honest, and above-board in their dealings, while others do their work in secret and bring improper influences to bear upon the law-makers. 

Can the passage of a good measure be obtained usually without a lobby? Lobbyists go to the State capitol to look out for their interests, or for the interests of those who employ them. Who guards the interests of the people as a whole? Sometimes a man is a member of the legislature and an attorney for a corporation at the same time. Is it always possible for him to serve two masters?

The Committees. State legislatures have to consider an enormous number of bills. In order to facilitate

the handling of these bills the membership of each house is divided up into a large number of committees. 

There is a committee for every important subject one for Education, Banks and Banking, Roads and

Highways, Appropriations, and scores of other subjects. As soon as a bill is introduced it is referred to the proper committee for consideration and report.

In this connection it should be said also that bills may originate, or start, in either house, except that money bills usually originate in the lower house. 

The committee has great influence over legislation. 

Practically no bill can become a law unless it can get the approval of the committee. Many members who know little about the particular measure will take the word of the committee.

If the committee likes the bill it is reported back "favorably." If the committee doesn't like it, it will probably not be reported back at all. It is then said to have "died in the committee." Most bills never see the light of day after being referred to the committees. 

The committee performs its best service by killing bad bills and by preventing foolish and freakish legislation. 

We are really suffering at the present time from too much rather than too little legislation. 

The committee does, however, find some bills to its liking. In such cases a "favorable report" is made and the bill has a good start towards its passage. It is then debated and probably amended. It is usually required that a bill be read three times on three different days before being passed. This is to prevent hasty legislation and also to let the people know what the legislature proposes to do. Sometimes, however, the

ACTIVE CITIZENSHIP 

نا rules are suspended and the three readings are cut short.

The Quorum. In order to do business in either house it is necessary that a certain number of members
